# Native supervisor and board

`goblins serve` runs one native Go supervisor and serves the embedded React/TypeScript board on `http://127.0.0.1:4310`.

The optional [Remote access setting](remote-access.md) in the CEO's Task panel
shares that same board and its existing terminals with the owner's phone over
Tailscale HTTPS. It is off by default and leaves the listener on loopback.
Use `--listen 127.0.0.1:0` for an ephemeral port; the command prints its actual URL.
Node, Vite, Docker, and a browser are not runtime dependencies of the supervisor.
Docker remains an optional project environment managed by the existing worktree profiles.
`serve` does not care where it was started: started from a Herdr pane, such as the CEO's own, it first drops that pane's variables (`HERDR_ENV`, `HERDR_PANE_ID`, `HERDR_TAB_ID`, `HERDR_WORKSPACE_ID`, `HERDR_STARTUP_CWD`, `HERDR_SOCKET_PATH` and `HERDR_BIN_PATH`) and keeps every other `HERDR_` variable, such as `HERDR_SESSION` and the user's configuration in `HERDR_CONFIG_PATH`, so a terminal it opens, or any other program it runs, is not refused by herdr as nested inside that pane.

The service takes the existing `.watch.lock` before opening recovery state.
Another `serve` holding it is refused, so two supervisors never run, and a `serve` started while this home's board answers as its recorded supervisor names that board and exits 1 before taking an address; a watcher holding it, the one the CEO's `stop-autoarm` hook hosts or a `goblins watch`, hands it over, because the supervisor supersedes it.
`serve` writes `state/serve.handover` naming its pid, start time and host, and writes it again every second while it waits; the watcher's wait wakes on that file, so it yields the lock at once, with nothing appended and no episode published, and no watcher takes the lock while a live `serve` waits for it.
On 2026-10-01 an install stopped `serve`, the CEO's Stop hook took the lock in the gap, and every new `serve` refused to start until the hook's window ended, which can run for hours.
A watcher in the middle of a cycle answers at once in `state/serve.handover.ack`, naming itself and the `serve` by pid and start time, and stops its monitor scan, orphan sweep and filing pass; `serve` waits up to five more minutes for a watcher that answered, so the Stop hook that rewakes an idle CEO is never ended for being slow.
A watcher from a binary older than the handover never reads the request, so after 30 seconds without an answer `serve` ends that one process, and only once it has proved it through the one handle it ends it by: the lock names it as a watcher by pid, start time and host, the process was created at that start time, and its command line is `goblins` running `hook stop-autoarm` or `watch`.
A holder running anything else, a terminal's host, a `serve`, a gate's test or another program, is left running and `serve` refuses as before; starting the board never ends a worker, a terminal's host, or a watcher's parent, and pending wakes stay in the durable queue.
While `serve` holds it, the Claude CEO's `stop-autoarm` hook still rewakes the idle CEO: it waits on the wake queue, rewakes once for each record no earlier rewake covered, and hosts the watcher itself again if `serve` stops.
It counts `serve` as the watcher until its heartbeat is older than the stall window (15 minutes, `GOBLINS_WATCHER_STALL`), because `serve` stamps its heartbeat in the loop that runs its reconcile cycle and a slow cycle can hold it past the turn-end guard's five minutes; past the window it reports supervision down, naming `serve`'s pid and saying to restart it, since nothing can take the watcher from a live `serve`.
A watcher changing hands is not supervision being down: the lock is free between a watcher letting it go and `serve` taking it, `serve`'s record is empty until it is written, and a `serve` that has just taken the lock has not stamped its heartbeat yet.
So while a live `serve` waits for the lock, or a record is there that cannot be read, or a live process holds it, the hook looks at the watcher again for up to three seconds (`GOBLINS_CLAUDE_AUTOARM_SETTLE_MS`) before that look counts against it; a lock nobody holds or asks for, or one a dead process left, counts at once.
When it does report supervision down, the banner's `Watcher:` line says what the last look found: no holder, an unreadable record, a holder that is not running, or a heartbeat that is missing, stale or dated in the future.
When its wait ends with its window and nothing queued, it rewakes the CEO to end a turn, which re-arms it.
Closing the browser disconnects a view, while Ctrl-C in the supervisor terminal, or `goblins stop` from any terminal, stops that process.
Once it holds the singleton and listens, `serve` records its pid and the board's address in `state/board.json`, and removes the record when it exits.
`goblins` with no command reads that record and asks the board's `/api/alive`, which answers with the supervisor's pid without building the fleet's snapshot: only a successful answer naming the recorded pid is the running supervisor, and anything else at the address, a page not found, an answer that names no pid or another one, makes the record stale.
On 2026-10-01 a healthy board took eight seconds to build its snapshot, the launcher's three-second wait read it as dead, and `goblins` tried to start a second supervisor.
A live supervisor gets a status line from the snapshot within three seconds, or one saying the fleet's status is still loading, or that the board could not read the fleet's state, and `goblins` starts and opens nothing and goes on to the CEO.
Otherwise it starts `serve` detached from its terminal, in a hidden console of its own that the programs `serve` runs share, so no console window opens, with its output appended to `state/serve.log`, waits up to a minute for the board to answer, which covers a watcher handing it the lock, and opens no browser: `goblins` opens the board only when it is chosen on its last screen.
Two `goblins` started at once each start a `serve`: the one whose start finds `serve.log` held by the other's, or whose `serve` exits finding the other taking the lock over or holding it, waits for the other's board instead of failing.
The detached `serve` listens on the board's address, which is the same every time: `127.0.0.1:4310`, or the numeric loopback address the `GOBLINS_BOARD_ADDRESS` environment variable names (port 0 there asks the OS for a free port, as a scratch home or a test does; the record holds the real address).
An address already in use is never replaced with another: `goblins` starts nothing and says who holds it.
It asks the listener's `/api/alive`, which a supervisor answers with its pid and its home, so the message names the Code Goblins fleet of another home by its folder, an older supervisor, which answers with no home, by its pid alone, and says "another program" for anything else.
Only this home's own supervisor, started a moment ago by another `goblins` and not yet recorded, is waited for instead.
`serve` takes `.watch.lock` before it listens, so only the supervisor that holds the lock ever holds the address: a `serve` a second `goblins` starts on a free port, having read the silent listener of this home's starting supervisor as another program, finds the lock held and exits, and that `goblins` waits for the board.
This home's own supervisor that holds the address but records no board within that wait is named by its pid, with the PowerShell line that ends it (`Stop-Process -Id <pid>`), since `goblins stop` decides from the record that is missing.
`goblins serve` without `--listen` uses the same address.
`goblins update` restarts the supervisor on the address the one it stops was serving, read from the record, so a home on an address of its own keeps it whatever shell runs the update; with no supervisor running it uses the board's address.
A later `goblins update --recover` starts the previous build's supervisor on the address the record names, whether or not that supervisor still answers, and on the board's address only when no record can be read.
An update holds `.watch.lock` itself from the supervisor it stopped to the one it starts, and again through a rollback, so the lock is never free in between.
The supervisor it asks to stop hands it the lock as it ends, where its build reads the successor a stop request names; one from before that releases the lock, and the update takes it at once, ending first a supervisor of this home that took it in that moment.
It starts the next supervisor suspended, changes the lock's record to name it, and only then lets it run; every build since v0.1.0 takes a record that names itself as its own.
A `serve` started meanwhile, by a `goblins` opened beside the update, is refused the lock as by any running supervisor, and `goblins` itself starts none while an update installs: it waits for the board the update brings back.
On 2026-10-09 two supervisors the update had not started took the lock from it, the second while its rollback restored the files, and the update ended with the board called down, the previous build on disk and the new one serving.
A stop request ends a supervisor within a second or two whatever its cycle is doing: a watcher of its own reads the request four times a second and cancels the cycle in progress, where the loop used to look only between cycles.
A record whose address does not answer, left by a supervisor that ended without removing it, is replaced by the next start, and a record naming anything but a plain loopback board address is ignored.
`goblins --board` finds or starts the supervisor the same way, opens the board root in the browser every time, and exits 1 naming the link when the browser cannot be opened.
Like `goblins --window` and the desktop window's `goblins --window --locate`, it keeps a running CEO or reopens the remembered harness on its saved conversation where possible, without attaching it to the caller's terminal.
The existing CEO launch lock prevents two openings from starting two CEOs.
A failed CEO start leaves the board reachable for Reopen, and the locator keeps its two-line address and state-folder output.
The quick start (`goblins` with no command) says first, under the banner, what it found and what it started, one ticked line each: `Supervisor already running` or `Supervisor started`, then, after the agent steps when no CEO runs, `CEO already running` and where, or `CEO started as` the agent, in the home.
Then `goblins` brings the Overlord to the CEO, after the quick start's agent steps when no CEO runs (docs/install.md, The quick start).
A CEO whose registration in `state/primary.json` names a live process is reused, never started a second time: `goblins` brings its registered workspace and tab to the front and hands its terminal to `herdr`, attached to the session the CEO registered in.
It decides from the registration alone and asks neither the board nor Herdr, so a supervisor that has not checked the registration yet or a Herdr that cannot answer changes nothing.
Otherwise it makes sure Herdr's server runs and starts the remembered harness as the CEO in a fresh `goblins` tab it creates in the CEO home, since Herdr starts an agent in its pane's own directory; it asks for no project, because the CEO works across every project from its home.
An old `goblins` tab with no agent in any of its panes is closed when every pane sits at its shell prompt, and renamed to `shell` when anything else runs in one, `goblins` itself included; a `goblins` tab with an agent in any pane is left as it is and no second CEO is started beside it.
For the final console choices, see [Everyday commands](https://github.com/fpresta0607/code-goblins/blob/main/README.md#everyday-commands).
Run inside a Herdr pane there is nothing to attach, so `goblins` only brings the CEO to the front.
`goblins status` reads the board record and prints the board's link, the status line and the supervisor's pid, and exits 1 when the supervisor the record names does not answer as itself there.
A supervisor started in the background has no terminal for Ctrl-C to reach, so `goblins stop` writes `state/serve.stop` naming the record's pid and waits up to 30 seconds for the board to stop answering.
The supervisor checks for that request on its notification tick, which comes at least every two seconds between cycles, and stops as it would on Ctrl-C, removing its record; a request naming any other pid is left over from a supervisor that already ended, so it is removed and stops nothing.
A supervisor also removes any request left from before it started, so a reused pid cannot stop it.
`goblins stop --force` ends the recorded pid's process tree with `taskkill /T /F`, or one process at a time when a machine service (Docker Desktop, the no-mistakes daemon, the Scrawl server) runs under it, which it leaves running, and removes the record instead, and a record whose supervisor does not answer as itself is removed without stopping anything; a supervisor whose snapshot is slow still answers, so its record is never taken for stale.
Restarting with the same CEO home recovers durable events, evaluations, actions, and lineage.

## Native hook setup

Every session the fleet starts carries its native session hooks in its own launch, so a Claude Code, Codex or Pi goblin reports its session, its parent, its prompts, its turns and its end the same way, and nothing global is written.
A Claude Code goblin's settings file, `state/goblin-claude-settings.json`, and the Claude CEO's, `state/ceo-claude-settings.json`, run `goblins native-hook claude` for SessionStart, UserPromptSubmit, PostToolUse, Stop, SessionEnd, SubagentStart, SubagentStop, PreCompact and PostCompact, beside their other hooks, though a goblin's PostToolUse posts to the board in its place ([The seal and the key alarm](#the-seal-and-the-key-alarm)); neither `~/.claude/settings.json` nor any other file every Claude Code session on the machine loads gets them, since the Overlord's own sessions outside the fleet would run them too, as one once took the CEO's lock.
A Codex goblin's launch, and the Codex CEO's, pass `--enable hooks` and one `-c hooks.<event>=...` override for each of those events and Interrupt, running `goblins native-hook codex` directly, rather than writing `~/.codex/hooks.json`; a goblin's also passes its goblin seal, a `hooks.PreToolUse` override for Bash ([The seal and the key alarm](#the-seal-and-the-key-alarm)).
Codex keys a hook's trust by where it is defined, the same `<session-flags>/config.toml` for every launch, and by what it runs, so the Overlord trusts them once, in a Codex goblin's `/hooks`, for every Codex session of the home; until he does, each Codex start reports that it started without trusting its hooks, and its hooks do not run.
A home whose paths would need quoting, such as one under a profile whose name has a space, cannot write the direct command into a `-c` override, so its Codex goblins start without per-launch hooks, which the start's output says, and the board reads them from their terminals; its Codex CEO starts without them too, and `goblins hooks install codex` writes them for it with its helper.
Pi takes its hooks from its extension, `~/.pi/agent/extensions/goblins-native.ts`, which `goblins hooks install pi` writes; a pi goblin starts with its goblin seal's extension as well, which also gives it [the turn-end nudge](#the-turn-end-nudge).
`goblins install`, which `goblins update` runs, rewrites an extension `goblins hooks install pi` wrote as its own build writes it, so a fix to it reaches every pi session started after the update; it writes none where none was installed and leaves a file it did not write.
Run `goblins hooks check claude`, `goblins hooks check codex`, or `goblins hooks check pi` to inspect the installed capability contract.
Run `goblins hooks install <harness>` to add the corresponding native lifecycle integration for sessions the fleet did not start; for Claude Code and Codex the fleet's own launches need none.
The verified minimum contracts are Claude Code 2.1.278, Codex 0.154.0, and Pi 0.85.1.
The check reads the harness's version through PowerShell with the execution policy bypassed, so the script shim npm installs for Codex and Pi runs even where the policy is Restricted.
Kimi Code is not a harness the fleet runs for now: it ran only in Herdr, and no native terminal knows its screens.
Bringing it back needs its screens captured for a native terminal (its startup prompts, its composer and the marks of a turn, as `internal/harness/screens.go` holds them for Claude Code, Codex and pi), an adapter that builds its launch and says how it is stopped and resumed, a hook that reports each prompt it takes, which is how a delivery is confirmed, and its name back in what lists the harnesses: `goblins spawn` and `goblins switch`, `goblins doctor`, the installer, the ticket labels and the board's Start.

Default destinations are `~/.claude/settings.json`, `~/.codex/hooks.json`, and `~/.pi/agent/extensions/goblins-native.ts`.
Use `--config-dir <absolute-directory>` for a custom harness home or an isolated test configuration.
Setup preserves unrelated JSON hooks/settings, takes a first backup before changing existing JSON, and replaces only its owned helper.
Its hooks go back where they stood in each event's list, so rerunning it changes nothing when nothing changed.
`goblins install` writes no hook into the same `~/.claude/settings.json`: the CEO's hooks come with its terminal's start, and an install only takes out the ones an older build wrote there.
It does not change models, gate policy, approval settings, or Codex hook trust.
Review the exact installed Codex definitions in `/hooks` before they can run.

Claude's helper pipes hook stdin to `goblins native-hook claude` using a bounded PowerShell command.
Codex runs each hook command inside the session's own shell, `powershell.exe -NoProfile -Command` on Windows, so its hooks run `goblins native-hook codex` directly: a helper there would start a second PowerShell for every event, and live on Codex 0.160 that took longer than the 3-second limit the hooks then had, so every hook timed out.
The direct command is written without quotes and with forward slashes, which PowerShell, cmd and bash read alike, so where the executable, home or state path would need quoting Codex keeps the helper.
Pi's extension calls the same executable directly from its native event handlers.
Hooks record identity and lifecycle metadata, not prompts, tool arguments, or transcripts.
They atomically spool an event and return without Git, network, gate, or browser work.
A Codex or pi CEO's hooks do no more than a Claude Code session's: the supervisor raises the idle wake from the turn end they spool and writes the compaction checkpoint and its wake from the compaction events, as it ingests them, for the registered CEO's own session (issue #1213).
Only the SessionStart's registration still runs in the hook, since only a process in the CEO's own session can prove it is one.
Claude Code, Codex and pi each hold a native hook to 30 seconds (`nativehook.TimeoutSeconds`), and end it there, which loses its event.
The hook's own work takes milliseconds; the limit is for the start of its process, which on a machine full of the fleet's builds and tests took up to 19 seconds, past the 3 seconds the hooks had until issue #1211, so a session's prompts and turn ends went unrecorded exactly while the machine was busiest.
A hook that finishes sooner ends sooner, so the limit costs time only while the machine itself is stalled.
The installer records the absolute executable/home/state paths, so reinstall the owned hooks after moving the executable; a reinstall replaces the hooks an earlier install wrote, in either form.

| Harness signal | Native meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Claude/Codex SessionStart | Session started |
| UserPromptSubmit / PostToolUse | Activity evidence |
| Stop | Settled; requests task evaluation |
| SessionEnd | Harness session ended |
| Codex Interrupt | Interrupted; requests evaluation |
| Claude/Codex PreCompact / Pi session_before_compact | The session says it compacts until its next event, and for a Codex or Pi CEO the supervisor writes its checkpoint; no turn starts or ends |
| Claude/Codex PostCompact / Pi session_compact | The session's compaction ended, and for a Codex or Pi CEO the supervisor queues one wake naming its checkpoint; no turn starts or ends |
| SubagentStart / SubagentStop | Reported child started / settled |
| Pi session_start / agent_start / tool_execution_end | Started / active |
| Pi agent_settled with no pending messages | Settled after continuations and retries |
| Pi session_shutdown | Session ended |

Claude Code raises SubagentStart only as its Agent tool starts a subagent, with that agent's type, and SubagentStop also as its own forked agents end, compaction, memory and summary among them, with no type and no start before it.
The supervisor accepts a session's events only after its start, so the hook spools nothing for an untyped Claude Code SubagentStop, which would otherwise wait in the spool until it aged out (issue #1212).
Distinct hook invocations receive distinct durable IDs, including two Stops within one native turn after a hook-requested continuation.
Replaying the same spool record retains its ID and is idempotent.
A Stop never establishes that the task shipped.

## Evidence and recovery

The supervisor reuses the existing monitor, watch signal signatures, wake queue, task metadata, native terminal steering, and frozen pipeline reader.
One Windows filesystem watcher consumes the spool, with a two-second recovery interval for missed notifications.
If the native-inbox state folder disappears, ingestion or the next board update recreates it and names the repair on the board; earlier hook events may be missing.
If that folder cannot be read or recreated, the board reports the folder problem and delayed hook events while other task updates and reconciliation continue, and ingestion resumes when access returns without restarting serve.
An event moved out of the folder while ingestion reads it, as setting the inbox's events aside in another folder does, has left the inbox: that pass takes the rest and reports no error.
One slow reconciliation pass runs each minute; there is no browser-driven task polling engine.
Unresolved tasks continue reconciling after SessionEnd, after becoming ready, and after their native session is retired.
Verified terminal tasks leave that polling path until new session activity supplies a reason to reevaluate.
A task whose harness is at work is left out of that pass, and a card reads its gate from the task's evaluation, so the progress watch, which reads each goblin's gate run once a minute, has the task evaluated again whenever that run moved: a step that changed status, a run that ended or a pull request whose state changed.
A run that did not move starts no evaluation, so an answered gate decision stops holding the card at Waiting within a minute while the turn still runs (#1305).

Fresh, matching Herdr monitor evidence distinguishes a working harness from an unavailable or stale one.
An abrupt process crash does not change the recorded native event into an invented end event.
The board shows runtime unavailability separately and continues evaluating independent task/gate evidence.
Silence, a vanished process, a Stop, and a prior `notify --done` are never substitutes for delivery evidence, except a [`--done --closed`](commands/notify.md), the only evidence a task ruled finished with no pull request has, and a [`--done --report`](commands/notify.md), the only evidence a local-only task that opens none has.

Review, tests, lint, documentation, push, PR, and CI must agree with the task's exact HEAD under its frozen policy.
A clean no-mistakes task with a detached HEAD reports review, unverified, because gate evidence requires a branch.
Failures in the evaluation's worktree and base Git reads make the evaluation unavailable and can be retried by later reconciliation; exit 128 reports fixed guidance for recognized permission, unsafe ownership, or missing repository diagnostics, and generic worktree and access guidance for unknown, conflicting, or truncated diagnostics.
Git's raw diagnostic output is not shown.
Unrecovered pipeline custody blocks native worker terminal input even if a run is failed or cancelled.
The browser can request evaluation or send contextual review comments to the verified CEO; it cannot approve gates, drag a task to done, or merge.
An explicitly connected terminal sends ordinary terminal input to that existing session, subject to the identity and custody boundaries below.

For a ship task, a merged PR remains **merged-awaiting-verification** until the supervisor verifies landed main content.
It checks repository identity, current remote default-branch HEAD, matching locally available main objects, and the exact tree entry (mode, type and object) or absence of every task-changed path on main, then rechecks the remote head.
A mode-only or type-only change with identical bytes therefore stays unverified until main holds the same entry.
Missing local objects produce an explicit verification-pending reason; the supervisor does not fetch or modify the worktree to hide missing evidence.
The pipeline's `terminal_head_verified_at` proves its terminal worktree head, not that the task's content was read on main.
Manual task modes retain their existing review/delivery authority and are not automatically marked done by native hooks.

Full-task diffs use a retained, validated project merge base from the same task generation when available, otherwise an established `origin/HEAD` merge base, whether the ref is symbolic or direct.
If neither is available, preview reports that limitation instead of substituting `HEAD^` and omitting earlier task commits.
Individual commit previews are separate, explicit views.

## Persistence bounds

The active store retains up to 512 session nodes, 512 retired-parent markers, 512 tracked tasks/evaluations, 256 actions, and 2,048 recently seen event IDs.
The serialized store is limited to 8 MiB and recent malformed-event diagnostics to 20 entries.
Ended sessions or sessions inactive for seven days can roll out when they have no pending action.
Needed unresolved task references survive session retirement, and missing parents remain explicitly retired or unreported.
Task admission may replace terminal history; 512 unresolved tasks or 512 recent sessions deliberately apply backpressure rather than silently dropping ownership.

The inbox admits 4,096 small records, each at most 64 KiB.
Ingestion sorts its bounded read window by event time before its 256-record consumption batch, retries records awaiting start/metadata evidence, and rotates the window if concurrent writers briefly exceed admission capacity.
A record still awaiting start, metadata or worktree evidence after an hour, or two minutes for a task the store never tracked, is set aside and counted in one diagnostic; a record held back by the bounds above waits however long that takes.
Transient persistence failures retain the inbox record and roll memory back to the last durable state.
The next cycles retry the save, and the board reports a persistence failure only once saves have kept failing for 30 seconds; other errors still show at once.
Malformed records leave a bounded diagnostic and do not wedge subsequent valid work.
An interrupted evaluation is safe to replay; an interrupted external delivery becomes uncertain and is not resent automatically.
Uncertain actions remain visible for operator inspection until the action bound needs their place.
A full bound makes room by dropping its oldest succeeded, failed or unconfirmed (`uncertain`) action no longer watched for a late take, so only queued and running actions and deliveries still watched can fill it.

## Board and orchestration

The header switches between Board, Orchestration and Connectors, with one main view visible at a time and one contextual pane on the right.
The first load fetches only what the first screen draws, under 1.5 MB at a phone's width, with its pictures as WebP (#1599).
Orchestration and the family tree that takes its place on a small screen, the Command Center, a file's diff and a command's terminal each fetch their code the first time they are shown; until the Command Center's code arrives its button counts what waits on him, as the tab's title and the app's badge do.
`frontend/tests/first-paint.spec.ts` measures that load: on main at 83505e5de it fetched 4.7 MB, a 1.3 MB script and 3.1 MB of PNG pictures.
A second load fetches none of the board's files under `/assets/` again (#1966).
Vite names each file it writes by a hash of its content, and the build adds `?v=` and the first 16 hex digits of its content's SHA-256 to each address it writes of a file it copies from `frontend/public/assets` (`frontend/vite.config.ts`).
The supervisor answers such a file `private, max-age=31536000, immutable`: a file its build manifest lists, or one whose `v` is the digest of the file it serves (`internal/supervisor/board_assets.go`).
Asked for without it, as the AFK return page and Scrawl ask for the fonts, a copied file can change under its name and stays `no-store`, as `/`, the API, `/manifest.webmanifest` and `/push-worker.js` do.
Scripts and styles go gzip-compressed to a browser that takes gzip, each compressed once on its first request and kept.
Board groups actual tasks into Tasks, In progress and Completed, side by side as a kanban by default.
Paused means on the bench and Waiting means live and on standby (the Overlord, 2026-10-16: "paused does it means youre on the bench to be resumed", "waiting doesn't mean you're stopped").
Every paused goblin sits at the bottom of In progress, under a thin Paused divider with its count, like a page break, whatever its pause waits for: memory, the Overlord's own Pause, another task, a date, his answer, an allowance reset, CI, a deploy or a pull request, its own included.
Its terminal is stopped and it does no work until a resume: his Resume, the CEO, or its condition clearing by itself.
The divider and its cards show only while a goblin is paused, pausing or resuming.
The Paused group runs top first as its goblins resume (`resumeOrder` in `frontend/src/start.ts`): a goblin resuming now or whose Resume the Overlord clicked, then each whose pause cleared or clears with memory, in the In progress order the supervisor resumes them in, so the Next up card is first, then each paused until a time (a date or an allowance reset), earliest first, then each waiting on another task, a pull request, CI, a deploy or a person, in the In progress order (the Overlord, 2026-10-17: "whatever goblins are going to resume first are at the top").
A goblin whose time passes moves up among those resuming at the next tick of the board's clock.
Its cards drag like In progress cards but carry no numbers; a drop the resume order undoes, such as a goblin paused until tomorrow dropped above Next up, goes back and says "Paused goblins stay in the order they resume." for a moment, and saves nothing.
A paused card always reads Paused and what resumes it in a few words, never Waiting: Paused until memory frees, Paused until you resume it, Paused until after the restart, Paused until Hank delivers, Paused until PR #512 merges, Paused until CI finishes, Paused until Sat, Paused until you answer, and its panel says it in a sentence behind Details.
A goblin paused until its own pull request merges reads its pause too, and its pull request's chip still says where that pull request stands under test.
A waiting goblin, one that reported `goblins notify --waiting-on`, runs and picks its work up the moment what it waits on arrives: it stays among the working cards, reads Waiting on Pearl, Waiting on CI, Waiting on its deploy or Waiting on the CEO, and its panel offers Pause and Stop, never Resume.
A live goblin whose heavy step waits its turn in the machine's gate line, one that reported `--waiting-on memory` or sits idle while a run of its waits there, holds its memory and reads its place from its fleet tree's `line_place`, such as 2nd in line for the machine, or In line for the machine before the supervisor has read it, never memory alone (the Overlord, 2026-11-02: "How is a goblin waiting on memory yet it's still in progress?", #1924).
One that reported `--waiting-on memory` whose fleet tree is read with a `line_place` of 0, since its run holds the turn or none of its runs waits in the line, reads Working, never Waiting on memory.
A goblin whose done report is in reads Landing on a train while a merge train carries its pull request and Landing, waits to merge otherwise, even before any test of it is recorded, until the test's own words say it failed, was cancelled or landed.
Once its pull request merged, it reads Merged, verifying instead, and so does a delivered goblin not yet verified whose pull request merged.
A goblin that closed its issue, or a local-only goblin that kept its report, in place of a pull request has nothing left to land or verify: it reads Done and stays In progress until cleanup retires it.
A paused card's status is drawn in grey, never in yellow.
The layout button in the header, left of the Command Center, switches the board between the kanban and a stacked layout, one column under another; its tooltip names the layout it switches to, and the browser remembers the choice in local storage, falling back to the kanban when storage is unavailable.
A board 960 px wide or narrower, such as a phone or a narrow window beside the panel, stacks either way, so a card never squeezes its title or status; the CEO's bar and a column's heading wrap too, so nothing on the board is clipped or scrolls sideways at any width.
While the board is that narrow and the kanban is the layout chosen, the layout button would change nothing, so it is dimmed and does nothing, and its name says why to a screen reader: the board is too narrow for columns side by side; close or narrow the panel, or widen the window.
While the stacked layout is chosen the button is never dimmed and always switches to the kanban, so the kanban can be reached again in a window that holds it.
A small screen (40rem or less), such as a phone, has no layout button, since a board that narrow is always stacked.
Beside a kanban board an open panel yields by default: in a workspace wider than 1410 px the board keeps 980 px (the 961 its three columns need, 2 for its border and up to 17 for its scroll bar) and the panel takes the rest, never under its own 420 px, so a window 1707 px wide (a 2560 by 1600 screen at 150 percent) shows the board at 980 and the panel at about 690.
A workspace no wider than 1410 px cannot hold the three columns beside a panel, so it keeps the even split, as the stacked layout and Orchestration do at every width.
The rule is `.workspace.with-pane.kanban` in `frontend/src/styles.css`, which `App.tsx` switches on with the `kanban` class while the Board view shows the kanban; a width set by dragging the divider is remembered and wins, and so does an open Changes section.
Until 2 October 2026 the panel took half at every width, which left the board 835 px on that window, one column under either layout.
Tasks lists backlog rows and briefs nothing has started: a `data/<id>/brief.md` with no live task record, status log or archive entry.
An existing delivered outcome keeps a stale Queued row out of Tasks and refuses Start, while its completed history remains available.
After verifying delivery and returning the worktree with cleanup, the CEO runs `goblins backlog done <id>` to move its legacy bold or checkbox Queued row to canonical checked form under Done, retaining its entire continuation, delivery evidence and archived source.
The command refuses live metadata, missing or unreadable delivery evidence, duplicate source rows and parked work, and repeating a completed closeout changes nothing.
Tasks and In progress are in priority order, top first, and every list of tasks the board shows follows it; Completed stays newest first, by when each piece of work finished: a stop when it stopped, delivered work when its goblin reported done, and a merged pull request no goblin reported when it merged.
In progress never pages: every goblin's card is in the column at once, in priority order, and the board scrolls when they run past the screen, with the Paused group after the last of them.
The browser draws only the In progress cards in or near the view, and each card out of it keeps the room it took when last drawn, so a long fleet costs a scroll no more than the cards it shows (#1599).
A Playwright trace's DOM snapshot reads every element's scroll position, which lays out each card the browser skipped, about a second an action on a long list, so the tests that hold one keep their traces without DOM snapshots.
Tasks, the Paused group and Completed show a list of up to ten cards whole, with no pager, and the board scrolls when they run past the screen.
Past ten, each of them shows a page: as many of its cards as fit, Completed its newest, in the visible board below the list when the columns are side by side and in one screen below the column's heading when they are stacked, less room for the pager, and never fewer than five cards, when the board scrolls; each page holds as many of its own cards as fit, row by row with each row as tall as its tallest card, and starts where the page before it ended, so a tall card shortens only its own page.
The floor of five is there because the page was once sized by the visible height alone, and on 2 October 2026 the memory meter and tall cards left a page of 19 queued tasks room for two.
A card's height is measured at the list's width, those not shown yet in a hidden container of no height beside the list, so the pages come from real heights.
A pager under a list, when one page does not hold it all, says which cards show, 1–5 of 18, with earlier and next buttons, and a sideways touch swipe of at least 48 px turns the page while a vertical one scrolls; in Tasks a swipe that starts on a card's rank is a drag, not a page turn.
A drag places a card anywhere in its list: held within 64 px of the top or bottom edge of what scrolls the list (the board's canvas, the CEO's panel or the page), the card waits while the list scrolls under it, up to 20 px a frame and only while it is held there, and in Tasks or Paused a card held for 0.6 seconds over a page arrow turns the page, takes the last place on the page it turned to, and turns again for as long as it stays there.
A keyboard move past the page's edge carries the page with the card, keeping its focus.
A card or list that fails to render tells the CEO why and shows only an icon-only Retry in its place, while the rest of the board stays usable.
Tasks lists the backlog's Queued rows in file order, then briefs without a row; In progress lists the goblins in the attention order kept in `state/attention.json`, then any goblin not placed yet, and `goblins fleet-view` lists its goblins in that order too.
Dragging a card, or Alt+Up and Alt+Down on a focused one, sends the whole list's new order to `POST /api/order` with the board's token, which the Host, Origin and token checks guard like every other change.
In progress and its Paused group are one attention order, by which the supervisor also resumes paused goblins, so a drop in either sends the working goblins' order followed by the paused goblins' order as the board shows them, and a drop among the working goblins keeps the paused order.
A Tasks order rewrites only the order of the rows in `data/backlog.md`'s Queued section, each row moving with its indented detail lines while notes, parked rows, rows with live metadata or a delivered outcome and every other section stay where they are, and a brief without a row gets one, `- [ ] <id> - <id> (repo: <project>)`, at the place it was dropped.
Task edits also emit unchecked checkbox rows, which both CEO and tasks-axi read, while CEO continues reading existing bold rows.
An order that is not exactly the queue the file holds, because a row was added, removed or renamed after the board showed it, is refused with 409 and changes nothing; an In progress order naming a goblin with no live task record is refused the same way.
The board shows the dropped order until a snapshot from the revision the save answered with arrives, and a refused order goes back with the reason under its column.
The snapshot's `memory` is the machine's available physical memory, the standby list included, and its available commit (RAM plus page file, which every process's private memory is charged against and which a new process needs even while physical memory looks free), both read with `GlobalMemoryStatusEx`, beside the fleet's 4 GB floor and the 5 GB mark at which the next queued task starts; the meter at the head of Tasks shows it as a number and a bar spanning twice the 5 GB mark, or the machine's memory if that is less, marked at the floor and at the mark, whose fill turns amber under the mark and red under the floor, and the first queued task that is not blocked is marked Next up.
Its `slot_hold` says why the supervisor starts and resumes no goblin by itself, a new session of about 1 GB leaving the machine's heavy line short of the floor it keeps for its next test run, and is empty when it may or the line cannot be read; while it is set over the mark the fill stays amber and the bar's tip adds **Nothing starts by itself, keeping the test slots open:** and it after the marks.
The snapshot's `scheduling` is what the scheduler made of its last reading with memory free: `text`, what it started or resumed or why nothing waiting started, and `waiting`, each task or paused goblin that could run and did not, with why; it is absent while memory is short, while a restart's goblins come back and on a board that cannot start goblins.
When nothing waits to start, `text` is empty.
The board reads none of it: whatever the scheduler did, no text sits under a meter's bar or between two meters, and with memory free and no `slot_hold` the memory meter's screen-reader text is **Enough memory for the next task**.
It also carries the kernel's paged and nonpaged pool sizes, read with `GetPerformanceInfo`, since a pool that keeps growing is a driver leaking memory, `pool_line`, the line past which the PC is better restarted, `pool_growth`, the pool tags that grew most since the Windows sign-in began, and, while commit is the tighter of the two, `holders`: the three apps holding the most commit, each counting its first process and every process it started, so the Codex app's MCP servers count as its program rather than as python, read from one system process list.
It also carries `handle_holder`, the process holding the most handles open, by its pid, program, start and count, read from the same kind of system process list, which opens no process, with its command line only when it holds a million or more.
The meter shows whichever of free memory and free commit is the tighter, memory on a tie, labelled "Commit free (memory plus page file)" when it is commit and followed by one line naming those apps; Start's tooltip, the Next chip and Resume's tooltip follow the tighter one too.
Under it, **Kernel pools** shows both pools together on a bar spanning twice the line, marked at the line, whose fill turns red past it; its tip gives each pool and the tags that grew most since the sign-in began.
The pools are memory Windows holds for drivers that no goblin can use and that only a restart of the PC frees once a driver leaks it.

The supervisor's minute memory reading keeps a record of the pools for each Windows sign-in in `state/kernel-pools.json`: once every 15 minutes a sample of both pools' sizes, two weeks of them at most, and in the sign-in's first and last sample what each pool tag holding a megabyte or more held, read with `NtQuerySystemInformation`'s pool tag list, which needs no administrator rights.
`pool_growth` compares those two, and a new sign-in moves the record before it to `state/kernel-pools-before.json`, since the restart that frees a leak also ends the sign-in that recorded it.
Each sample also keeps `processes`, how many processes the PC started since it booted (the kernel's process objects, pool tag `Proc`), and `tokens`, how many tokens the kernel holds (pool tag `Toke`), so two samples read side by side say how many processes the PC starts an hour and how many tokens it keeps: on 2026-10-19 about two in five processes left their token behind for good, and the paged pool grew with them until the PC had to be restarted.
`board` names every process the board itself started since `board_started`, by program and by the code that started it, as every fleet program counts the processes it starts through `execx`.
A tag names the driver or kernel part that allocated the memory, as poolmon shows it, and the string search `findstr /m /l <tag> C:\Windows\System32\drivers\*.sys` names the driver files that carry it.

The line is 7.5 GB of the two pools together, measured on the Overlord's PC on 2026-10-18 as the point where free memory can no longer hold more than one goblin at a time.
That PC has 31.7 GB of memory.
Four days into a boot its pools held 10.96 GB, and with every goblin paused it had 3.6 to 6 GB free, 4.8 at the middle, so each goblin resumed was paused again at the floor within minutes.
A goblin's terminal holds about 0.8 GB (six measured, 0.70 to 1.06) and a run in the heavy line up to 2.5 GB, the line's own record, so two goblins at work, one of them in a heavy run, need 4.1 GB above the 4 GB floor: 8.1 GB free with no goblin, 3.3 GB more than those pools left.
The line is 10.96 less 3.3, 7.66 GB, kept at 7.5 so the board offers the restart before the fleet falls to one goblin at a time.

Past the line **Restart PC** shows on the line under the CEO panel's header row, before Update, on the board on this PC only: a board at any other address, his private board included, never shows it, as it never turns AFK mode on.
His press asks once, in a question that lists what the restart does, with Cancel focused, and his Restart PC sends `POST /api/pc/restart`.
Past the token and origin checks the supervisor takes it only from a board it proves the Overlord's own on this PC, as it proves one for AFK mode's on, refusing a press through his private board, through a proxy or from a browser an agent started with one short sentence under the button while the CEO is told what it found.
It refuses a press while the pools are under the line, on a board that cannot restart the PC, such as an example board, and while a restart runs.
Then, in order, it:

1. Tells the CEO first, with a `memory` wake keyed `restart`: what the pools hold, what happens next, and to start, resume and merge nothing and end its turn.
2. Waits up to two minutes for a start or a change of a goblin under way to end, while nothing starts, resumes, pauses at the memory floor or comes back by itself, and the Overlord's own Starts and Resumes wait their turn.
3. Pauses every working goblin at once through `goblins pause <id> --reason restart --until <when this sign-in began>`, which has each write its handoff before its terminal stops, as every pause does, and pauses a helper with its parent.
   A goblin whose latest report is `done` with a pull request, or with the issue it closed or the report it kept in place of one, and that took no prompt since, as one the CEO steers back to work takes, waits on its merge or on nothing and is not paused, so no resume brings it back after the sign-in.
   A restart pause leaves the goblin's open gate run running, as every pause does, so which run survives the restart depends on where it was.
   A run parked at a gate decision survives the Windows restart, and the resumed goblin carries on from it.
   A run whose step was executing ends failed at the restart with its head kept: the resume names it failed, its successor keeps its spent allowances, and what a gate fixer had not committed is not kept.
4. Reads back that each is paused for the restart. One that is not stops the restart short: Windows is not restarted, a few words under Restart PC say so for a moment and it is offered again, the CEO is told why, and the goblins already paused stay paused until the PC restarts or they are resumed.
5. Ends every run still in the machine's heavy line, holding a turn or waiting for one, each by its process id once that process proves to be a `goblins` run, whose command ends with it in its job and whose turn goes back to the line.
6. Waits up to three minutes for the CEO's turn to end, as Update waits for it.
7. Tells the CEO what the restart did, then restarts Windows with `InitiateShutdown`, closing no app by force, so one with unsaved work asks him first, and starting again after he signs in each app registered to be.

A test binary never restarts Windows, and every test of the sequence replaces that call with a fake.
After he signs in, the comeback brings the CEO back, on its conversation where it can, and the scheduler resumes each goblin paused for the restart, since its pause clears in any sign-in after the one it was taken in, one at a time as memory allows and in his In progress order.
The snapshot's `pc_restart` says `stopping` while the fleet stops, `restarting` once Windows was asked to restart, and `stopped` with its `problem` when the restart stopped short, and Restart PC reads Restarting the PC while it runs.
The snapshot's `subscriptions` lists `claude` and `codex` only while a registered CEO, or a goblin whose terminal the monitor saw active, busy, idle, parked or harness-erroring within the last two minutes, runs that harness; a paused, ended, archived or launching task, a saved model and quota data alone never show one.
No billing proof is read: every goblin's terminal strips the harness billing keys before the harness starts, so a harness the fleet starts runs on its subscription.
Each entry carries that provider's weekly `quota-axi` window (`seven_day` for Claude, `weekly` for Codex) as `percent_remaining`, `resets_at`, `read_at` and `status` (`available`, `stale`, `unavailable` or `auth_required`), and `floor_percent`, and nothing else: no account, balance or provider error.
`serve` reads `quota-axi` once a minute, in the fleet pass that checks the allowance floor, and every read refreshes the dials.
That pass also reads each provider's weekly floor, `config/fleet.json`'s `weekly_floor_percent` (5 unless set), and `floor_percent` is the floor it paused by, or null while that file cannot be read, when the pass pauses nothing at a floor and the board's error says why.
The dials' own read, under a 15-second bound, runs only when no read was made for a minute and a half, so the supervisor never asks Anthropic's usage endpoint twice at once, which refused one of each pair as rate limited on 2026-10-08.
A read that measures nothing, refused or failed, leaves the reading before it, which reads `stale` once it is older than an hour or its week has reset, while a sign-in the provider asks for replaces it at once.
A snapshot only reads what the reads kept.
The Tasks column draws each as a ring around the provider's mark beside the memory meter, filled to the remaining percentage, turning amber within five points of the floor, and a dashed ring with **?** for anything but a fresh reading; its tip gives the reset time in the viewer's time zone, the reading's age and the reserve: **5% reserve** for a floor of 5, **5% reserve reached** at or under it, **No reserve, runs to 0%** for a floor of 0 and **Reserve unknown** while the floor cannot be read.
The snapshot's `key_spend` states what an API key a harness bills by, rather than by a subscription window, has spent: Pi's OpenRouter key, whose spend is shared by every caller of that key and is never a sum of what the fleet's goblins estimate they used.
It carries one entry for each distinct credential among the live Pi sessions, and none for a Pi session on another provider: a session's provider is the one its model names in Pi's own `provider/model` form, else Pi's `defaultProvider` in the profile's settings, and a session whose records name none is shown as unproven rather than attributed.
A credential source is the profile folder and the environment a session's terminal started with, as the fleet's launch records name them: the project's worktree manifest `env` over the machine's user environment, read as a spawn reads it, and Pi's own `~/.pi/agent` when neither names `PI_CODING_AGENT_DIR`.
`serve` resolves the key the way Pi's own resolver does, `auth.json`'s provider entry ahead of `OPENROUTER_API_KEY`: `$NAME` and `${NAME}` read the entry's own environment and then the session's, an empty value in either falls through, a braced name that is no environment name stands as written, and an entry that resolves its key by a command is refused rather than run, so drawing a dial executes nothing.
It then reads `GET https://openrouter.ai/api/v1/key` under a 10-second bound on the dials' own timer, once for each source a snapshot found a live OpenRouter session using, and never while a snapshot is built.
The entry carries the key's own dollars in the window its own limit resets over as `spent_usd`, `limit_usd`, `remaining_usd`, `period` (`daily`, `weekly`, `monthly`, `lifetime`, or empty for a window this build cannot count), `resets_at` and `read_at`, the external `byok_usd` beside them and never added to them, `includes_byok`, and `blocking` with the budget layer that stops requests first when that is not the key's own (`scope`, `spent_usd`, `limit_usd`, `period`), so a member or workspace budget is never called the key's spend.
A window with no budget row of its own still rolls over at its documented UTC boundary, the next midnight for a daily window, the next Monday's midnight for a weekly one and the first of the next month for a monthly one, so a reading taken in a window that has ended shows stale rather than as this period's spend.
`status` is `available`, `missing`, `stale`, `auth_required`, `rate_limited`, `unavailable` or `unattributed`, and `detail` says why in a few words this build fixes: no provider reason phrase, document or configuration text reaches it, and an answer with no key document or no usage figure is no reading at all.
A reading is `unattributed` when the fleet's records do not prove it is the credential a session started with: a key whose value came from the environment, whose file was written after that session started, or for which no provider or session start was recorded, never shows its dollars as the value, and the tip names the last dollars by their age and why they are unproven.
No key, authorization header, auth document or credential file time reaches the snapshot: `key_id` is a hash of the credential the reading was made with, so a rotated key never shows the dollars of the key it replaced, and `profile` names the Pi profile in use, `default` for Pi's own folder.
`serve` also wakes the CEO about the machine and the forge, through the same wake queue as every other wake, so `goblins drain` shows them and each says what to do next in one line.
`memory_ready` (wake kind `memory`): `serve` reads the memory meter once a minute, and once available memory and commit both read at or above the 5 GB mark on two readings in a row, while a queued task could start or a live goblin's latest report is `waiting on memory`, and a new goblin's session, about 1 GB, would still leave the machine's heavy line the floor it keeps for its next test run, it wakes the CEO once, naming the floor it kept for that next test run and how many runs wait in the line, so it never reads as a start at the 5 GB mark while the line refuses one (#2369), and the next startable task in queue order and the goblins to release, and never the task the supervisor is starting already; it wakes again only after a reading falls under the 4 GB floor and crosses back, and never twice within fifteen minutes.
`ci_finished` (wake kind `ci`): every two minutes `serve` asks GitHub's API from its own process, as the account `gh` is signed in as, about each watched repository, which are the repositories live goblins work in, kept for six hours after the last one leaves.
Each poll makes one PR list, one batched GraphQL comparison when the repository has open PRs the fleet watches, one read of the account `gh` works as when a listed PR is no goblin's by what the goblin said or has checked out, or when a goblin's own is behind with its checks passed, and one main-run list per watched repository, plus one run read for each red run it reports; it lists every open PR, even in a repository with no live goblin.
It also asks git, and never GitHub, two things in each live goblin's worktree: the branch it has checked out and its reflog, which names every branch it had checked out before.
Each `ci_finished` it raises costs one more GraphQL call, which reads the tests that failed once.
When every check on a live goblin's open pull request has concluded (the one it recorded, reported `done`, or opened from a branch its worktree has or had checked out), it wakes the CEO once, keyed by the goblin, with the result and the names of the failing checks; a rerun that finishes with a new result wakes again.
In a repository whose checkout has a workflow its pull requests trigger (`pull_request` in a file under `.github/workflows`), the rollup is finished only once a check run of a workflow is in it: GitGuardian or Vercel alone is not, and a head no workflow reports for within ten minutes of the rest wakes once saying no workflow ran.
A pull request that conflicts with its base raises no `ci_finished`, since GitHub runs none of its workflows; its `pr_health` conflict wake says its workflows cannot run.
When the newest push run of a workflow on a repository's default branch finishes red, it wakes the CEO once for that run, keyed `main:<repository>`, naming the workflow, the failing jobs and the run id; a cancelled run is not red.
Every `ci_finished` wake names the tests that failed once and passed on their second try in the runs it reports, which left those runs green: a pull request's wake for the workflow runs of its head, and the wake for a red run of the default branch or for a paused goblin's awaited run for that run alone.
A workflow that gives a failed test a second try names each such test in a warning titled Failed once on one of its checks, and the supervisor reads the warnings of GitHub Actions' checks on the commit as it raises the wake, never on a poll that raises none.
A read that fails, or that finds no workflow check at all, is said in the wake as tests that could not be read, never taken for none.
A pull request with no workflow check is not read.
A merge train makes the same read as its run passes, for the workflow runs of the train's head, and names those tests in its record (`failed_once_tests`, or `failed_once_unread` with why) and in its `merge_train:` message.
Runs are judged newest first by their ids, and GitHub sometimes answers with an older page of them, so a listing whose newest run is older than one a poll already read for that repository, before or since it was last watched, is passed over until the next poll.
One pull request wakes at most once every five minutes, and one workflow on one repository's default branch wakes at most once every five minutes, so a push that turns two workflows red raises two wakes, each naming its own workflow and run.
A repository with no `origin` remote is a local one, and one whose `origin` is a path, a `file://` URL or a host GitHub does not serve is hosted somewhere this monitor does not read: the fleet reads a repository's pull requests, tickets, trains and task evidence on `github.com` only, so neither is watched - `gh` is asked nothing about it, it raises no error and no wake, and it is never answered with `gh`'s sign-in advice.
The `origin` is read through Git's effective configuration, so one kept in an included or per-worktree configuration is honored rather than read as none.
The checkout is read before its `origin`, since git answers from the nearest repository above a folder as well as from the one in it, so a recorded checkout that is missing, that is no Git checkout, that is now only a folder inside another repository, or that git refuses to read keeps one line in the supervisor's error naming it and is never polled as another project; whether git answered no checkout or a refusal is decided by whether the folder holds its own `.git`, never by the words of git's message, and a refusal is reported with git's bounded, redacted words.
An `origin` that names a GitHub host but no repository is reported the same way.
A `github.com` repository that still cannot be read (`gh` is signed out, a read of GitHub fails, or its default branch cannot be found) keeps one line in the supervisor's error on the board, naming it, from the first failing poll until a poll reads it again.
Once the same failure has been met on three polls in a row it wakes the CEO once as `ci_unreadable` (wake kind `ci`), keyed `repo:<repository>`, with the error and the next step that failure names: restore the checkout at its recorded path or correct the project path, check the checkout's Git repository, its ownership and its access, repair the checkout's Git configuration, set `origin` to the repository's own URL, or for a read of GitHub sign `gh` in with `gh auth login`, point `origin` at GitHub, or set `origin`'s default branch with `git remote set-head origin -a`.
A read that times out under the fleet's load, as listings did on 2026-10-08 and took about 600 ms minutes later, is read again on the next poll and wakes nobody.
It does not wake again while the failure stays the same; a different failure met on three polls in a row wakes again, and so does one that cleared and came back, never twice within five minutes for one repository, and a failure clearing raises no wake.
The failure is compared by its exact text, so one whose text differs on every poll keeps its line on the board and does not wake, and one whose text changes, as when a goblin starts in a repository `gh` cannot read, wakes once more.
A 403, 429 or exhausted allowance pauses every later GitHub read in that repository, across restarts, until its retry or reset time, or an hour when GitHub gives no usable time; the reads it holds fail with the same text every time, so a refusal that never clears still wakes once as `ci_unreadable` on its second retry.
`pr_health` (wake kind `pr`), keyed `health:<owner>/<name>`: a watched PR that conflicts with its base or falls behind the default branch wakes the CEO once per condition and head, naming the owning goblin and the safe update, or a teammate's author and link.
A PR is a live goblin's when the goblin recorded it or reported it `done`, when its head is the branch the goblin's worktree has checked out, or when the account `gh` works as opened it from a branch one of the goblin's worktrees, its own or an extra one, had checked out before.
The supervisor reads those branches from each worktree's reflog at every poll and keeps none of it in memory, so a goblin on its second branch still owns the PR of its first, before and after a restart of the supervisor or of the goblin.
On 2026-10-10 seven wakes called a goblin's own PR a teammate's, each raised after its worktree had moved to the goblin's next branch and before the goblin reported the PR `done`.
A PR another account opened is a goblin's only when the goblin said so or has its branch checked out, so a branch a goblin only looked at makes no teammate's PR its own.
A PR no live goblin holds is a teammate's when another account than the one `gh` works as opened it, and the fleet's own account's, the Overlord's own or a finished task's, when that account did: each is reported with its author and link, and the fleet pushes to neither.
While that account cannot be read, such a wake says only that no live goblin holds the PR.
One rule decides a behind head for every PR a live goblin holds that the account `gh` works as opened in this repository: once its checks have passed it wakes no one, since a merge train tests the default branch and its riders together, whether the goblin reported the PR `done` yet, whichever of its branches it is on, and whether it is held.
A behind head whose checks are still running waits until they conclude, for three hours at most, as long as a train waits for its own run, and is judged then.
A behind head wakes the CEO when its checks failed or it has none, and when no train will take the PR, a teammate's and one no live goblin holds, and a conflict always does.
The fleet watches a goblin's own PR wherever it is, and every open PR in a repository the fleet owns: one whose owner, as GitHub puts it in the PR's own address and never as a remote's name says, is the account `gh` works as (GitHub's `user`) or an organization `config/fleet.json`'s `github_owners` names, compared without regard to case.
Another owner's PRs are neither compared nor reported: on 2026-10-07 a checkout of a fork whose `origin` was the upstream raised 39 wakes for strangers' PRs there.
A poll raises at most one `pr_health` wake per repository, naming each PR whose head fell into a condition the CEO was not woken for; a PR whose head and condition stay as they were is never named again, and a repository's wakes are at least five minutes apart, a held one waiting for the gap.
GitHub lists a head whose mergeability it has not worked out yet as `UNKNOWN`, so a behind head waits up to ten minutes for that answer and is then reported once, as conflicting or as behind, rather than behind and then conflicting.
The comparison asks the repository the PRs' own addresses name, never one a remote of the checkout names.
If GitHub cannot name that account, only goblins' PRs are watched in that poll, and the failure keeps one line in the supervisor's error, as `pr_unread` does.
`pr_unread` (wake kind `pr`), keyed `repo:<repository>`: an open PR head whose comparison was not read, and a listing that stops at its limit of 100 open PRs, keep one line in the supervisor's error on the board, during a backoff too, until a poll reads them all.
A head whose own comparison comes back missing or invalid in an otherwise readable response, conflicting or not, wakes the CEO once, and the listing limit once until the listing falls under 100 and comes back, never twice within five minutes for one repository; the wake names the heads, the reason and the next step, and recurring poll errors raise no further wake.
A comparison request that fails as a whole (a refusal, a timeout, a server error or a response with no comparisons) keeps only its line on the board and wakes for no head, so a head it left unread still wakes once if its own comparison later fails; a refusal reaches the CEO as `ci_unreadable`.
It never raises `ci_unreadable`, and CI wakes and readable PR health from that repository continue.
`pr_overlap` (wake kind `pr`), keyed by the goblin: the same poll checks new teammate PRs and issues against running goblins, sharing one overlap read of the repository that starts at most every ten minutes under the existing allowance/refusal backoff, and reading each goblin's area under a thirty-second deadline of its own.
The overlap read asks for the viewer, the open PRs with their files and the open issues, and no branch: it compares no branch's files, so no branch can meet a goblin's area, and GitHub lists branches by name, never by their last push, so on 2026-10-08 paging the 403 branches of `fpresta0607/code-goblins` under one deadline timed out pass after pass.
A poll reads two pages of it, each under a thirty-second deadline of its own.
A read with more pages, or one whose page failed, takes up where it left off on the next poll rather than starting over, and the overlaps its pages read so far are checked as they arrive.
The area is the branch's committed change from its default-branch merge base, including renamed and deleted paths, with issue words from the brief's Task and Acceptance criteria.
An item must have opened strictly after the goblin's current spawn generation began; an item opened during the generation stays eligible if later committed changes first make it overlap.
Pre-existing open items remain visible in the ordinary ticket report and do not wake on polling startup, restart or area changes.
The wake names the teammate, item link, overlap and goblin, asking the CEO whether to continue, wait or narrow the work.
Fresh live evidence is checked before and after the read, including after a per-PR done report; stopped or paused goblins, bots, the signed-in viewer, branch-only work and the goblin's own recorded or claimed issues in that repository never produce these wakes.
Each item wakes once per goblin generation across restart, acknowledgement, closure and area changes.
Unknown item or generation timestamps, unread authors and incomplete reads keep a board line without guessing times or raising `ci_unreadable`, while readable overlaps continue.
They wake the CEO as `supervisor_error` only once the read went unread on three polls in a row.
A goblin's area is read on the branch it has checked out at that moment, never one read at the start of the poll; a branch, head or base its own git work moves during the read is read again once, and a goblin still moving is left for the next pass, never reported: on 2026-10-08 a goblin that switched branches between the poll's start and its read showed as a failed read for ten minutes.
What the fleet wakes remember, in `state/fleet-wakes.json`, survives a restart, so a restart neither repeats a wake nor loses one, and a failing repository's line shows again at once.
The board lists no queued wakes of any kind: these reach the CEO as the others do, through its Stop hook or the line typed into a Codex or pi CEO's terminal.
A Claude Code CEO's Stop hook arms while a task is in flight or queued work could start, so a red main with neither reaches it at its next turn end with one.
A snapshot with no commit limit has not reported commit, so the meter shows memory rather than zero commit, and the labels under the bar give way toward the left so that neither leaves the box when a mark is at the end of the bar.
The snapshot's `disk` is the free space and size of the less free of the drive the home is on and the Dev Drive `config/dev-drive.json` names, read with `GetDiskFreeSpaceEx`, beside `config/fleet.json`'s disk floor (`disk_floor_gb`, default 15 GB), under which no goblin and no gate test run starts, and the lower mark at which the CEO is woken (`disk_wake_gb`, default 10 GB); every start, wake and AFK disk decision reads that one reading.
Its `drives` are each distinct drive among the home, the Dev Drive and the gate home (`NM_HOME`, or `~\.no-mistakes`), each named by its letter, with `dev_drive` on the Dev Drive and `error` on one that cannot be read; two folders on one drive are one entry, and a gate home that cannot be read, as before the first gate run, defers to a readable folder on its drive, while a home or Dev Drive folder that cannot be read marks its drive with `error`.
When the home's drive or the Dev Drive cannot be read the snapshot still carries the drives, so the board shows which one it is, while every start refuses as before.
In the memory meter's box, under memory, a **Disk free** line shows each drive, as `Disk free (C:)` or `Disk free (D: Dev Drive)`, with a number and a bar spanning twice the floor, or the whole drive if that is less, marked at the floor and the mark, whose fill turns amber under the floor and red under the mark; a drive that cannot be read reads **Unreadable** with no bar, and its tip says why.
A reading redraws only the drive lines it moved, and nothing in the meter animates.
Start's tooltip and the Next chip name free disk when it is what is short.
`idle` (wake kind `idle`), keyed `fleet`: while work the scheduler could not start (a start or resume that failed, or a row that needs the CEO) waits with memory free and nothing started for 30 minutes, `serve` wakes the CEO naming each waiting task and why, and again every 30 minutes it lasts; work that waits on another task, on memory or already finished, or a picked-up [root issue](contract/root-issues.md#root-issues) read closed, wakes nobody.
Keyed `turn`: when the CEO's turn ends with no goblin at work while work that could run waits and memory and commit are free, the turn is reopened with the next work named, a paused goblin whose pause cleared first, then the top of the queue, with the exact `goblins` command; Claude Code's turn-end guard blocks the stop with it, and for a Codex or pi CEO the supervisor queues it from the turn end the native hook spooled and types it into its terminal.
The same next work reopens a turn at most once every 30 minutes, and only the session holding the home, or the registered native CEO, is reopened.
`stale_row:` (wake kind `notify`), keyed by the task: a row under `## Queued` whose task the CEO retired, with `goblins cleanup` or `goblins kill`, and that has no brief written since is not started by the scheduler, which wakes the CEO once for that retirement naming the row, so the CEO moves it to `## Done` or queues new work under a new id.
`ceo_wait:` (wake kind `check`), keyed by the goblin: a live goblin that has waited 10 minutes on the CEO, by a `waiting on ceo` report, a `done` on a pull request no merge train takes because it is a draft or held, or a wait or pause on a backlog row of kind `goblins`, done or not, wakes the CEO naming what it waits for and how long, and again at most every 30 minutes until it reports again, takes a prompt, a pause takes hold, it stops or is retired ([Supervision](contract/supervision.md#supervision)).
`disk_low` (wake kind `disk`): `serve` reads the disk with the memory meter, and when free disk falls under the mark it wakes the CEO once with what is free, what each part of the home holds, what the gate's home holds, which bucket holds the most and what the janitor last freed; it wakes again only after a reading back at or above the floor, and never twice within fifteen minutes.
`handles:` (wake kind `memory`), keyed `handles`: `serve` reads which process holds the most handles open once a minute, and when one holds a million or more it wakes the CEO once for that process, naming its pid, program, start, count and command line and how to end it by its pid, `goblins reap --force <pid> --apply`; it wakes again only for another process or after a reading under the line.
A normal machine's busiest process holds about 9,000 handles, and the system handle table a pause reads to find a goblin's job is bounded near 6.7 million, which a recursive search of the whole drive filled on 2026-11-11; a pause that meets that bound now names the process holding the most handles in its error.
`heavy_line_stalled` (wake kind `idle`), keyed `heavy-line`: with the memory meter `serve` reads the machine's heavy line, and when its readings in a row have found a turn free, runs waiting and memory and commit at the floor of the run at its head for five minutes, it wakes the CEO once with the turns, the runs in line, the memory against the floor and, when one live process has held the line's lock a minute or more, that lock's owner pid and how long it has held it ([goblins gate turns](commands/gate/turns.md)). It wakes again only after a reading finds the line moving, waiting on memory or unread, and memory under the floor clears it too (#2377).
`defender_detection` and `defender_upload` (wake kind `check`), keyed `defender`: every ten minutes `serve` reads what Microsoft Defender recorded under the fleet's folders, as `goblins defender` does, and wakes the CEO once for each detection and once for each sample Defender sent to Microsoft that it has not told the CEO of, naming the file and the test and task that made it. The first reading only marks where the watch begins, so what Defender recorded before raises nothing, and each later one reads from an hour back of the one before. It only reads Defender's own records and changes nothing of Defender's. A Defender that cannot be read on three readings in a row is said on the board as a failed read, and raises no wake.
`window_shown`, `start_loop` and `left_running` (wake kind `check`), keyed by the goblin, or `goblins` for the CEO's terminal: every five seconds `serve` places what each running goblin and the CEO's terminal started, as the family tree places a goblin's processes, and wakes the CEO once for a process that shows on the Overlord's screen, once for a command line one owner started as ten new processes within two minutes, and once for each done or failed report after which the goblin rests with something it started still running a minute later ([Supervision](contract/supervision.md#supervision)). Each is told once across restarts of `serve`, and none ends anything. A console window a goblin's process shows is hidden at the reading that sees it, once in the process's life, and its `window_shown` wake says the supervisor hid it. The console of a program running as an administrator, above the supervisor, is left, since Windows lets no process attach to it, and its wake says it shows.
`allowance_warning` (wake kind `allowance`), keyed by the provider: with the same once-a-minute `quota-axi` read, when a window that bounds a running goblin's model passes 85 percent used, `serve` wakes the CEO once for that window and reset, naming the window, when it renews and the goblins on it.
`allowance_renewed` (wake kind `allowance`), keyed by the provider: a session window, or a weekly one under a floor of 0, seen used up while goblins ran on it is kept in `fleet-wakes.json` until it renews, and meanwhile the scheduler starts and resumes nothing on that harness; at the renewal `serve` wakes the CEO once naming the goblins it stopped, since Claude Code goes on by itself only where its screen offered to, so the CEO peeks each and sends an idle one on. Such a goblin is waited out, never paused, and a weekly floor above 0 still pauses goblins before their week is used up.
`shared_busy` and `shared_ready` (wake kind `shared`), keyed by the service: once a minute `serve` reads the gate daemon, the heavy-run line, the Docker engine and the Scrawl server into `state/shared-services.json`, which [`goblins shared`](commands/shared.md) prints, and when goblins report `waiting on gate-daemon` or `waiting on docker` while it is not usable it wakes the CEO once, however many wait, naming them, and once more when it is usable, naming the goblins to tell, each told once; a wait on the heavy line is a wait on memory, which `memory_ready` tells. A spinning Scrawl server raises one `shared_busy` for each spin and no `shared_ready`.
No card on the board carries an action button, in any column: a card shows its goblin, its status, its project, when it last worked and its ticket, pull request and harness mark as icons, and a click opens its panel, where its actions are (the Overlord, 2026-10-16: "remove all pause, stop. buttons as well as edit and stop buttons from the Goblin Board cards").
A queued task's panel carries Start, a labelled button beside Remove, unless the task is blocked, when its status shows what it waits on instead.
