A goblin's card there holds its rows as one group in the middle of the size the canvas gives every card: its name, status, project and clock start at one left edge beside its portrait with no more than a line's gap between them, and its marks end the clock's row (`frontend/tests/orchestration-card-rows.spec.ts`).
A baby goblin's card there is the same shape at its smaller size, its head beside its rows, with its name on up to three lines, what it runs on up to two and its state whole on one line, even the longest, Unknown just started.
The lineage list puts the clock at that same edge in a card no wider than 360px, while a narrow column of the board keeps it at the card's edge, where a numbered card's marks leave it room.
A wider card in the lineage list keeps the board's one-row card, with the clock at its top right corner, and in either a wider card's marks take at most half the list's width beside its text, so Browser active never squeezes its name and status.
A card there, or in a column of the board, no wider than 360px keeps its marks on the clock's row while the widest of them has room beside the clock and gives them the row under it when it has none, so Browser active, the one mark no line can break, is never drawn over the clock.
Marks too many to fit beside the clock take a second line, and the clock stays right under the project (`frontend/tests/orchestration-card-rows.spec.ts`).
Dragging a card or using Alt plus an arrow key changes only its saved browser position; connectors retain their reported parent identity.
The tree fits and centers itself in the visible canvas, scaled up to fill it but never past 125% and down as far as 10%, so the whole of any tree shows, and refits whenever the panel opens or closes, the window resizes, a goblin appears or leaves, or a dragged card is dropped.
The canvas moves and zooms like a map instead of scrolling: dragging it, from empty space, a line or a baby goblin, moves the view, and the wheel or a trackpad pinch zooms between 10% and 150% about the point under the pointer; the plus and minus buttons and keys zoom about the middle.
Every line of the tree, from a goblin to a goblin under it and from a goblin to each of its baby goblins, leaves its parent's bottom on its own, beside its siblings' lines a line's gap apart in the order of the children they lead to, and ends at the middle of its child's top, at every zoom, after a drag and after Arrange (`frontend/tests/tree-branches.spec.ts`).
Moving or zooming the view stops the automatic fit until Fit, or the 0 key, restores it.
A family of goblins with no cards under them wraps into the rows that show it largest in the canvas's shape: one row in a wide canvas, more in a tall, narrow one such as the canvas beside an open panel.
Each row after the first is offset, by half a card where it can be, so its connectors drop clear of every line that runs down from a goblin above, its middle or a line beside or between its branches, and so never read as that goblin's.
A connector to a later row drops straight from its parent where nothing is in its way, and otherwise runs down the clear way nearest its card, past every card and baby goblin between and a line's gap from any other connector there, then turns toward its card in the gap above that card's row (`frontend/tests/tree-routes.spec.ts`).
A connector from a goblin whose baby goblins hang open runs down beside them on a line of its own, so it never passes behind one of its own baby goblins nor shares a line with their branches.
A goblin whose branches are wider than its card takes as many columns as they need, so no card or branch covers another.
A goblin waiting on another sits in the row under the one it waits on, half a card over, and the dashed line between them runs straight down what the two cards share; a sibling with nothing under it gives up that place and takes the nearest free one, and a chain or a cycle of waits keeps its family places.
Only a card the Overlord moves is saved, so the canvas keeps arranging every card he has not placed himself.
A card he placed stays where he put it, and an arranged card whose place it covers takes the nearest free place, along its row first and then the rows below, so the canvas never arranges a card onto another.
A connector pulses for a few seconds when its goblin reports a new status line or files a wake record; a report that lands while the board is hidden never plays later.
Arrange resets positions and opens every folded card, then frames the tree again; storage failures remain visible.
Narrow screens use a collapsible nested list whose branches carry no words (the Overlord, 2026-11-11); past the third level it stops indenting, and a card there shows no parent.

### The family tree

Under each live goblin the board shows what it has running: its sub-agents, the shells and monitors it left running in the background, the jobs of processes its harness started, and its gate run.
Its gate run is the no-mistakes run it names as its wait, or else its branch's newest run, and an ended run that started before the goblin's current generation is left out as an earlier goblin's.
The supervisor reads it every 15 seconds from what the harness and the machine already keep, and writes nothing to either (`internal/fleettree`), and serves it on the goblin's task in `GET /api/snapshot` as `tree`.
Claude Code's sub-agents, background shells and monitors come from the goblin's own conversation, with each sub-agent's transcript and each shell's output for its last activity and last line.
That conversation is the one the board recorded for the goblin's generation, or the one the running Claude Code process records for itself in `~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json` with its own creation time, never the newest file in the worktree's folder, where another session can work.
A Codex goblin's child agents come from their own rollouts, which name the thread that spawned them; pi records no sub-agents or background jobs, so a pi goblin shows none and none is guessed.
A job is one process the harness started after its first two minutes, with everything under it, named by what it does: a test run or build by its command line, a dev server by the port it listens on, a browser, or other; each shows its private memory, and the goblin's card and panel show the memory of the harness and everything under it.
A job a background shell or monitor runs counts in that child rather than as a baby goblin of its own: its first process evaluates the child's command, read back as typed from the quoting Claude Code and Windows wrap it in, or writes to the child's output file, as a PowerShell command does.
Git Bash runs each program of a loop or a pipeline by replacing its own process, which leaves the program with no parent, so a detached program, such as a sleep in a monitor's loop, counts in the child whose processes host the console it shares.
Any other job that is no test run, build, dev server, browser or machine service, such as a command the goblin runs in the foreground, a command wrapper or the gate line's waiter, counts in the goblin itself and is no baby goblin (the Overlord, 2026-10-13: "do we need to render the cards"), and the monitor still reads its processor use as the goblin's.
A detached job is shown too: a process whose parent has exited and that started after the goblin's harness, with everything under it, that is the goblin's by its terminal's mark or by the task's folders one of its processes works in or runs a program from ([Whose a process is](#whose-a-process-is)), such as a server or a browser bridge left by a command that returned, or a test suite a script started in the background.
It reaches the harness through no parent, so until 2026-10-09 the tree dropped it, and two browser bridges held 3.7 GB that no card showed.
Its card says Detached before its state, and its memory counts in the goblin's total.
A run or a script at work in it is the goblin's work, and the monitor and the stall check count its processor use as the goblin's progress: on 2026-10-11 a goblin's whole test suite ran detached for hours, under a launcher the goblin built in its scratch folder, and drew two false `progress_stalled` wakes.
A server or a browser left detached is no sign that the goblin works, so a leftover that serves or draws a page never hides a stalled goblin, and neither is a machine service a goblin started, such as the no-mistakes daemon, whose agents work for every gate.
The host above the harness is the goblin's terminal and never a job of its own, though it works in the task's folders.
Each process that reaches no harness is asked whose it is once, since its mark and the program it runs never change, and a tree is the goblin's by the first of its processes that says so, top down.
A Git Bash tool carries no mark and a script can unset it for what it starts, so a tree whose processes have said nothing yet is asked again as processes join it.
A tree that began before the goblin's harness, such as the Overlord's own browser with its renderers coming and going, was not started by the goblin and is never asked about.
A browser the shell started to open a link, such as a sign-in's, is the Overlord's from then on, though it started in the goblin's tree: known by the command line Windows registers for a link's browser (Chromium's `--single-argument`, Firefox's `-osint`), it and everything under it, its tabs and native messaging hosts included, are left out of every job and of the goblin's memory, here and in the start watch's placement, while a browser a goblin runs for its work stays the goblin's. The supervisor, `goblins serve` or an update's copy of it, is left out the same way, with everything it runs, such as its own gate-status reads in a goblin's worktree: it is the fleet's own service, though an update in a terminal started it, and neither it nor its console host carries that terminal's mark ([Supervision](contract/supervision.md)).
Each child carries a state: working, waiting (an idle job, or a gate waiting on a decision), done, failed, or silent, a working child with no activity for ten minutes, the monitor's stall interval.
A monitor is done once Claude Code says it ran out its time, which it records only as the monitor's last event.
A background shell or monitor whose processes have all ended is done once it has been quiet for those ten minutes, since Claude Code records no end of one stopped from its own screen or ended with the conversation it ran in, and the ten minutes leave time for the record of one that did end.
Until 2026-10-13 such children stayed silent for as long as their goblin ran, ten of them under one goblin for up to nine hours.
A child whose process still runs stays however quiet, such as a shell waiting in the gate line.
While the command line of one of the harness's processes cannot be read, no child is ended by its processes, since that process may be any child's.
Once the goblin's harness has ended, every child its record left open ended with it.
Each also carries when its source last changed and when it was read.
Each child is drawn as a baby goblin whose prop says its kind (a magnifying glass for a sub-agent, a laptop for a shell, binoculars for a monitor, glasses and a shield for the gate, an antenna for a dev server, goggles for a test run, a hard hat for a build, a tablet for a browser).
Its tip is its task: a sub-agent's prompt, a background shell's or monitor's command, what a job runs without its shell or its program's folder, a helper's task, or else what it does.
A baby goblin is named after its goblin in the order its goblin's children started, Kip Jr., then Kip II, Kip III and on, with a short title for its own job, as goblins are called by name and title.
The title is made from its own description as the one who does it, such as Board Starter for Start the board or Dictation Tester for Test dictation, and is its kind's own, such as Server Keeper or Process Wrangler, when the description starts with no such deed.
A helper goblin keeps its own name.
A [helper goblin](contract/dispatch.md#helper-goblins) the supervisor started for a goblin is a child of that goblin's tree too, drawn with the sub-agent's head and named Helper goblin in its tip: its title, its own tree's memory, and where its own records say it stands, working or silent by its own activity, waiting while it is paused or asks its parent, done once it reported done and failed once it reported failure.
A helper its parent's tree holds while its parent is in progress is not drawn again as a card of its own on the canvas or in the lineage list, as a sub-agent never is; any other keeps its card and hangs under its parent's card, the parent's session or, with none reported, its task, while its parent is in progress, and under the CEO while its parent is paused or finished, even while the last tree the supervisor read for that parent still lists it, as one its parent waits on keeps working, and every helper's card on the board says whose helper it is.
On the canvas a goblin's running children hang on branches under its card, each a baby goblin with its name and title, what it runs in plain words, its state and for how long in a plain chip, and its memory, and the rows below make room.
What it runs is the program its job starts, such as Docker Desktop, the Stripe listener, the app server on its port or a shell running a script, a sub-agent's, shell's or monitor's own description, or Helper goblin, and a command it cannot put in plain words reads as its program's name (`whatItRuns` in `frontend/src/fleet-tree.ts`).
None is dimmed, idle and silent ones included, since each can be opened, none shows a command line, and none is yellow: a silent one's chip and dot are as plain as an idle one's.
They hang in about as many columns as rows, up to four columns, centred under the card, so a big family grows down rather than across.
Each baby goblin has a branch of its own, and the branches leave the card's bottom side by side, a line's gap apart in the order they run, so none sits on another, none crosses another and none comes nearer than that gap to another line or to a baby goblin it does not lead to (`frontend/tests/tree-routes.spec.ts`).
A branch to a baby goblin in the first row drops into the middle of its top, after turning aside in a step of its own where it has to, the outermost nearest the card.
A branch to a baby goblin lower down drops through the gap beside its column on the side of the card's middle, never out past its column and back, then turns into the gap above it and drops into the middle of its top.
The middle column of three takes the gap either side with fewer lines, and a gap widens to keep its lines a line's gap apart.
Resting the pointer on a baby goblin or a card, or moving the keyboard to it, lights its own line from its parent end to end.
The chevron under the card folds them, with any cards under it, to their count, the same rounded rectangle as the baby goblins: a head and a number for each kind still running, how many work, are silent or idle, and their memory; pressing the count opens them again.
A finished child is not drawn there, so a goblin whose children have all finished shows neither; its panel still lists them.
At phone width the lineage list shows the count under the goblin's card, and pressing it opens each child on a rail, which runs down beside them and turns into the middle of each one's top, as the rail of a goblin's goblins does into the middle of each card's top.
The goblin's panel opens with What's working: its counts, then one row per running child with what it does, its state, for how long and its memory, a silent child's last line, the finished ones folded away, and when it was read.
A goblin's card on the board shows nothing of what runs under it: the canvas and its panel's What's working do.
Baby goblins live on the canvas and in the lineage list, never as cards of their own on the board's columns.

Pressing a baby goblin, on the canvas or on its rail, opens the same panel a goblin opens, with the baby goblin's name and its task on one line above its terminal.
One whose output holds nothing yet says No output yet, never a blank terminal.
A sub-agent's terminal is its own record as its goblin's harness keeps it, Claude Code's transcript of the sub-agent or a Codex child agent's rollout, which the board reads again every half second while it shows and draws as Claude Code draws a conversation, in the same terminal surface, font, size and colours as a goblin's terminal (the Overlord, 2026-10-15: "Baby goblins should look and feel just like the same Claude shells").
Its task is a block after > on Claude Code's grey, what the agent said follows ●, and each tool it called follows a ● in the colour of its outcome, green when it succeeded, red when it failed and grey while it runs, with the tool's name in bold and what it was called on.
Under a call, what came back follows ⎿ in grey, its first lines with the rest folded into +N lines, and an error in red.
Every line wraps where the terminal's row ends and goes on under the text, never under its mark, and while the baby goblin works, Claude Code's working line turns in its orange under the last line.
It follows new lines while its last lines show and keeps its place while the Overlord reads further up.
Claude Code can draw a transcript itself only by running a session on it, which would not follow the record as it grows, so the board draws it with Claude Code's marks and colours (`frontend/src/claude-transcript.ts`).
A sub-agent runs inside its goblin's harness and has no terminal of its own to type into, so this terminal only reads.
Any other baby goblin's terminal is its own output too, never its goblin's terminal: a background shell's command after > and the newest lines it wrote to its output file, a monitor's command and its events, a job's command and each of its processes with its memory and what it runs, and a gate run's steps with each one's status and what it last did.
`GET /api/tasks/<id>/agent?node=<child id>` answers a child's newest lines, only for a child the goblin's tree holds and from the record the supervisor's own read of that tree found, never a path the browser names.
A helper goblin opens its own panel and terminal, since it is a goblin of its own.
None of it wakes the CEO: the monitor's stale and idle rules read their progress evidence through the same reader, so a goblin counts as working while any child works, and the board and the wakes never disagree about it.
A child without its own reported native transport explains that limitation without borrowing its owning task's terminal or model.
Bounded accepted-message receipts produce a brief travelling connector pulse and receiving-card glow where exact caller native identity proves the reported parent.
Native-creation receipts instead produce a quiet birth highlight on that proven parent branch plus the child-card glow and entrance; creation is never rendered as a message receipt.
Every pulse, card glow and birth highlight is its own layer over the connector or card, which plays out over the effect's whole life and fades before it is removed: a pulse's dash keeps travelling until the fade ends, so none freezes in place or vanishes at full strength, and the connector underneath never changes.
Each effect runs on the clock of the report it shows, so one drawn late, on a branch expanded mid-pulse, joins its animation where it is and still ends on time.
A newer report on the same connector plays its own pulse while the earlier one finishes and fades.
A pulse's lit line fades by opacity and its dashes, 14 px long and 244 px apart, travel by transform along points of its connector, so the canvas under a pulse is never redrawn while it plays.
A goblin at work glows, still, in the board's green, and a working baby goblin glows softer.
A baby goblin its goblin starts grows in on its branch with a pulse down the branch, and one that reports, by new activity, a new last line or a new state, sends a pulse up its branch.
One that finishes or goes sends its last pulse up, fades where it hung and then leaves, while the others slide into their new places and the canvas eases to its new fit.
Every motion on the canvas is a transform or a fade, played once for the snapshot that showed it: nothing of it runs between snapshots, while the board is hidden or while its stream is down, and with reduced motion a baby goblin simply appears or goes.
On 12 October 2026 the stand-in canvas `frontend/tests/fixtures/canvas-alive.html?demo`, with an event every 1.4 seconds, at 1600 by 1000 in headless Edge, took 49 ms of main thread and 258 ms of all Edge's processor time each second before this motion, and 38 ms and 230 ms with it, since the CEO's pulse moved by transform instead of `stroke-dashoffset`.
While nothing changes it took no main thread time either way.
The caller convention is `GOBLINS_SESSION_ID` plus `GOBLINS_SESSION_HARNESS`, with native `CODEX_THREAD_ID`, `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` and `PI_SESSION_ID` fallbacks; same-harness or directory/time resemblance never establishes a sender.
When sender identity is unavailable, only target evidence is shown.
Each effect expires independently; initial load, reconnect, hidden-tab return, instance replacement and replayed receipts never fabricate new activity.
Reduced motion uses a short static outline instead of movement.

Changes is closed when a panel opens and reads nothing until it is opened: then it reads the change set's file list and shows its summary first, with Files on GitHub linking a GitHub pull request's Files view, and each file is a closed disclosure whose diff is read only when it is opened; no diff is read in the background or on a timer.
Changes presents stacked file disclosures with lazy syntax-highlighted unified, split and code previews.
A file over 256 KiB shows its changed lines in unified and split views, with a note that the code preview is off; every Git read behind a preview is capped at 1 MiB.
Select a visible old/new line or contiguous range, including unchanged context, by its line number, which shows a comment icon on hover and focus.
Dragging across diff lines opens the same comment box for the lines it covers, and a double-click or triple-click still just selects text to copy.
A comment box floats beside the selection without moving the diff; Enter sends it, Shift+Enter starts a new line and Escape cancels.
Sending queues a durable review record containing the exact file, side, range, revision, HEAD and diff fingerprint, and the box shrinks to a chip whose check marks show delivery.
The server validates those coordinates and derives bounded selected code; it never sends a review comment to the worker.
Admission first proves the registered primary CEO live with the same process, pane, agent and terminal checks as delivery, then pins its fingerprint; a stale registration is refused and nothing is queued.
An identical retry answers from its durable record without a new probe and keeps its pinned recipient even if the primary changes.
An empty untracked file has no selectable line, while a newline-only file has one empty line.
New annotations use only the verified CEO normal native message channel, and success requires transport acceptance.
They do not append a second actionable wake record; existing historical wake records remain untouched and old unpinned reviews are not adopted or replayed.
Review remains available during gate custody, while native worker terminal input retains the custody checks.
Drafts bind the task session identity at selection and survive view, file and recipient changes without silently moving to a restarted task.
An unchanged submitted payload keeps its request ID after an ambiguous HTTP failure; an SSE outcome is displayed instead of dispatching it again.
An interrupted external delivery becomes uncertain and is not replayed automatically.

A goblin panel shows a task in a native terminal from its host, as described after this Herdr view, and the CEO and a task in Herdr through Herdr.
A panel with no terminal to show says why without opening any view: No CEO is running while no CEO runs (the snapshot's `ceo_runs`), the task's status, such as Queued or Starting, for a task with no terminal yet, and This child has no separate terminal, with **Open owning task**, for a child session.
The installed Herdr build `0.9.0-preview.2026-09-08-62431dbd033b` exposes `terminal session observe` and `terminal session control` over NDJSON.
The browser renders its real ANSI screen frames using xterm, loaded the first time a panel or a command's card shows a terminal.
A goblin panel's view of a Herdr pane is a live view of the pane, and it never resumes or answers an agent.
An open view keeps its pane live and sized to the panel at all times (the Overlord: "it should always be live"): once the view is shown it takes the pane's controller (`terminal session control --takeover`) and sizes the pane to the panel at the chosen text size, 20 px unless Ctrl+Plus, Ctrl+Minus or Ctrl+0 chose another, which native terminals share, and keeps it whether or not the board's window has the focus and while another view is shown; a Herdr window shows the pane at the board's size.
The panel keeps an even inset around the screen, and a panel that changes size asks for the grid that fills it at once, then at most every 40 ms while it keeps changing and once more when it holds still.
Dragging the divider between the board and the panel is the exception: every resize makes the pane's program redraw its whole screen, so while the divider moves the screen keeps its grid, shown scaled into the panel, and the view asks for the panel's grid once, when the drag ends; the board lays itself out at most once an animation frame during the drag.
`tests/acceptance/terminal_drag.mjs` drags the divider 400 px out and back beside a live terminal and fails when the view resized the pane before the drag ended; on 27 September 2026 the view on main asked for 53 to 64 resizes during one drag, and now asks for none during it and at most one on release.
Only another client taking the pane over, such as Open in terminal, makes a view show the pane at the size Herdr lays it out at, until the Overlord comes back to the board or types in it.
A view that sized the pane, however it ends, closing the board included, has the supervisor return the pane to that size, as a Herdr window does when a controller leaves; with no Herdr window open, nothing else would.
A Herdr window that shows the pane takes its size back once the controller leaves.
Taking control sends the program nothing but its size: attaching, resizing, taking over and detaching sent a program that logs every input byte nothing at all, and a test proves the view sends the pane only its size until the Overlord types.
A switch opens beside the view and takes over on its first whole screen, after any input on its way, so the screen never blanks and no key is lost; a size refused, such as a goblin whose review gate owns it, leaves the view showing the pane at its own size, and a review gate that takes a goblin over while the board sizes its pane ends that sizing view on its next check, which does the same.
Shown at its own size, the pane's frames arrive as Herdr lays it out; the view sizes its font to show the whole pane in the panel, bound by whichever of the panel's width or height runs out first and measured from the cells xterm drew, with no floor, centers it and anchors it to the panel's bottom so the input line stays there; the panel never scrolls, nothing is cropped and no scroll bar is drawn.
Rows are drawn at the font's own height, xterm's default line height, and a screen whose rows round up past the panel steps down half a pixel at a time until it fits whole; a 120 by 40 pane is 14 px maximized in a 1600 by 1000 window and 22 px in a 2560 by 1440 one.
A view is refused when Herdr reports no size for the pane; when Herdr lays the pane out at a new size the view ends, and the panel reconnects on its own to show it whole.
Typing needs no separate step: the view's lease takes keys, escape sequences and one bracketed paste at a time, in order, each typed into that exact pane over the Herdr session's socket.
Herdr's socket answers one request on a connection and then closes it, so each input is one pipe round trip of about a millisecond, and no key starts a process; the supervisor reads where each session's socket is from `herdr status` once and keeps it, reading it again only after the socket cannot be reached, and a view is refused when the socket cannot be found.
The supervisor also reads the session's snapshot and a pane's process info, which prove a view's pane, on that socket in milliseconds rather than through a herdr process of about a second each, and reads through the herdr command when the socket cannot be reached or its answer does not decode.
The view proves its pane, terminal, process and gate custody in full when it opens and on every five-second tick; an input starts no process to prove it again, and only rereads the task's record (or the CEO's registration) and checks that the verified process is alive, so a changed generation, pane or registration or an exited process is refused on the next key and anything else on the next tick.
A tick's Herdr commands run beside the screen, never in its way, so a frame, and with it the echo of a key, is never held behind a tick.
A paste reaches the supervisor in pieces of at most 64 KiB, which its lease joins into one paste before anything is typed, so no key or other command lands inside it.
The joined paste, with every escape character inside it removed so pasted text cannot end the paste early, is typed in one request: `pane.send_input` on an observing view, which applies the program's own paste mode, or the control stream on a sizing view.
A paste is limited to Herdr's 1 MiB request, less 1 KiB for the request's envelope, measured after JSON encoding; the view refuses a larger paste before sending any of it, the lease ends the view without typing a paste that grows past the limit or is interrupted by any other command, and an input Herdr refuses ends the view with an unknown outcome.
A refused input, or one whose outcome is unknown, ends the view with the reason in plain words; nothing is resent, and reconnecting starts from a fresh full screen.
The view never scrolls a pane that keeps scrollback: Herdr sends a view only the pane's live screen, never its history, and `pane.scroll` would move the view of every Herdr window on that pane without reaching the board.
So the live screen always follows the pane's bottom, and scrolling up with the mouse wheel, anywhere over the panel and not only over a screen that fills part of it, or Shift+PageUp reads the pane's last 3,000 lines through `POST /api/terminal/history` (Herdr's `pane.read` of its recent output, with colors, on the same socket) and shows them in a terminal of their own over the live screen, at its size and font.
The history scrolls by itself with the wheel and Shift+PageUp and Shift+PageDown; scrolling down at its bottom, Escape, or typing returns to the live screen, and what is typed reaches the pane.
On a touch screen one finger swiped over the panel does what the wheel does, up the screen opening the history and moving through it, and the page under the panel never moves; xterm reads the wheel and nothing of a finger, so until 22 October 2026 a swipe on a phone moved the page instead (the Overlord, 2026-10-22, "scroll in terminal on mobile remote doesnt work, it scrolls the screen"; `frontend/tests/terminal-swipe.spec.ts`).
A history read types nothing, so a gate that owns the pane stops typing but not reading history; it reads only the pane the view was verified on, and at most 5,000 lines.
A Claude Code pane whose history holds no more lines than its screen, as Claude Code's fullscreen interface draws in the terminal's alternate screen and keeps no scrollback, scrolls itself instead: every turn of the wheel sends it Herdr's own wheel scroll (`terminal.scroll` with source `wheel`, at most 50 lines, never any text), which the supervisor refuses for any pane whose agent is not `claude`.
Herdr hands the program one wheel notch for each such scroll, whatever number of lines it names (measured on a fixture: scrolls of 3, 4, 12 and 50 lines each arrived as one notch), so turns are never joined into one scroll.
The view judges whether a pane scrolls itself as soon as each connection draws its first whole screen, and again whenever the screen's rows change, from a read of two screens and one line, which tells a pane with no scrollback from one with some exactly as a read of its whole history does.
A judgement holds only for the rows it was read at, since a pane asked for a new grid while being read may answer at that grid.
The wheel never waits for that read, except for turns in the moment before the judgement at the screen's rows lands, which wait and are then scrolled rather than dropped.
The view trusts the judgement for 30 seconds and while its connection lasts; after that, the next turn scrolls at once and the history is read again beside it, and a pane that has since built scrollback returns to the read-only history.
Scrolls go out one at a time, and when the wheel turns faster than they can be sent, at most two wait behind the one on its way and the rest are dropped, so the pane stops soon after the wheel does; a turn the other way replaces the waiting ones.
Scrolling such a pane counts as working in the board, like typing: a view that does not hold the pane takes it first and scrolls it by that turn once it holds it; a take that is refused, such as a goblin whose review gate owns it, is not asked for again until the Overlord comes back to the board or types, and the wheel says why instead: "A review gate owns this goblin's pane now; scroll it in Herdr."
Herdr's frames carry no mouse modes, so a click never reaches the program and Claude Code's "Jump to bottom (ctrl+End)" note cannot be pressed; instead, a plain click (under 4 px of movement, not a drag or a selection) on a pane the board has scrolled up and could scroll now sends Ctrl+End, which jumps it back to its bottom, and Ctrl+End typed in the view does the same.
The jump waits out the double-click interval, 300 ms after the release, and a further press in that time cancels it, so a double-click or triple-click selects the word or line it aimed at; a click on a pane the view does not hold, such as one a review gate owns, does nothing.
A NUL key such as Ctrl+Space is typed like any other key.
Shift+Escape moves keyboard focus out of the terminal to the panel's first control; ordinary Escape stays with the pane.
Releasing a drag selection copies it to the clipboard, the way Herdr does, and Ctrl+Shift+C, or Ctrl+C while text is selected, copies the current selection.
Closing, switching, disconnecting or restarting invalidates the lease; reconnection starts with a full screen frame, never replayed input.
At most eight views are open, frame gaps disconnect, and oversized UTF-8 paste is rejected before sending.
Adjacent printable keystrokes coalesce into bounded ordered inputs; control keys and paste wrappers stay inputs of their own.
Until the first frame is drawn a plain line under the terminal's box says the terminal is connecting, never a cover over the box he types into (the Overlord, 2026-10-27: "the connecting loader overlays and looks improper on box"), and a view that has stopped says why in a pill with Reconnect beside it.
The terminal carries no options menu or help text; xterm's default input mode accepts InsertText and IME Unicode, and paste works.
Text written into the terminal's hidden text box without a key, as a phone's dictation, an emoji picker or a keyboard's suggestion writes it, is sent as the change it made to the box: what it added is typed, and what it replaced is erased with Backspace back to where the change began and typed again, never erasing text before a key typed after it.
A phone's dictation writes each partial result over the one before it, and until 21 October 2026 xterm sent each as new keys, so one dictated sentence reached the program about forty times, each a little longer (`frontend/tests/dictated-input.spec.ts`).
Keys, pastes and an input method's composition are sent as xterm sends them.
On a touch screen that text box lies, unseen, over the terminal's whole screen, so a long press offers the phone's own Paste and a tap brings up its keyboard; elsewhere xterm keeps it off the screen, and until 21 October 2026 it did on a phone too, so nothing under a finger could be typed into and a long press offered no Paste (`frontend/tests/phone-paste.spec.ts`).
There the box is drawn at 2% opacity with nothing in it to see, since an iPhone offers no Paste in a box it reads as transparent, under 1% opacity, which is how xterm leaves it; until 22 October 2026 a long press on an iPhone offered none (the Overlord, 2026-10-22, "i cant paste").
A touch screen also has Paste, Add a picture and Copy text beside the terminal's microphone, in the CEO's terminal and every goblin's, native or Herdr (`frontend/tests/phone-terminal-tools.spec.ts`).
Paste reads the phone's clipboard, which the phone asks to allow, and types its text as a desktop paste does or adds its picture; Add a picture opens the phone's photo library or camera; Copy text lays the terminal's history and screen out as plain text, which a long press selects and the phone's own menu copies, since xterm selects only by a dragged mouse.
There is no second model session or generated reply.
The native interface exposes rendered screen updates rather than original historical PTY bytes, and omits Kitty keyboard negotiation, graphics and host mouse notifications.

A task whose record names the `native` backend runs in a `goblins host` of its own, and `GET /api/terminal/native?task=ID&generation=GEN&token=TOKEN` relays one view of it over a WebSocket; a goblin panel opens it for every such task, and the snapshot names each task's `backend` so the board knows which view to open.
A CEO registered in a native terminal is relayed the same way with `?ceo=TERMINAL&token=TOKEN`, and the snapshot's `ceo_terminal` names that terminal (native terminal `ceo` while a CEO is starting there unregistered), empty while the CEO runs in Herdr, so the CEO's entry in the panel shows the native terminal instead of a Herdr view that cannot show it.
The view is refused unless the registration names a live CEO in exactly that terminal, or, with no CEO registered, the terminal is native terminal `ceo` and its host answers, and it closes once that stops holding, so typing never reaches a terminal the CEO has left.
The upgrade needs the board's own origin and the board's token in the query, since a browser cannot set a WebSocket header, and anything else is refused with 403.
Every later refusal closes the socket with its reason, which a browser can read: a replaced generation, a task that runs in Herdr, no running host, a host that did not answer, or 32 native views already open, a limit of their own apart from the eight Herdr streams.
A view of a goblin that the supervisor starts or resumes waits for its host instead, since the board shows the new session a second or more before its host runs, and is refused for no running host only once the start or resume has ended without one.
The view is bound to its terminal once, by the host's pipe, whose server process must be the host the record names, so a key costs no check and starts no process.
The first message is the text `{"type":"history","bytes":N}`, the number of output bytes that follow as the host's history, even when it is empty.
Output arrives as binary messages, the history first; typing goes back as binary messages, and a resize as the text message `{"type":"resize","cols":C,"rows":R}`.
The host repaints the whole screen from its own copy on every resize, even to the size it already has, as the inbox conhost repaints and OpenConsole does not, so a [visible native view](#the-message-box) can show the whole screen however much of the history the host still keeps.
Its copy resizes as xterm.js resizes its own: a line wider than the new width is wrapped anew and a line a wrap split is joined again, all but the line the cursor is on, which xterm.js too leaves to the program.
Lines a view brings back from its scrollback as the terminal grows are drawn over by the repaint, as they were by the inbox conhost's.
The repaint writes a wrapped line on past the row's end, so a view marks it wrapped and reflows it too, and it places the cell after a wide character by its column, since the board's xterm.js draws an emoji one cell wide.
A resize's repaint sets none of the modes the program set, which every view that followed the output already has, since xterm.js reports the focus each time it is asked for focus reports.
The host starts a replay at the next line or escape sequence past its 4 MiB limit, never inside one.
A replay that no longer starts at the terminal's start ends with a repaint that sets the program's modes too, so a view that sends no size, such as one that only watches, shows the whole screen and takes the keys the program expects.
Every view of the terminal, the one that sent the resize too, is told each size the terminal took as `{"type":"size","cols":C,"rows":R}` at its place in the output: after what the terminal wrote before the resize and before what it wrote after, whichever viewer resized it, `goblins attach` in an Open window included, and the history carries the sizes it was written at the same way.
A view changes its grid only there, once xterm has parsed the output before it, never ahead of the terminal, so output is always drawn on the grid it was written for rather than wrapping and landing at the wrong columns.
A view draws another viewer's size until it is typed into, which sizes the terminal to it, and `goblins attach` takes the terminal back the same way with its next key.
Nothing else in the view takes the size back: not the view becoming whole or coming into sight, its font loading or its panel resizing.
Only a size the terminal takes after it took the view's own counts as another viewer's; until then the terminal is still on its way to the view's size.
A program's terminal queries, such as a cursor-position or device-attributes request, never reach a view: the host answers them or leaves them unanswered, so no view, open or replaying, types an answer into the program.
A size under 20 columns or 5 rows, measured while a panel was hidden, or over 1000 columns or 500 rows is ignored and announced to nobody.
A host tells a viewer sizes only when its handshake asks for them; for a host started before hosts told sizes, the relay tells every view the size each of its own resizes gave the terminal, at the end of the output it had read by then.
A view acknowledges the output it has parsed with `{"type":"ack","bytes":N}`, N counting every output byte so far: the relay sends a view at most 1 MiB beyond what it acknowledged and keeps reading the host, and a view that falls 8 MiB behind is closed with code 1013 and "The view fell behind the terminal's output.", so the host never waits on a slow window and a view that stays open never loses a byte.
A view is told when key presses have sat unread in the terminal's input, as `{"type":"unread","unread":true}`, and with `false` once the program has read them.
For a harness whose screens the supervisor knows, the CEO's being the one it registered as by the time of the read, the relay reads the terminal's screen as a view opens, which no output puts off, and once its output has paused for 300 ms, which a turn's spinner never lets it, and tells the view as `{"type":"screen","asking":true,"open":true}` each time what it read changes: asking while the harness waits on an answer at a screen of its own, which only its terminal takes, and open while something of its own is drawn in its input line's place, a picker, a panel or that question.
For Claude Code that is a dialog the supervisor knows while no turn, composer or footer shows, or a list of numbered choices with one focused, as its permission prompt draws, while none of them shows under the list (`Screens.IsAsking` in `internal/harness/screens.go`), and something is open while it asks, or while no row of its composer's footer shows and no turn runs (`Screens.IsOpen`).
What the conversation shows above decides nothing: a dialog's words a diff quotes have the composer waiting under them, and a mode a reply names lies above a list that asks.
It also says `"draft":true` while its prompt line holds words never sent, typed in the terminal itself or put back when the harness was stopped, read as a delivery reads them (`spawn.WithoutInlineOffer` and `Screens.ComposerHoldsText`), so the board's box holds a message rather than typing it onto them, and the view's own submit refuses to.
While the view was last told `true`, the screen is read 300 ms after the first output that follows, pause or not, so the turn an answer starts clears the card at once.
A busy program takes a key only once it is free, as Claude Code does while it starts a tool and a shell does while a command runs, and until then nothing on its screen shows the key was pressed.
The board's terminal, while the board shows no message box for the session, says so once a key has waited a second, and for at least a second, with the note Program busy. Typed keys are waiting. in its top left corner, so a key is not pressed a second time: on a screen where a key switches something on and off, as Enter does on a row of Claude Code's permissions screen, the second press undoes the first, which is how an Enter the Overlord pressed there on 9 October 2026 approved nothing.
A view opened while keys wait is told so first and shows the note once it is whole.
A host tells a viewer of it only when the viewer's handshake asks, so `goblins attach` and a relay from before hosts told it are told nothing, and a host from before tells nothing.
`TestOnlyAViewerThatAskedIsToldOfAKeyABusyProgramLeavesUnread`, `TestANativeViewIsToldOfAKeyABusyProgramLeavesUnreadUntilItIsRead` and `frontend/tests/terminal-keys.spec.ts` cover it.
Gate custody and the task's generation are checked when the view opens and on every five-second tick.
While custody holds typing, as a review gate that owns the goblin's work does, the view stays open and its screen live: the relay tells it `{"type":"held","reason":"..."}` when it opens and whenever that changes, and an empty reason once typing is free, and the view says it on a plain line under its screen as Typing is paused. A session's message box, once shown, replaces that line with its queue note, in its banner over the box. The screen stays live.
A key or a paste sent under custody is dropped and never typed, and a resize is ignored, so the terminal keeps its size until a resize arrives after custody ends.
A new no-mistakes task's worktree starts on a detached HEAD, before its goblin makes a branch, so it has no branch for custody to be read on.
Typing into it and board messages for it go through once the gate proves no run of its own works in that worktree and HEAD has not moved since git made it, its HEAD history being that one record (#1987, `TestTypingIntoANewTasksDetachedWorktreeReachesItsTerminalOnlyOnceProvedFresh`).
A worktree a gate run works in reads as the gate's hold; one whose gate records cannot be read, or whose HEAD moved before or during the check, stays held as a detached HEAD that names its commit and why.
Until 22 October 2026 the first input under custody closed the view, and xterm sends input by itself, a focus report once a program asks for them or a reply to a query the replayed history holds, so a goblin a gate owned opened as a Reconnect button with no screen and no words (the Overlord, 2026-10-22, "every time I open up some of my goblins in the task panel it always says reconnect even though it's connected"; #1304; `TestANativeViewUnderAGateStaysOpenAndTypesNothing`, `frontend/tests/terminal-held.spec.ts`).
The terminal's end closes the view with its exit code in the reason.

Native hosts explicitly disable Windows execution-speed throttling for themselves, their new system console server, and the suspended terminal process before it runs.
A hidden console has no foreground window to earn interactive scheduling automatically; on a loaded hybrid processor, automatic scheduling can leave it competing on saturated efficiency cores while performance cores are parked.
The policy changes neither process priority, processor affinity nor the machine's power plan, and preserves other process power controls.
The console server is selected from direct children created within a serialized `CreatePseudoConsole` call, with its creation time and system image checked through the open handle used to set the policy; existing consoles are left alone.
The terminal's existing job applies interactive scheduling to automatically managed descendants, including a harness launched through cmd or node, while preserving a descendant's explicit EcoQoS choice.
This intentionally gives the terminal's ordinary process tree an interactive default, including background tools that have not chosen their own power policy; it can use more processor power than Windows' automatic background policy.
Processes that break away from the job are excluded, and every descendant is checked against the exact job through its open handle before any change.
Job notifications handle new processes, with job-local reconciliation at most once per second when input arrives because Windows does not guarantee notification delivery.
Descendant scheduling errors are logged and cannot discard terminal input; required host, console-server and initial-process policy failures refuse startup.
The latency regression uses one native console event per read, as libuv does, and checks key p95 below 50 ms, the second slowest of 40 keys within 250 ms, no key past a second, and an ordered 2,000-character burst within two seconds during idle and continuous output.

The fleet's own controls run one priority class above normal, and never higher.
They are the supervisor, each terminal's host, and the commands that are a short conversation with one of them or with the home's own records: `status`, `stop`, `attach`, `send`, `answer`, `peek`, `fleet-view`, `drain`, `watch`, `notify`, `question`, `register`, `pause`, `resume`, `kill`, `update`, `process-plan`, `session-start`, `hook` and `native-hook`.
Each raises itself as its first act (`priority.AboveTheWork`) and gives the class back when its work ends.
A terminal's host and its input waker are started at that class too (`priority.StartAboveTheWork`), since a host's launch waits 15 seconds for the host's record and the host waits on its waker, and a process that raises itself does so only once its code runs.
Coming to exist was most of each start: on 2026-10-15, beside ten busy threads on the goblins' cores, the efficiency cores then among them, a host took 0.5 to 0.8 seconds to reach its own code and its waker as long again to say it was ready, at normal priority, and the waker started raised was ready in 0.15 to 0.35 seconds.
The rest of a host's start, its console and its program's start among it, took milliseconds either way, so those two starts at normal priority are what grew until a host missed the bound on a busier machine (issue #891).
A process that is not at normal priority is left as it is: one that runs lower was put there on purpose and hands its class to what it starts.
At the priority of the builds and tests it supervises, a control waited its turn behind them after each of its system calls.
Measured on 2026-10-10, each control at normal priority and then raised, beside sixteen busy threads on every core, and beside ten kept to the cores goblin work kept on that date, which left half of the performance cores to the Overlord's apps and held the efficiency cores in its mask:

| Control | Every core, normal | Every core, raised | Goblin cores, normal | Goblin cores, raised |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| One reading of the machine's processes, which the janitor, a stop's sweep and the board's trees make | 7.7 s, from 4.4 to 10.2 | 0.04 s | 0.017 s | 0.013 s |
| The same reading in a hidden process that asks Windows for nothing, as a pause the supervisor starts | not measured | not measured | 0.9 s, from 0.14 to 1.8 | 0.013 s |
| A host's handshake, with its client raised as well | 58 ms, from 39 to 102 | 0.2 ms | 0.7 ms | 0.2 ms |
| A typed key back from the console, through the host | 152 ms | 0.2 ms | 0.4 ms, worst 47 | 0.1 ms |
| `goblins send --key`, its own work once its process runs | 107 ms | 2 ms | 1 ms | 1 ms |
| One verified copy of a 33 MB program, of which an update's prepare step makes five | 3.5 s | 0.07 s | 0.06 s, and 3.6 s in a process that asks for nothing | 0.05 s |
| A `goblins` command or a hook coming to exist | 0.65 s | 0.61 s | 0.02 s | 0.015 s |
| `goblins peek`, its own work | 2.4 s | 2.0 s | 0.07 s, worst 0.9 | 0.013 s |
| The supervisor's progress reading of one goblin, two git commands | 0.89 s | 0.77 s | 0.17 s | 0.14 s |

Windows keeps a hidden process that asks for nothing on the efficiency cores, which goblin work could fill on that date, so a control that made no such request gained most from the raise.
Goblin work has kept off the efficiency cores since issue #2075, so those figures are not a reading of the mask it has now.
A goblin's harness and everything started in its terminal run at normal, a `goblins` command a goblin runs there included, and so does what a gate agent starts.
Windows gives the child of a raised process the normal class as long as its start names none, and no start in the fleet names one but a host's and its waker's.
`TestAHostRunsOneClassAboveItsTerminalWhichRunsAtNormal`, `TestWhatARaisedProcessStartsThroughExecxRunsAtNormal`, `TestWhatARaisedHostStartsForItsConsoleRunsAtNormalButItsWaker` and `TestARunItemARaisedSupervisorStartsRunsAtNormal` hold that for each way the fleet starts a process, `TestALaunchedHostIsStartedOneClassAboveTheWork` that a host is raised from its start, and `TestEveryProcessStartGoesThroughCommand` reports a priority class named anywhere but `internal/priority`, and a start above the work anywhere but a host's launch and its waker's start.
`TestEveryControlHasAnExecutablePriorityCheck` makes every control one a test runs and sees raised, and `TestNonControlsStayAtNormalPriority` holds each command listed there with why it stays at normal; a command in neither list is not a control and runs at normal.
The last three rows are what the raise cannot reach where goblin work has every core, as on a machine with a single performance core.
A command or a hook is started at its caller's priority and raises itself only once it runs.
For a peek, and for the monitor's reading of a screen, the terminal's input waker asks the console for each row, and the console answers at normal priority.
Each progress reading starts git twice in a goblin's worktree, and a process the fleet starts runs at normal.
One slow key in a run is a hosted runner's scheduling noise (504 ms seen with the test's job to itself); two slow keys fail, and a failure names the slow keys by number.
Apart from that policy, a goblin's terminal keeps to performance cores, all of a machine's cores where they are of one kind, and off those the fleet's work leaves to the Overlord's own apps: half of the machine's performance cores, rounded up to whole cores, the last of them as Windows numbers them.
`goblins spawn` starts a goblin's host with `--leave-cores-for-apps`, and the host keeps the terminal's process to the threads of the other performance cores before it runs.
Windows starts every process on the threads of the one that started it, so everything the goblin starts keeps to them, whether it stays in the terminal's job or leaves it, as what Git Bash runs does.
A processor limit on the job would not hold a process that leaves the job, so the host sets none.
On 2026-10-10, on a PC of six performance and four efficiency cores, with the efficiency cores in the mask, a test Chrome showed its window after 0.53 s with nothing added, 4.11 s under goblin work on every core, 2.33 s with that work kept off two performance cores and 0.98 s with it kept off three, each a median, of two starts for three cores.
Kept off three, the work got through 59% as much while it filled every core it kept, and nothing less when it did not.
The test Chrome was never the window in front, so a start the Overlord clicks himself may be quicker than these at any share.
The efficiency cores stay with the system's background work, because Windows keeps a hidden process that asks for nothing on them and does not move that work to a performance core when they fill, and what a terminal starts asks for nothing.
On 2026-11-05, on the same kind of PC, eight hidden CPU-bound processes of 6 seconds' work each were started together: with the efficiency cores in the mask 1 or 2 finished within the wait, the efficiency cores 100 percent busy and the performance threads 13 to 70, and without them all 8 did, the performance threads 50 to 100 percent busy.
There is no such limit on a machine with one performance core, on a machine with more than one processor group, or where the cores cannot be read, which the host's log says, and a terminal is never given a thread its host may not itself run on.
The CEO's terminal, a run card's terminal and a staged harness's proof start keep every core.
Installing a build or restarting `serve` leaves existing hosts running their original code; the persistent policy takes effect in newly launched hosts, so resume each existing session only when its active work permits a host restart.

Every native terminal runs on Microsoft's own console host, `conpty.dll` and `OpenConsole.exe` from its ConPTY package (1.25.260930003, MIT, listed in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES`), which goblins.exe embeds.
Windows' inbox conhost (10.0.26100, as Windows 11 and GitHub's runners ship it) makes a program's waiting console read outside its console lock, so a key typed at that moment either sits unread until more input arrives or crashes conhost, a null read in `ConsoleWaitBlock::~ConsoleWaitBlock` that ends the terminal.
microsoft/terminal#18816 fixed both in a conhost Windows does not ship yet.
On GitHub's runners the crash ended 28 of 4,200 runs of `TestTypedKeysReachAProgramThatPrintsWhileItReads` on the inbox conhost and none of 3,500 on OpenConsole.
The host writes the two files to `goblins\conpty\<version>` in this Windows user's own LocalAppData, as the user's profile names it whatever `LOCALAPPDATA` and `USERPROFILE` say, so a test that points them at a folder of its own never loads the files from a folder it deletes as it ends.
That folder's access list lets only this Windows user in.
The host keeps each file open so nothing can write, rename or delete it, and loads them only once each one's SHA-256 matches the copy goblins.exe embeds.
When they cannot be written or loaded, the terminal runs on the inbox conhost and the CEO is woken once, a `check` wake keyed `conpty` starting `console_host:` with the reason.
OpenConsole asks its terminal for its device attributes as it starts and takes the first answer typed after that as the answer, and every later one reaches the program as typed input.
The console answers that query itself, as the board's xterm.js would (`ESC[?1;2c`), and leaves it out of the terminal's output, so no viewer, nor one replaying the terminal from its start, types an answer into the program.

The host keeps the terminal's screen (`internal/vtscreen`): the main and alternate screens, the cursor, the style, the modes and the scrolling region, read from the output by go-vte, a port of the parser Alacritty uses.
No small Go terminal library that keeps a screen fits: midterm and vt10x draw every character one cell wide, so the cursor they report is wrong on any line with a wide character, and charmbracelet/x/vt is published only as pseudo-versions and brings about ten modules.
After every resize OpenConsole asks its terminal where the cursor is (`ESC[6n`) the next time a program needs it, and the program waits up to 500 ms for the answer, which no view can give, since every view would answer it and so would every replay.
The host answers at once from its screen, which takes each size before the pseudo console does, so a console call after a resize returns in under a millisecond, where it took 513 ms unanswered (`TestAResizeNeverStallsTheProgram`), and spawn's redraw nudge reaches the program again.
The host answers what every terminal answers: the cursor position (`ESC[6n` and `ESC[?6n`), the device attributes (`ESC[c`, as xterm.js does) and the status (`ESC[5n`).
It leaves every other query unanswered, as the inbox conhost does: the secondary and tertiary device attributes and the terminal's version, mode reports, window reports, colour and clipboard queries (OSC 4, 5, 10 to 19 and 52 with `?`), the kitty keyboard query (`ESC[?u`), the colour scheme query (`ESC[?996n`), DECRQSS and XTGETTCAP.
No query reaches a view, so a program asks one terminal, the host, whether no view, one or several are open: Claude Code and pi ask for the kitty keyboard protocol with the device attributes after it, and learn at once that it is not there (`TestTheHostAnswersTheProgramsQueriesAndKeepsThemFromViewers`).
Output read in pieces can end inside an escape sequence or a character, and the host holds such a start back until its end comes, up to 1 MiB, so a query is kept from views however it was read and a repaint never lands inside a sequence.
OpenConsole also asks for focus reports and Windows key events as it starts (`ESC[?1004h ESC[?9001h`): it takes a view's focus reports as focus events, never as typed input (`TestAViewersFocusReportsNeverReachTheProgramAsTypedInput`), and it holds an Escape ending as the inbox conhost does, which the hold below covers.

Every native terminal also runs an input waker: the host's own program again, attached to the terminal's pseudo console and told each time the host writes input or resizes the terminal.
It is for the inbox conhost, which can queue input without waking a program that waits for it in a blocking read, `ReadConsoleInputW` or `ReadConsoleW`, while another of its threads prints, so the input sits unread until more arrives.
On OpenConsole the waker finds nothing unread.
The waker counts the console's unread input 25 ms after the last write and again at doubling intervals up to 1.6 s, and while any is unread it writes a menu event, which wakes the reader and which every reader skips, so input a program was left waiting for arrives within about 25 ms and a program that reads promptly never sees one.
It outlives Ctrl-C and Ctrl-Break, ends with its console, and notes its wakes in the host's log at most once a minute.
No wake helps a program that is not waiting to read, so the waker also says when typed keys wait on a busy program: after each write it looks at the console's input every 50 ms until none is left unread, every 250 ms once it has sat for 3 s, says so once a key press has sat unread for a second, and says when all of them were read.
It follows each record from one look to the next, with when it first saw it, so the wait it reads is the oldest unread key's own: Claude Code reads typed keys in bursts while it streams, and while keys keep coming some key just typed is unread at most looks though none waits long, which timed from the first key of the run read as one long wait and lit the note for keys read within 300 ms.
Once said, it stays said at least a second however soon the keys are read, and a key typed after it went has waited no time, so the note changes no sooner than a second after it last changed: a wait just past the line once showed it for 50 ms, and he saw it flash (the Overlord, 14 October 2026).
Of keys that are all the same, a key read and another typed between two looks reads as none read, so such a wait is read long, never short.
A window's focus, a resize and the waker's own wakes are input too, and none of them was typed, so none of them counts (`TestKeysABusyProgramLeavesUnreadAreSaidUnreadAndThenRead`, `TestKeysAProgramReadsAtOnceAreNeverSaidUnread`, `TestInputThatIsNoKeyPressIsNeverSaidUnread`, `TestKeysABurstyProgramReadsWithinItsSpellsAreNeverSaidUnread`, `TestKeysThatWaitAreSaidUnreadSteadily`, `TestTheWaitTheWakerReadsIsTheOldestUnreadKeysOwn`).
`TestTypedKeysReachAProgramThatPrintsWhileItReads` types 3,000 keys into a program that echoes each while it prints and fails on any key left unread for a minute, and on a terminal that ends first it says how the program and the console server exited, with the program's own reason from its log, and `TestInputLeftUnreadIsFollowedByAWakeEveryReaderSkips` checks the wake itself.
Once a terminal has read one key event written as Windows sends it, as `goblins attach` writes every key, the console server, OpenConsole and the inbox conhost alike, holds an input that ends in Escape, or in Escape and a character that starts a longer sequence (`[`, `O`, `P`, `]`, `X`, `^` or `_`), as a sequence still arriving: an Escape typed alone reached the program only with the next key, as Alt and that key, and Alt+P or Alt+] swallowed every key after it.
The host holds such an ending back for up to 100 ms: the next input takes it on when it comes that soon, as the rest of a key event `goblins attach` split across two writes does, and otherwise it is written as the Windows key events it stands for, Escape or Alt and the character, which conhost delivers at once; `TestAnEscapeTypedAfterAWindowsKeyEventReachesTheProgramAtOnce` and `TestAKeyEventSplitAcrossTwoWritesReachesTheProgramWhole` cover it.

The board draws a native terminal with xterm at the panel's size: the view measures the cell xterm drew and fits the columns and rows the panel holds with an even inset of at least 10 px, and sends them as the resize, so the program and the view agree on the size.
The spare width is split between left and right, the bottom keeps the same space, so the last row, the input line, sits that far from the panel's bottom, and the spare height, under a row, goes above the grid; no space is kept for a scroll bar, since none is drawn.
xterm draws with its WebGL renderer, and with its DOM renderer in WebKit, the engine of every browser on an iPhone and of Safari, where that renderer drew nothing (in Playwright's WebKit; the Overlord's iPhone showed black terminals), and where WebGL is unavailable or its context is lost.
The font starts at 20 px, and at 12 px on a small screen (40rem or less), where a phone 390 px wide holds about 50 columns of a harness's screen; Ctrl+Plus and Ctrl+Minus step it between 12 and 28 px and Ctrl+0 restores the size it starts at, saved in the browser, and a new font size resizes the pseudo console, so the terminal gains or loses columns instead of shrinking its text.
Holding the right mouse button and turning the wheel steps it the same way, up to grow and down to shrink, a step a notch, with a smooth wheel's small turns adding up to one.
The wheel then neither scrolls the terminal nor reaches its program, and the menu that would open when the button comes up does not, so its Paste is never offered, while a right click that never turned the wheel does what it always did.
The wheel turned with Ctrl and Shift held steps it the same way, and then neither scrolls the terminal, reaches its program nor zooms the page; Shift turns a wheel sideways in Chromium on Windows, so that sideways turn counts too.
The size is one for every terminal on the board: a size chosen in any one, by any of the three ways, applies at once to every open terminal, shown or not, and to every one opened later, each refitting its grid to its panel once in sight, and it is kept in the browser's storage for the board's address, which an update keeps, so a reload or an update keeps it and each device, such as a phone, keeps its own.
The terminal keeps 5,000 lines of scrollback and the wheel scrolls it; the panel around it never scrolls.
On a touch screen one finger swiped over the terminal scrolls it as the wheel does, by the lines it moved over, and the page never moves: the history scrolls, or a program that takes the wheel, by the mouse or on the alternate screen, gets a turn of the wheel a line.
While the wheel has the history up, the line the program waits on, the cursor's row, stays pinned over the panel's last row in its own colors, with a thin rule above it and the history scrolling over it, and a Jump to bottom button sits above it in the middle.
New output while he reads leaves the history where it is, and typing, a paste or the button returns to the live end, the button handing the terminal the keyboard.
A program on the alternate screen keeps no history in the terminal and draws its own input line, so the wheel goes to it as before and nothing is pinned.
Codex and pi draw on their own full screens, in the CEO's terminal and every goblin's: Codex in its alternate screen and pi with `--tui-mode fullscreen` where its help offers fullscreen.
Claude Code draws in the interface the operator's `tui` setting names, which is its own full screen when that setting names fullscreen.
Each harness on its own full screen keeps its input line and footer on the bottom rows while its transcript scrolls above them under the wheel, and offers its own way back: Claude Code's own, Codex's Back to bottom (Esc) and pi's Jump to latest message (End).
A drag selects in the harness, which copies the selection when the button is released, Codex because it starts with `tui.copy_on_select=always`, and Shift and a drag selects in the board's own terminal instead.
Until 17 October 2026 Codex started with `--no-alt-screen` and pi with `--tui-mode regular`, both inline, so their footers scrolled away with the history, only the cursor's row stayed pinned, and Codex drew a short or cleared conversation from the top of its screen, its input line high above blank rows.
Output is written through xterm's own write queue and never re-renders the page.
A chunk as large as one host read, 32 KiB, is part of a larger redraw, so the view opens a synchronized update (DECSET 2026) before it and ends it when the redraw's short tail arrives or the stream has been quiet for 8 ms, and xterm paints the redraw as one frame; a smaller chunk, such as an echoed key, is written as it is.
The update's markers only go where the stream sits between escape sequences and characters and outside any synchronized update the program opened itself, and xterm ends an update left open after one second.
Each connection draws into its own xterm, kept out of sight until the history, the repaint its size asked for and any open update are drawn, so the panel never shows a blank, cleared or half-drawn screen; until the first one is whole the plain line under it says the terminal is connecting, as it does in Herdr's view above.
A view that fell behind, a host that did not answer, a restarting board or a dropped connection reconnects on its own up to five times, keeping the last screen in place with a Reconnecting note until the new connection is whole, and does the same while the board's own connection is down; a terminal that ended keeps the last screen in view with how it ended in a bar across the bottom and no Reconnect, and any other close does the same with its reason and Reconnect.
The new connection takes the keyboard when the view it replaces had it, so every key pressed after a reconnect reaches the program as before it; on 13 October 2026 it did not, and the Overlord's arrows, Enter and Escape on Claude Code's permissions screen in the CEO's terminal went nowhere.
A key or paste typed while the view has no open connection, reconnecting or closed, is not sent, since a key sent once the view is back could act on a screen he no longer sees, and the terminal says Not sent. The terminal is reconnecting. or Not sent. The terminal is closed. in its top left corner until a new connection is whole (`frontend/tests/terminal-reconnect-keys.spec.ts`).
The relay closes a view as ended, a normal close (1000), when what it names no longer runs: its program exited, no terminal runs for it, its command is not running or ended, its task restarted or was replaced, or the CEO no longer runs in it.
Nothing brings such a terminal back, and a new session or terminal is one the board opens by itself, so the board offers no Reconnect for it, as the Overlord asked on 2026-10-12 of a failed command's card ("it shows the reconnect button when it shouldn't").
Every other refusal closes as refused (1008) and keeps Reconnect, with its reason beside it; until 22 October 2026 the button showed alone.
A paste goes as it is typed, in pieces of at most 64 KiB, in order.

Every key typed in a native terminal goes to its program as xterm sends it, the bytes of a plain terminal, in the CEO's terminal and a goblin's alike, whether the program is idle, busy or on a full screen of its own.
That is Enter, Escape, Tab and Shift+Tab, Space, Backspace, Insert and Delete, the arrows, Home, End, Page Up and Page Down, F1 to F12, letters, digits and punctuation, Ctrl with a letter, Alt with a letter, and the arrows, Home, End and the page keys with Shift, Alt or Ctrl.
A program that asked for application cursor keys is sent the arrows, Home and End in that form.
Claude Code and pi read those bytes as they are.
A program that reads key records, as Codex does, never sees the bytes: the pseudo console makes of each the key Windows names for it, with its character and its Alt, Ctrl and Shift.
Bytes cannot tell a few keys from another, as in any terminal that sends bytes, and such a program reads Ctrl+H as Ctrl+Backspace, Ctrl+I as Tab, Ctrl+M as Enter, Ctrl+J as Ctrl+Enter and Ctrl+Space as Ctrl+Shift+2.
Shift+Enter is the one key sent per harness, as the line break of its composer: a line feed to Claude Code, the Ctrl+J key event to Codex, and Enter to anything else.
The board keeps these keys for itself:

| Key | What the board does with it |
| --- | --- |
| Ctrl+Plus, Ctrl+Minus, Ctrl+0 | Steps or restores the font size |
| Ctrl+Shift+Space, held | Dictates |
| Shift+Escape | Leaves the terminal for the panel's first control |
| Ctrl+Alt+Up, Ctrl+Alt+Down, Ctrl+Alt+1 to 9 | Switches terminals |
| Ctrl+V, Ctrl+Shift+V, Shift+Insert | Pastes |
| Ctrl+Shift+C, Ctrl+Insert, Ctrl+C while text is selected | Copies |
| Shift+Page Up, Shift+Page Down | Scrolls the terminal's own history |

Of these, the screens of Claude Code, Codex and pi name only Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V, and both still reach them: Ctrl+C with nothing selected goes to the program, and Ctrl+V with only an image on the clipboard sends the key the harness attaches an image on.
The harness reads that image from this PC's clipboard, so a picture pasted or added on a board opened on another device, such as a phone, is first drawn as a PNG of at most 2,048 pixels a side and put on this PC's clipboard through `POST /api/terminal/image`, as a bitmap and as the PNG, and the key is pressed once it is there (the Overlord, 2026-10-22, "theres no copy and paste of screenshots or adding of attached images").
That replaces what this PC's clipboard held; a paste on this PC's own board, whose picture is already there, sends only the key, as before.
Windows Terminal keeps the same keys for the font size, copy and paste.
Ctrl+Shift with any other letter, Ctrl+/ and Ctrl with 1 or 9 send nothing, since xterm has no bytes for them.
A browser tab keeps some keys before any page sees them, Ctrl+T, Ctrl+N and Ctrl+W among them, and those never reach a terminal in a tab.
`frontend/tests/terminal-keys.spec.ts` presses every key of the table in both terminals, for Claude Code, Codex and pi, idle, busy and on a full screen, and `TestEachKeyTheBoardSendsReachesAProgramThatReadsKeyRecordsAsThatKey` reads the key record each one becomes.

Native terminal presentation follows [the message box's lifecycle](#the-message-box).
The deck draws only the terminal of the session the panel shows: a session he left keeps no connection, offers its message box no link and holds no key or microphone, so nothing typed or said in another session's pane reaches it, and a switch back connects afresh and replays its screen (2026-11-04).
A panel that shows nothing has no deck, so Close, Back on a phone and closing the CEO's panel in Orchestration each end its terminal's connection, and opening it again connects afresh and replays its screen with his draft still in the box (the CEO, decision 1016436).
The Task view of the same session is still that session's panel, so its terminal stays connected there.
The shown terminal fills the panel, with no list beside it; the Overlord picks the goblin on the board.
Ctrl+Alt+Up and Ctrl+Alt+Down step through the terminals, the CEO first and then each goblin with a terminal, and Ctrl+Alt+1 to Ctrl+Alt+9 jump to one in that order, matched by key position; the board catches them before a terminal sees them, except while a dialog such as the Command Center is open, and a switch hands the keyboard to the session's message box, or to its terminal where it has no messages or Classic draws it ([The Messages view](#the-messages-view)), a Herdr terminal included.
A key typed with AltGr, which Windows reports as Ctrl+Alt, stays the terminal's, so a layout that types a brace or bracket with AltGr and a digit keeps it.
A divider between the board and the panel sizes the panel, keeping at least 360 px for the panel and 280 px for the board, and a maximize button gives the panel the whole window; every panel opens beside the board until it is maximized, a Board panel with a terminal or messages and one with its task list alone each keeping the last choice, and the Orchestration view following the latter's so its graph stays beside the panel, and the width and both choices are saved in the browser, a width saved on a wider window is held to the same bounds, and on a narrow window the board and the panel stack and the divider is hidden, the panel filling the screen on its own once opened on a small screen (below).
The divider's handle is always drawn, not only under the pointer, so the panel's edge shows it can be dragged (the Overlord, 2026-10-29: "more drag based").
The first open of the board in a browser, which is one that records no first open (`ceo-first-open` in its local storage) and keeps neither a panel width nor a maximize choice, arranges the panel once: with a CEO running it shows the Board with the CEO selected, its panel on the view it opens on beside the board at the default width, not maximized, and the keyboard in that view: a Claude Code CEO's message box, or else its terminal.
With no CEO running in a home that has not chosen one, the first-run page comes first, and Start lands on the same view.
On a small screen (40rem or less) an open panel fills the screen, so the first open there opens no panel and shows the board alone, with the keyboard and the scroll left where they are; between that and 1410 px the two sit side by side with the board stacked, since its three columns need 961 px beside the panel.
The record is written as the board first shows, so nothing arranged later is undone, and a browser that kept a layout before the record existed counts as having had its first open.
