The first open also owes the browser the quick tour, recorded as `ceo-tour` in its local storage: `due` from that open until the tour starts, then `seen`, so it shows once whatever becomes of the layout's record; a browser that recorded its first open before the tour existed is owed none, and so is one that keeps nothing in its local storage, since it could not keep the tour to once.
A tour owed starts once a CEO runs and the board shows, so a CEO started from the first-run page gets it too, even across a reload before that CEO registers.
The tour is a modal dialog of three steps, each lighting one part of the board with the rest dimmed and the CEO's card beside the part on the first side with room for it: the CEO's panel, the board, then the Command Center's button; a part out of sight, such as the board behind a maximized panel, lights nothing, and its card sits at the window's foot.
It starts on the Board with the CEO's panel on the view it opens on, Next or Enter moves on, and X or Escape ends it at once; at its end the keyboard goes to that view, the CEO's message box or its terminal, except on a window too narrow for two columns, where it returns to where it was.
On a small screen the tour keeps the board and the CEO's panel stacked, one under the other, so it can light each, and the CEO's panel fills the screen once it ends.
The question mark in the top bar replays it from any view on a screen wider than 40rem, and since its first step says how to dictate, a tour owed spends the terminal's voice hint.
The header shows no pill while the board is live, in a browser, on a phone or in the desktop window, and a warning right before the Command Center's button names a connection problem while it is not (the Overlord, 2026-10-28: "dont need live status pill in top right corner of desktop", and his mobile corrections of 2026-11-04).
The panel of anything but the CEO (a queued, running, paused or completed task, or a session on Orchestration) has Back in its corner where the CEO's own panel has Close: an icon button with a back arrow, named Back to the CEO for a screen reader, which returns the panel to the CEO's panel on the view that panel last showed, or on the view a panel opens on when it has shown none, keeps a maximized panel maximized, and hands the keyboard back to the card or node the panel was opened from, or to the panel when that is hidden behind it.
Escape outside a terminal does what the corner button does: back from a task's panel, close on the CEO's.
Nothing is stacked over the CEO's header: its way back, Close, or Back on a small screen, is in the header's one row, in Classic and in Modern, and its picture there opens the choice of chat style as every session's does (#1987), where until then it went back to the board too.
`frontend/tests/iphone-ceo-header.spec.ts` checks it at an iPhone 15's size in WebKit, and `frontend/tests/panel-header-row.spec.ts` checks the picture's focus ring and its menu on the desktop, maximized or not.
On a small screen (40rem or less), such as a phone over remote access or the board's Home Screen app, a panel opened on anything, a card, the CEO's bar, a node or a train, is the whole view, and the board or the graph leaves the screen until Back, the corner's one button there, or Escape returns to it, with the keyboard on the card or node the panel was opened from (the Overlord, 2026-10-20: "It should become the full screen below the header banner").
While a panel fills that screen the top bar is put away, so a terminal or a conversation keeps the room it would eat (the Overlord, 2026-10-27: "header banner not needed on movible as it eats space"); it comes back with the board or the graph when Back, the corner button or Escape returns to them.
The Command Center's inbox sits in that top bar, so an inbox that opens while a panel fills the screen, as when a Run, an Answer or the AFK report names an item that no longer waits, steps the panel back to the board with the keyboard on the card or node it was opened from, as Back does, but with the page left at the top bar, where the inbox shows open, even when that card lies below the first screen (#1816).
While no panel is open the top bar there is the brand with the connection and the Command Center beside it, and the view switch under them; the tour, the layout button and Open CEO are left off, as are the controls that act on the PC: Open in terminal, Open in VS Code and Open folder.
The panel is held to the visual viewport, which the on-screen keyboard shrinks while the layout viewport stays as it was, so when the keyboard opens the terminal shrinks to the room above it, sends its new rows to the goblin's terminal and keeps its input line and footer in sight, and takes the room back when the keyboard closes; when Safari moves the visual viewport down the page to keep the focused field in sight, the panel moves with it.
`frontend/tests/phone-terminal.spec.ts` checks this at 390 by 844 and 360 by 800, with the keyboard played by a visual viewport the test shrinks.
An iPhone's keyboard leaves the panel about 320 px, so the panel's header is its one row there too, with Back and the view switch, and the terminal there has no floor of its own: the line he is writing stays above the keyboard as a long message grows, in the terminal view and in Modern's box (the Overlord, 2026-10-29: "also temrinal inlit doent scorlll to bottom the longer and longe rth wmessge goes", then "on mobile").
`frontend/tests/iphone-caret.spec.ts` checks this in WebKit at an iPhone 15's size.
The panel's controls are one line at every width the panel can take, 360 px at its narrowest, at the end of its header's one row, or in a row of their own over a baby goblin's header: its controls (Open in terminal and Maximize or Restore today; on a small screen only Open in terminal, while the box shows), then the corner button, Close or Back.
No control overlaps another or is cut, and none is drawn smaller to fit: an icon button stays 44 px square.
The corner button always shows.
A control the row cannot hold at full size goes into More, a menu at the corner that lists each with its icon and its name, opens on its first item, moves with Up and Down and closes on a choice or on Escape, which hand the keyboard back to More, or on a press anywhere else.
A choice that gives the row room for every control again, so that More goes, hands the keyboard to the panel, and Escape still closes it or goes back.
The row is `PanelRow` in `frontend/src/panel-row.tsx`, used by every panel, and what fits is `rowFit` in `frontend/src/panel-fit.ts`.
A control is a `PanelControl` (an id, a name for its menu item, an accessible name, an icon, an importance and what a press does); its importance is written beside the others in `PANEL_IMPORTANCE`, the highest staying in the row longest, and `App.tsx` builds the list.
Until 2 October 2026 the row gave the controls half of what the switch left, so in a panel dragged narrow they were drawn over the switch.

Every terminal pane, native or Herdr, shows a voice bubble in its bottom-right corner, in a strip of its own under the terminal, so it never covers the terminal's text.
A command's terminal on its Command Center card has no microphone button, as the Overlord asked on 2026-10-12: Ctrl+Shift+Space still dictates into it, and its bubble shows over the terminal's corner only while the keys are held, so a dictation never resizes the command's terminal.
It is drawn like the board's dialogue boxes: a microphone in a stepped pixel frame, outlined in Bone while idle and in Moss while it records, and its first-visit hint and recent messages open in the same leather dialogue frame.
That frame is at most 400 px wide and never wider than its terminal less 28 px, so in a panel dragged narrow it is whole; until 2 October 2026 it was sized by the window, and a narrow panel cut off its left side.
Holding Ctrl+Shift+Space in a terminal, native or Herdr, dictates into it through the speech model the supervisor runs on this PC, in a browser tab and in the desktop window alike.
On a touch screen, where nothing is held down with ease, the bubble is the dictation itself: a tap starts it, the bubble shows its bars and is named Stop and send while it listens, and a second tap stops it and types what was said, then presses Enter to send it (the Overlord, 2026-10-21, "Can't hold on my phone screen"); the microphone of a message box or an answer box is tapped the same way and adds the words to its box (`frontend/tests/touch-dictation.spec.ts`).
While it listens the board holds the screen awake: a dictation ends when the page is hidden, and speaking touches nothing, so until 22 October 2026 a phone's own idle timer locked the screen in the middle of a long dictation and it kept only what was said before (the Overlord, 2026-10-22, "it only records like the first few sentences", "It's like a timer or something").
There the bubble's recent dictations are not offered, since a tap dictates.
The phone's private board dictates on this PC's speech model too: the phone's browser records its own microphone, which the private board's HTTPS address allows, and its sound crosses the tailnet to this PC alone.
It was chosen over the phone browser's own speech recognition, which can send the sound to the browser's maker, because voice stays on the Overlord's own devices, its words are the model's measured below, and its delay is that of a dictation at the PC with the tailnet's round trip added.
The bubble says it listens on the frame the keys go down, before the microphone or the speech engine is ready.
The first press opens the microphone, and the board records it through the small audio worklet it serves at `/assets/dictation-capture.js`, which hands the page its samples as they arrive, and while the keys are held the bubble's bars are recent samples of that same microphone's level, each a dot below the loudness of a voice, so silence and a room's hum show a flat dotted line; no bars are drawn while the bubble is idle.
The microphone then stays open while the board has the focus, and the worklet keeps the last second it heard, so every later press records from the moment the keys go down, and a phrase begun with the press is kept whole.
It closes when the window loses the focus or the page is hidden, and once 30 minutes pass with no dictation, and the next press opens it again.
So the first press after the board takes the focus loses what is said while the microphone opens: on this PC, with Chromium's stand-in microphone, a median of 5 ms in the desktop window and 155 ms in a browser, at most 0.42 s, and a real microphone adds the time it takes to start.
A microphone opened before any press would show as in use whenever the board had the focus, before anyone dictated, which is why it waits for the first press (the CEO's decision of 11 October 2026).
The audio context the worklet runs in is made while the page is idle, before the first press needs it, and runs while the microphone is open; Safari has no idle callback, so there it is made a second after the view, and a browser without Web Audio makes none.
Until 11 October 2026 the bubble said it listened only once the first samples arrived, 0.1 to 0.7 s after the press on this PC, because every press opened the microphone again and the bars built a second audio context of their own, which held the page still for up to 0.27 s.
Until 9 October 2026 the context was suspended at every release and the bubble said it listened as soon as the microphone was open, 0.4 to 0.7 s before a busy PC had the context running again, so a short dictation recorded nothing and was told as "Nothing was heard."
A recognizer that has not begun to listen five seconds after the microphone opened ends the hold, and the CEO is told.
A hold let go before anything was recorded, as before the microphone opens, types nothing, asks the supervisor nothing and says nothing.
As the recording begins the page tells the supervisor, with `POST /api/dictation/warm`, so it loads the speech engine while the words are said.
A dictation of any length goes in pieces while the keys are held: a piece of four seconds or more is handed on once 0.3 s of quiet follows speech in it, and one that reaches twenty seconds with no such pause, as in a noisy room, is cut where it was quietest.
Each piece goes as soon as it ends, in one channel at 16 kHz, as a WAV to `POST /api/dictation` on the board's own address, one at a time and in order, so its words are recognised while the Overlord is still speaking.
Releasing any of the three keys sends what followed the last piece, and the words of every piece are typed together, in order, as one line through the terminal's paste, so nothing is sent until Enter, and the page asks nothing else of anyone while it dictates.
Once anything loud was said, room noise after the last piece and a piece with nothing loud in it are not sent, because the model writes words of its own into a room's hiss, such as "It is a good idea."
A part is loud when its 10 ms reach -40 dB of full scale, or stand 12 dB over the sound's own quiet, the mean of its quietest 200 ms, and above -70 dB, so a microphone set low or a softly spoken word is speech too: with only the -40 dB rule, real recordings turned down by 25 dB lost 691 of their 2252 words on the board, words the model heard in the same sound handed to it whole.
A dictation with nothing loud in it at all is sent whole, as it is.
A dictation typed right after another, with no key pressed in the terminal between them, starts with a space, so the two do not run together; the recent dictations keep the words alone.
The bubble goes idle at the release, without waiting for the words, and the next dictation can start while the words of the last are still on their way; the words of each are typed when recognition finishes.
A native terminal's paste follows the program's own bracketed paste mode, and the Herdr view, whose screen is redrawn from frames, always sends a bracketed paste, as its clipboard paste does.
Releasing the keys anywhere on the page, the window losing focus or the page being hidden also stops listening, and the window losing focus or the page being hidden closes the microphone.
A blocked or missing microphone or silence is explained in a note for six seconds, and only those: a dictation with speech in it is never told as nothing heard.
"Nothing was heard." is said once, at the release, of a hold that listened and had no words in it, and never once the next dictation has begun, when it would read as being about that one.
A recording that cannot begin, as when the worklet cannot be loaded, goes to the CEO at once and the bubble never says it listens.
A piece whose words could not be had is asked for again half a second later and once more two seconds after that, with its sound kept, and when that does not help, the supervisor's words, such as a machine with no memory to spare or a board reached from another machine, go to the CEO through `POST /api/ceo/report` and nothing is shown on the board or typed, as every failure the board sees goes to the CEO.
So does a dictation with speech in it that the engine answered no words for, and one whose words were not in within 120 seconds of the release.
The supervisor keeps the sound of the last piece with speech in it that the engine answered no words for in `state/dictation-no-words.wav` in the home, replaced by the next, so that report can be played again and its cause found; it never leaves this PC, and the report names the file and says how many seconds were recorded in how long a hold, since a microphone that opens at the press records less than the hold.
A sound with nothing loud in it, or one the engine heard words in, is not kept, and a sound that cannot be kept is a failure that goes to the CEO.
A blocked microphone is explained where it is allowed again: in a browser tab, the browser's site settings; in the desktop app, whose window grants the page every permission itself, Windows Settings > Privacy & security > Microphone, with Microphone access and Let desktop apps access your microphone turned on.
The install sets the speech model up, so a dictation finds it ready; where it could not, the first dictation sets it up, and the note stays and follows it, asking the supervisor each second: how many of its megabytes have arrived, then that it is being checked and unpacked; it ends with "Dictation is ready: hold Ctrl+Shift+Space and speak.", or with why the set-up failed, which stays until the next dictation.
The words of a dictation the set-up refused are not kept, and the note says to dictate again when it is ready.
Such a refusal is shown at once and not asked again, since asking again would not set the model up sooner.
While it listens a muted line beside the bubble names the model, and the foot of its list says what heard the recent dictations.
A browser with a speech recognition of its own offers **Use this browser's speech recognition instead** there, off until it is ticked and kept in that browser: Edge and Chrome recognise speech in their vendors' online services, so with it the audio leaves the machine while the keys are held, and that line and the list say that this is what listens. The desktop window has no such recognition and offers no choice.
The browser's recognition ends by itself after about eight seconds of silence and after a long stretch of listening, so while the keys are held the board starts it again and joins what each part heard: a pause in a hold shows no note and types nothing early, and the whole hold is typed at the release.
One that ends within a second of starting cannot listen and is not started again, and a failure that is real, a blocked or missing microphone or no network, is still said once and ends the hold.
Clicking the bubble lists the board's five most recent dictations, newest first, from every box that dictates, a terminal, a message box and an answer box in the Command Center, in every window, the desktop window and a browser tab alike; the board reads no other dictation app.
The supervisor keeps that one list, the newest ten, in `state/dictations.json` in the home, so it outlasts a restart and never leaves this PC: `GET /api/dictations` reads it and `POST /api/dictations` adds one, both only from the board's own page at 127.0.0.1, as dictation itself, and nothing about the words is logged.
An open list reads it again when its window takes the focus, with what another window added meanwhile.
Each has Copy and Paste into this terminal, which pastes as dictation does and hands the terminal the keyboard back, and Escape closes the list.
A message box's list says Paste into the message instead: it puts the chosen words where the caret or the selection is, keeps the rest of the draft, and reads no clipboard, so what an older dictation adds is never taken for an empty clipboard (the Overlord, 2026-10-27).
A first visit shows a hint about the shortcut beside the bubble once, until it is dismissed.

The supervisor's speech engine is what makes that dictation free and local.
`config/voice.json` is its one setting: the engine and the model, each a download pinned by its address and SHA-256, the files of it that are kept, and the engine's library and how it loads the model.
The build carries that setting, so an install of a newer build brings a newer pin; a home that keeps a `config/voice.json` of its own uses that one, and one that cannot be read leaves the supervisor without dictation rather than falling back.
The pin today is Parakeet TDT 0.6B v2, English, by NVIDIA ([nvidia/parakeet-tdt-0.6b-v2](https://huggingface.co/nvidia/parakeet-tdt-0.6b-v2), [CC BY 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/)), converted to ONNX and quantized to int8 by sherpa-onnx in its build of 16 August 2025, run by sherpa-onnx 1.13.8 (Apache 2.0) on ONNX Runtime (MIT), both from the sherpa-onnx project's releases, 483 MB to download.
It was chosen on 14 October 2026 by a measured comparison on this PC with the fleet busy, on 95 public recordings of real speech (LibriSpeech test-other, Common Voice accents and EdAcc conversations) through the board's own path, against Moonshine tiny, which it replaced, Moonshine base, Parakeet TDT 110M, Parakeet unified 0.6B, Canary 180M flash, Whisper small.en, distil-whisper small.en, Whisper large-v3-turbo and Qwen3-ASR 0.6B: it got the fewest words wrong, 7.1 in 100 against Moonshine tiny's 19.2, of the models that answer a five-second line within about a second.
To swap it, pin another model's download in `config/voice.json` and give its files in `args` with the flags of sherpa-onnx's own `sherpa-onnx-offline`: a transducer's `--model-type`, `--encoder`, `--decoder` and `--joiner`, or a Moonshine model's `--moonshine-encoder` and `--moonshine-merged-decoder`, with `--tokens` and `--num-threads`.
`POST /api/dictation` takes a WAV sound of at most 8 MB and answers its words, and refuses any other body as a bad request before reading it, only from the board's own page on this PC, at 127.0.0.1 and through no proxy, or from the Overlord's private board, which the remote access gate admitted as his own Tailscale login; `POST /api/dictation/warm` takes the same two.
Until 21 October 2026 the private board dictated nothing, so his phone could only use its keyboard's dictation.
The install puts the engine and the model in `caches/voice/` under the home with `goblins dictation setup`, which keeps what is there already and removes an engine or model an earlier pin left once the pinned one is in place, so a machine never keeps two models.
A board whose build pins a model the home lacks while an earlier one is there, as after an update to a build that pins a newer model, fetches it as it starts and removes the earlier one; an update that pins the same model keeps it.
Otherwise the first dictation downloads what is missing into `caches/voice/` and answers that it is being downloaded, with how many of its megabytes have arrived; `GET /api/dictation` says which model listens and whether it is ready, missing with why the last download failed, or being fetched with how far it is.
Each download's size is pinned with its checksum in `config/voice.json`, so the first answer already says how big it is.
A download is kept only when it matches its SHA-256, holds every file named, and no program or library in it is linked against a Windows networking library; otherwise nothing of it is kept and the answer says why.
Offline, the answer names the address to download and the file to save it as in `caches/voice/`, where it is held to the same checksum.
The page's warming, or else the first dictation, starts the engine in a process of its own, a hidden `goblins voice-worker` that loads sherpa-onnx's library and the model once, and the next dictations go to it while it is loaded, one at a time, through a pipe: the sound is never written to a file, and the worker gets none of the fleet's environment beyond what Windows needs to start it.
Loading starts as the keys are pressed, so it is done before they are let go, and the engine stays loaded between dictations, holding about 800 MB.
The worker asks Windows for the scheduling `goblins serve` asks for: a board started at login runs hidden, and Windows otherwise throttles the hidden worker, which then took 4 to 5 s to answer.
The engine hears a sound in pieces of at most eight seconds, so its memory stays the same at any length and a home that pins Moonshine still works: Moonshine tiny answers no words at all for a sound of 9.3 s or more, which made every long dictation "Nothing was heard." until 8 October 2026.
A longer sound is cut at its quietest places, a piece with nothing loud in it is left out when the sound has speech elsewhere, and the words of the pieces are joined in order.
Each piece ends half a second after its last loud part, loud as the page reads it, cutting a longer quiet end and making up a shorter one with silence: Moonshine, the pin until 14 October 2026, writes until it hears the end of the line, so a sound cut mid-word ran on, repeating itself, and a long quiet end could make it answer nothing.
The page records at the microphone's own rate, usually 48 kHz, and averages each piece down to the model's 16 kHz.
On this PC, with the fleet busy, the words of a five-second line are in the terminal 0.2 to 1.2 s after the keys are let go, and of a longer dictation 0.3 to 0.4 s after the last piece is said, since only what followed the last piece is still to be heard, and the engine holds about 800 MB at every length. Started cold, it loads and answers a five-second line in 2.7 to 2.8 s, and since loading starts as the keys are pressed, that is hidden behind anything longer said.
It is ended after 5 minutes without a dictation, when it breaks or a dictation is abandoned, when there is no room for it, and when the supervisor stops, and the next dictation loads it again; a fault in the engine ends that process, never the board.
The Overlord's dictations in one burst came up to four minutes apart on the board's record and the bursts at least eleven minutes apart, so a burst loads the engine once; until 3 November 2026 it was kept for 30 minutes, set for Moonshine tiny's 140 MB and not revisited for parakeet, which held about 850 MB through every pause (#1612).
It needs 2 GB of free memory and of free commit and says so when the machine has less, which is far under the fleet's 4 GB floor on purpose: dictation is how the Overlord types, and it must not refuse because the fleet is busy.
`goblins doctor` says dictation is ready, naming the model, its version and the engine, or names what is missing, how many megabytes, and the fix, `goblins dictation setup`.

Key-to-echo latency, measured with `tests/acceptance/terminal_latency.mjs` against the example fixture on 25 September 2026: the Herdr view on main e6f7ea97 took p50 74 ms and p95 592 ms with 3 of 100 keys unechoed after 5 seconds and 4.6 s to a live screen, and the native view p50 24 ms and p95 34 to 36 ms with none missed and 0.4 s to a live screen.
With synchronized redraws and the 20 px font, measured with the DOM renderer in headless Edge, the native view took p50 28 ms and p95 41 ms with none missed.
`tests/acceptance/terminal_checklist.mjs --url <board> --title <task> --fill <command>` opens a task's terminal from its card in headless Edge, runs the command in it once for history, and checks the terminal with real wheel and key input: the inset on each side at every size Ctrl+Plus and Ctrl+Minus reach, any scroll bar in sight, history on the wheel and the way back down, and typing in the history returning to the bottom.
On 27 September, against a PowerShell terminal on a scratch home, the view that reserved a scroll bar's width kept 10 px left, 16 to 22 px right, 6 px above and 2.8 to 28.8 px under the input line; the even fit keeps 10 to 16 px left, right and below at every size from 19 to 22 px, with the rest above, and the other checks pass as before (`docs/evidence/cg-native-desktop/terminal-checklist`).
Switching, measured with `tests/acceptance/terminal_switch.mjs` over 30 round trips between two native terminals: when each switch remounted the view it took p50 44 ms and p95 55 ms from the click to the other terminal drawn whole, with 47 blank frames; with every opened terminal kept live it took p50 16 ms and p95 21 ms with no blank and no half-drawn frame, and a first attach shows no half-drawn frame.
Since 2026-11-04 each switch remounts the view again, so no session he left keeps a live connection.
An idle board draws nothing: a working goblin's status dot wears a still ring rather than an endless pulse, because a pulse restyles and repaints the page on every frame for as long as the board is open.
`tests/acceptance/board_idle.mjs` measures an open board while nothing happens on it and fails when the page runs an endless animation or restyles itself more than 30 times a second.
On 27 September 2026 the pulse kept the live board (five working goblins, 2560 by 1440, headless Edge) at 423 to 431 ms of GPU-process CPU and 201 to 226 ms of renderer CPU every second, with 113 to 141 style recalculations a second; without it the same page took 15 to 28 ms, 27 to 35 ms and 7 to 17.
`docs/evidence/cg-board-ux/pr1b/side-by-side.mp4` records one Claude Code session shown at once in the board and in Windows Terminal through `goblins attach`, while the board switches to another native terminal and back and then to the CEO and back: the Claude Code answer arrives while the board shows the CEO, and is already drawn when the board switches back.
The CEO in that recording still runs in Herdr, so its first open shows Herdr's connecting state over an empty pane, which is not the native view.

CEO transport reads the `state/primary.json` registration and binds each queued message to its fingerprint.
The primary CEO writes that registration itself: Claude's SessionStart hook does it after the digest settles custody, and a Codex or pi CEO runs `goblins register`, which the first prompt goblins starts it with tells it to do.
`goblins register` refreshes it by hand.
Registering the same process in the same terminal again leaves the file byte-identical, so a compact, clear or resume keeps the fingerprint pending questions, reviews and answers are bound to.
A CEO registers from native terminal `ceo`, the `goblins host` that tells the program it starts which terminal it is through `GOBLINS_HOST_ID`, and from no other: that terminal's program is [the CEO's own session](contract/supervision.md#the-ceos-own-session).
Registration trusts no variable alone: the terminal's program, named by its host's record by pid and creation time, must be one of the caller's own ancestors, or the caller must carry the terminal's proof value (see [Goblin questions](#goblin-questions)), and the host must answer on its pipe, since a host that was killed leaves its record behind.
That harness holds the home's session lock from then on: registering takes it, and takes it over from a live holder that is another process, which is no CEO, recording the takeover in `state\custody.audit` and queueing one `check` wake that names the holder it replaced.
The registration names that terminal, and it stays valid while the host's record names the registered process as the terminal's program.
Plain `goblins register` names the harness from that program: `claude.exe` is Claude Code, and the `cmd /c` a native terminal runs an npm script shim through, by its path, is named by the base name of the script cmd runs, `codex.cmd` or `pi.cmd`, read from cmd's own command line; any other program is refused.
A session in no native terminal, a Herdr pane included, or in any other native terminal, registers nothing, and a registration an older build wrote for a CEO in a Herdr pane reads as not reachable: the board delivers nothing to it and takes no proof from it until the CEO is started again in a native terminal.
A message for the CEO is typed into its terminal once, then Enter submits it, over a delivery connection of its own: the host acknowledges each part once it has written it into the terminal's input, and it is never typed again.
A Claude Code CEO is given a line longer than 512 bytes as one bracketed paste, which it takes whole however it reads it: typed as keys, a CEO slow to read in a turn read a message of 1,512 bytes in two parts on 2026-10-29, took the first 1,024 bytes as a paste and the rest as keys, and handed its model only the keys at its next tool call (#1740).
A shorter line is still typed as keys, since Claude Code takes it as keys however it is read, and a path alone given as a paste becomes an image.
A line that starts with `?` goes to a CEO of any harness as one bracketed paste: Claude Code and Codex take a `?` typed at an empty prompt line as their shortcut-help key, as the CEO's Claude Code took the Overlord's lone `?` on 2026-11-02, and a paste is written as it is.
The board shows it delivered only once the CEO has it: an idle CEO once its own prompt hook, which names the native terminal its harness runs in, reports taking it, and a CEO in a turn once its harness's own record of the conversation, found by the session its registration names, shows the answer handed to its model.
A screen turning to work is no proof, since Enter may have chosen a dialog's option instead.
A prompt hook is no proof for a CEO in a turn either: Claude Code 2.1.292 runs it when it queues text typed during a turn, before it takes the text at its next tool call.
A CEO inside a turn takes an ordinary message only at its next tool call, so a message typed into a turn is neither failed nor delivered the moment its submit key goes, and the delivery waits on no take: its action stays on its way (`status: running`, with `awaiting` naming the terminal, when it was submitted, the digest of what was typed and, as `is_held`, that a turn under way has yet to take it) and reads "Queued in the CEO's session. The CEO takes it at its next tool call, or as its current turn ends." with one check; its Modern bubble reads Queued with its controls, as [The message box](#the-message-box) says.
One he sends now interrupts that turn with the harness's own send-now key instead, as [the message box](#the-message-box) says, and reads `Typed while it was working and sent now.`
A harness in a turn can read what is typed into it late, so the delivery waits up to 30 seconds for its words to show on the prompt line before it presses the submit key; one that gave up after five left its words there unsubmitted once the harness read them, and every later message waited behind them (#2306).
One that ends with no submit key taken clears its words off the prompt line, or has the supervisor clear them once they show, and reads `Not sent`, as [Board and orchestration](#board-and-orchestration) says of a message to a goblin.
An idle CEO slow to start its turn is given five seconds to show the message taken, and one that has not shown it by then is sent too, not failed.
The supervisor then settles it: the record showing it taken makes it delivered, with two checks, however late that comes.
It warns only when the message can no longer arrive or plainly has not: the terminal closed or was started again, or its screen has shown no turn for three minutes with the message still not taken, counted from the last look that showed a turn, so a message behind a long tool call never warns.
The warning is the action's `advice`, written for the Overlord (open the terminal and press Enter if the answer is waiting in its box, or type it there), and the action reads `uncertain`; a take that comes after the warning still delivers it, a message's after its terminal closed too, since a record written late, as on a loaded machine, shows the take only once the terminal is gone (#2541).
A message held in its harness's own queue reads `Not sent` instead once that terminal is gone, as [Board and orchestration](#board-and-orchestration) says, while an answer held there still warns.
A second answer is refused while the first is sent, so nothing is typed twice, and a supervisor restart leaves a sent message sent.
A goblin's answer typed during its turn is settled the same way, by its task and spawn generation and the record its harness keeps for its worktree, and its review item reads delivered only then.
A host started by an older goblins cannot acknowledge, so the board refuses anything it sends that CEO with nothing typed until the CEO is started again.
The board shows a native CEO's terminal in its panel, from the CEO bar and from Orchestration.
`goblins` shows a CEO registered in a native terminal in its own terminal, and with no CEO running starts a new one in native terminal `ceo`, in the CEO home, running the remembered harness itself (`claude.exe` for Claude Code) so the terminal ends with it.
Its environment starts, as a native goblin's does, from the one Windows gives a new process of the user, never the launcher's, so a CEO started from inside another Claude Code session is not that session's child (which would save no transcript): the session markers and harness billing keys are dropped, the CEO is placed in the supervisor's home and projects root, and it keeps the launcher's Herdr session and configuration without the pane variables `serve` drops.
Each registration records the CEO's conversation in `state/ceo-conversation.json`, the latest one included when the same process registers again after a compact, clear or resume, which leaves `primary.json` as it was.
Plain `goblins register` names no conversation, so a Codex CEO that registers with it records the thread Codex gives every command it runs (`CODEX_THREAD_ID`), and a pi CEO the session pi 0.85.1 gives every command it runs (`PI_SESSION_ID`); no harness takes another's variable for its own, since a CEO started inside another harness's session inherits that one's.
A home whose CEO last ran in a native terminal brings a closed CEO back there, on that conversation when its harness is the one chosen: `claude --resume <session>`, `codex resume <session>` or `pi --session <session>`, as a pi goblin is resumed; a terminal that ends within three seconds, as a harness that cannot resume the conversation does, or during startup settling, is started again on a new one, and the output names the conversation it left; so is a Claude Code conversation whose context is past the 400K tokens a CEO compacts within, by what its last request after its last compaction carried, or one Claude Code has written no transcript for ([A CEO whose terminal ended](#a-ceo-whose-terminal-ended)), and a pi conversation whose file pi has not written yet, since pi 0.85.1 writes a session's file when its first reply ends and exits on a `--session` it finds no file for.
A fresh terminal that also ends during startup is reported as failed before the quick start offers to attach.
`goblins resume` restarts a CEO that runs in native terminal `ceo`, holding `state/.ceo-launch.lock` so two restarts never start two CEOs: it closes the terminal, which ends the harness and everything it started, and when the conversation in `state/ceo-conversation.json` was recorded by the process that terminal ran and can be resumed, it starts the harness again there with the arguments a closed CEO comes back with.
Otherwise, for a conversation whose context is past 400K tokens, one in Claude Code with no transcript, one in pi whose file pi has not written yet or one not recorded for that process, it starts the CEO there on a new conversation, as the remembered harness, with the digest a closed CEO's new conversation starts with, and says why (the Overlord, 2026-10-09, of a refusal: "this makes no sense").
It stops nothing only where it cannot restart the CEO, when the terminal has no host record or does not close or the CEO's program is not on its PATH, and with no CEO running there it goes on as `goblins` does.
Run inside terminal `ceo` itself, where `GOBLINS_HOST_ID` is `ceo`, it closes nothing, since closing that terminal would end the restart with it, and says to run it in another terminal or from the board.
The restarted CEO is told with its first instruction that it was restarted, and on a new conversation why, and its startup dialogs are answered as a closed CEO's are, and a terminal that ends within three seconds is started again on a new conversation, with the conversation it could not resume named, and one that ends while those dialogs are answered is reported as ended, with nothing offered to attach to; `goblins resume` with no task named is the same command.
Whenever a CEO coming back, closed or restarted, starts on a new conversation because its harness ended at once, or a closed one's ended while its startup dialogs were answered, `state/ceo-conversation-left.json` records the conversation and its resume arguments, and the snapshot's `ceo_conversation_left` says so on the board until the CEO next comes back on its conversation, which removes the file.
The Harness section of the CEO's task panel offers the same restart for a CEO registered in its native terminal: **Restart** sends `POST /api/ceo/restart` immediately, guarded by the Host, Origin and token checks like every other change, and a CEO that could not be restarted says why beside its button.
Beside Restart, a dropdown shows the selected harness's name and icon and offers Claude Code, Codex and pi, starting on the current harness.
Selecting a harness sends nothing until Restart is pressed, which sends `harness` as `claude`, `codex` or `pi` without a confirmation dialog.
Both controls are disabled while restarting and appear only under Harness in the CEO's task list, never in its header or the CEO bar.
An omitted harness or the same choice keeps the existing resume behavior.
A different choice starts a fresh conversation with the home's digest and saves the harness in `state/ceo-harness`, as `goblins resume --harness <harness>` does.
An explicit harness wins over a saved selection, as the dropdown, `goblins --harness` and `goblins resume --harness` all promise: the CEO starts as it with no engine flags, and the selection stays saved with a note on it that the CEO started as another harness, until the CEO that started names the engine it runs, which the record then holds in the selection's place, so a CEO started as the selection's own harness before then runs it.
The selection keeps that harness as `overridden`, so every later start or restart that names no harness, such as a harness Update or `goblins resume`, keeps the harness the CEO runs, on its own conversation, until the CEO starts as the selection's own harness, a selection is saved again, or the record names the running CEO's own engine.
A selection the fleet cannot read names no harness to start, so the CEO starts as the home last ran it and the board reports the selection's own problem rather than a CEO that cannot start.
The requested harness must be available and its choice saved before the current terminal closes, so a refused choice leaves the CEO running.
A restart that started the CEO on a new conversation, which the response's `resumed: false` says, shows The CEO restarted on a new conversation beside Restart, and the line stays while the restarted CEO starts and Restart is gone.
The snapshot's `ceo_terminal_since` says when the host of the CEO's terminal started, and the board's view of the CEO names it, so a restart, which replaces that host under the same terminal id, opens the view again on the new host instead of leaving the old one's ended screen.
Below Restart, a CEO engine selector picks the harness, model and effort the CEO runs, from the same installed engine catalog a task's selector reads, and `POST /api/ceo/engine` saves them in `state/ceo-engine.json` with the Host, Origin and token checks every change takes.
That file is the CEO's one record of its model and effort, as a task record is a goblin's ([The message box](#the-message-box) says how every view reads it): the supervisor keeps it on what the CEO's own conversation says it runs, so a `/model` or `/effort` typed in the CEO's terminal reaches it, and where none is saved it writes the file from that conversation, so the CEO's next start runs what it ran.
A selection that waits, or that a problem holds for the CEO's next start, is the Overlord's choice and stays as saved, whatever the running CEO names.
Saving restarts nothing and interrupts nothing: the selection waits for a safe turn boundary of the CEO, which is the stopping point an update waits for, on the same two readings ([Harness updates](#harness-updates)), with nothing in the terminal's job beyond its own program line, and a selection that is waiting reads Pending under the selector, with the reason after it in muted words, outliving a supervisor restart until the boundary comes.
The terminal's job is read against the harness its program runs, so a Codex or pi CEO's `cmd` and npm shim, the launcher they start and what the harness starts as it launches are the terminal's own program line rather than work, while a job the harness started after launching holds the selection, and a machine service the close spares stays spared with what runs under it.
A selection that cannot be verified stops pending instead of restarting the CEO on a guess, and one whose catalog entry has gone reads Not applied, followed by the words saying it stays saved, with the model and effort it names, for the CEO's next start, which applies it by design.
A session that changed after the save already had the selection applied by the restart that changed it, so the selection stops pending with no problem, while a restart as another harness or a session that began before the save reports the selection as saved for the CEO's next start.
A selection saved while native terminal ceo runs but nothing has registered in it waits with the reason that the CEO's terminal is starting, so a restart it waits on is not read as one a session left behind, and one that finds a session without a saved one takes it as the session its restart replaces instead of reading itself as applied.
The restart then starts the CEO with the harness's own flags: `--model <model> -c model_reasoning_effort=<effort>` for Codex, `--model <model> --effort <effort>` for Claude Code and `--model <model> --thinking <effort>` for pi, and `--model <model>` alone where the record's effort is `default`, which the supervisor records and the selector cannot save ([The message box](#the-message-box)).
On the same harness the CEO comes back on its conversation with them applied, as a Codex goblin's switch resumes with the same flags, and a different harness starts a new conversation the way Restart's dropdown does.
Any start as the selection's own harness carries those flags and, once its terminal has launched, clears a problem an earlier start, or an apply while it started, left on the selection, leaving whether a change is pending as it was; a start that fails before it launches leaves the problem.
`goblins --harness claude|codex|pi` chooses the harness the CEO starts as, and the home remembers it in `state/ceo-harness`; without a harness named, a home with a saved CEO engine starts as that engine's harness, or as the harness an explicit start chose over it since, else as the remembered one, which is why the quick start asks only where neither is there to read.
The board's first-run page reads the same remembered harness, shows it as chosen, and remembers the one it starts, so the terminal and the board hold one answer.
In a native terminal Claude Code runs as `claude.exe` and codex and pi as their npm script shims through `cmd /c`, as a native goblin's do; goblins starts no CEO in Herdr.
Each is the program the launcher's `PATH` finds, started by its path, so a stub harness first on a rig's `PATH` is the one that starts, never the one the terminal's `PATH`, the user's, would find, and a harness the launcher cannot find is refused; the board's connection checks and repairs start `codex` the same way.
A harness that is not installed or not signed in goes to the quick start's install or sign-in step, and nothing is remembered or started until it is ready.
Its command line ends with one first prompt, which has it run `goblins register` in that terminal and then do what AGENTS.md says a CEO does at the start of a session; Claude Code needs none, since its SessionStart hook registers it and prints the digest.
That is a home's first CEO, which waits for the Overlord.
Every start that brings back a CEO that ran in the home before, by its conversation record, ends instead with a first instruction on every harness: that it is the home's CEO started again, why (its terminal ended and how, the machine restarted, the Overlord reopened it, it was restarted, `goblins` started it again), and on a new conversation why, and to read the digest, run `goblins drain`, handle and acknowledge its wakes and carry on with the fleet without waiting for the Overlord, so nobody types into it; a Codex or pi CEO is told to run `goblins register` first.
It names none of the characters `cmd /c` reads, since a Codex or pi CEO starts through its npm shim.
A Codex CEO starts with `-c tui.copy_on_select=always`, as a Codex goblin does, and a pi CEO with `--tui-mode fullscreen` where its help offers fullscreen, read from `pi --help` as a pi goblin's is, so each draws on its own full screen with its input line and footer kept at the bottom.
A Codex CEO also starts with `--no-daemon` where the installed Codex's help lists it, read from `codex --help` as a Codex goblin's is, so the commands its harness runs descend from its own terminal instead of a shared background app-server an earlier session left running; a Codex whose help does not list it is given none, since it would refuse the start.
What a CEO in each harness gets is one table, `supervisor.CEOCapabilities`: how it is woken, how it registers, whether a closed one comes back on its conversation, and what it goes without.
The quick start's notes, the first-run page and `goblins doctor`, whose `ceo harness:` line names the remembered harness with a `goes without:` line for each thing it lacks, all read that table, so none says more than another.
A real Codex CEO and a real pi CEO started this way were proved in scratch homes on 2026-10-02: each registered itself within 15 seconds of its terminal opening, ran `goblins session-start` and the other start-of-session checks, and acknowledged a queued wake within 12 seconds of the supervisor typing its line; the Codex CEO's terminal was then closed, and started again with `codex resume` and the same first prompt it registered again on the conversation it had (`TestACEOStartedAsGoblinsStartsItRegistersIsWokenAndComesBack`, run only when asked).
A harness starts on the model its own configuration names: a Codex whose configured model the signed-in account cannot use shows Codex's error in the terminal, runs no first turn and so never registers, until the model is changed and it is told to run `goblins register`.
A CEO already running keeps its harness, and goblins says the choice applies to the next start.
A Claude Code CEO is woken by its own Stop hook, which waits on the wake queue.
A Codex or pi CEO in a native terminal is woken by the supervisor, once it has registered by running `goblins register` in that terminal, as its first prompt tells it to: while wakes it has not been told about are queued, the supervisor types one line into its terminal, `ceo watcher wake: N queued wakes (...); run goblins drain, ...`, only when two readings of its screen a second apart show it idle at an empty composer, so never mid-turn and never over text left unsent, at most one line every 30 seconds, and each queued record is covered by one line only.
The line is proved taken as `goblins send` proves a send, and one that nothing proves taken is reported and not typed again.
A wake left untyped is recorded in `state\ceo-wake-skips.jsonl`, once for each new wake and each new reason, with why in plain words, such as "its screen shows it in the middle of a turn", and the screen's last eight rows; the file keeps the newest 200.
On 2026-10-13 six wakes waited 16 minutes for an idle Codex CEO and nothing said why.
Once the oldest untyped wake has waited five minutes while the CEO is not in a turn, the CEO cannot be told why, so the Overlord is: one Command Center item in plain words, such as "The CEO has not been told of 2 fleet updates for 5 minutes, because its screen shows work it started still running. Restart the CEO so it reads them?", whose one action is the board's own Restart the CEO, with its confirmation, which the supervisor carries out.
A CEO in the middle of a turn is never offered it, however long its wakes wait: it takes them as the turn ends, and a restart would end it at work.
A turn is read before anything else on the screen, by the harness's working row or any row ending in its interrupt hint, so words of a dialog a busy CEO read do not count; only a command left running or a shell count is work at an idle prompt.
That reads a CEO as busy when such a hint is merely quoted on its screen; its wakes are still recorded, and it is never offered a restart, which errs toward a CEO at work.
The reason must hold on every look for a minute, counted afresh for each stretch of untyped wakes, before the item is made, an item already made is taken back once the CEO is at work again, and a CEO that no longer runs, or whose terminal runs another program, is offered nothing.
The item runs no command, and the supervisor refuses to run it as one, since a CEO a run terminal started is not proven to outlive that terminal.
It is made once for that oldest wake and taken back while nobody ran it once no wake waits untyped, whether the line was typed or the CEO ran `goblins drain` itself.
A restart that ran and did not help is offered again once it has been over five minutes, and one that expired at once.
Only a wake the supervisor chose not to type is recorded: a Claude Code CEO takes its wakes through its Stop hook, and a line typed but not proven taken is reported as before.
A Codex or pi CEO someone starts in Herdr by hand has no wake path: it sees reports only when it checks the board or runs `goblins drain`.
`goblins attach` shows a native terminal in any console: the registered CEO's, or the one named; `--state <dir>` names the fleet's state folder for a console that does not inherit the supervisor's environment.

The panel's Open in terminal button, shown while it shows a session's terminal or messages, and never on a small screen (40rem or less, #2222), opens that terminal in a new Windows Terminal window beside the board, through `POST /api/terminal/open`, which takes only what the view shows and runs the supervisor's own programs.
For a goblin or CEO in Herdr the pane is proved as its view proves it, Herdr brings its workspace and tab to the front, and the window runs `herdr --session <session>`, so it opens on that pane; for a native terminal the window runs `goblins attach --state <dir> <terminal>`, since a Windows Terminal window does not inherit the supervisor's environment.
A goblin whose no-mistakes gate owns its task is refused with the gate's reason, with nothing brought to the front and no window, as typing into it is; the CEO's own terminal has no gate.
A board without Windows Terminal, or a terminal it cannot prove, says why under the button.
With no CEO registered, `goblins` and `goblins attach` show native terminal `ceo` while its host answers, since the CEO started there may not have registered yet; a CEO registered in Herdr always comes first.
The board follows the same rule for the CEO's panel, so a CEO the first-run page started can be answered there, for example Claude Code asking whether to trust its folder, before it registers.
A starting CEO has not registered yet: Claude Code registers through its SessionStart hook after its onboarding and sign-in, and a Codex or pi CEO when its first prompt runs `goblins register`.
So while native terminal `ceo` is up with no CEO registered the snapshot says `ceo_starting` and carries no registration problem, the board opens that terminal by itself once per page (a panel the Overlord closed is not reopened), and the CEO bar says "Starting: answer anything it asks in its terminal."
The board's root shows the first-run page when no CEO runs in a home with neither a saved harness nor a previous CEO, and otherwise shows the normal board.
The snapshot's `ceo_runs` means a CEO is registered and running, or native terminal `ceo` is up for one that is starting.
Its `ceo_configured` means the home has a valid saved harness, so reopening the app keeps the board visible before registration and after a failed start.
That home offers Reopen when its CEO is not running, without asking the quick start again.
A home whose CEO registered and has since ended, with no terminal up for a new one, has a closed CEO (the snapshot's `ceo_closed`), however it ended: he closed it, it crashed, or the machine restarted.
Its board shows no first-run page and no registration problem: the CEO's bar says `The CEO is closed.` with one action, Reopen the CEO, and the CEO's terminal slot says it is closed.
Reopen sends `POST /api/ceo/reopen`, guarded by the Host, Origin and token checks like every other change, and the supervisor brings the CEO back as `goblins` does: as the remembered agent, in native terminal `ceo`, on the conversation it last registered with where its harness resumes one, and on a new one when there is none or the resumed terminal ends within three seconds, told with its first instruction that the Overlord reopened it.
It is refused while a CEO runs or is starting, a CEO that cannot come back says why on the bar, and the terminal of the reopened CEO opens by itself, as after Start.
Only a sound registration whose process ended is a closed CEO: one that cannot be read keeps its problem, since it is wrong and stays wrong.
A home no CEO ever registered in shows the first-run page only when it has no saved harness.
The page shows as done what the terminal quick start already knows, and asks for neither again: the Code Goblins home the CEO starts in, and the agent remembered in `state/ceo-harness` when there is one.
It offers Claude Code, Codex and pi as one row of icon tabs, moved with Left and Right, opening on the remembered agent or else on the recommended one, Claude Code, whose tab is marked Recommended; under the row is whether the marked agent is on PATH, its sign-in when it is, and the few words the table says on what a CEO in it gets.
The sign-in is what the agent's own status command (`claude auth status --json`, `codex login status`, `pi auth check`) says when run in the environment the CEO's terminal starts with, so the page says what that terminal will find even where the supervisor runs with another home, as in a scratch profile: Signed in, Not signed in, or Sign-in unknown when the command does not say; the page asks every installed agent at once, and Start asks none.
It asks for no project: Start starts the agent as the CEO in native terminal `ceo` in the home, as `goblins` does, and remembers the agent as goblins would.
The projects folder, where goblins find a project by its name, is optional: the field opens on the recorded `GOBLINS_PROJECTS_ROOT`, Look lists the git checkouts directly in a folder, and Start records a folder he looked at as the machine's projects root, as `goblins install --projects-root` does.
Start starts any of them this machine has, since the fleet wakes all three: an agent that is not installed says so and holds Start back until another is picked, and so does a Claude Code the terminal cannot start itself (anything but `claude.exe`).
Start is refused, with the reason, while a CEO runs or is starting, for an agent it cannot start, and for a folder he looked at that is not a full path, cannot be read or holds no checkout; with no folder looked at it starts all the same. An example board (`goblins serve --example`) records the folder for itself alone, never as the machine's setting.
After Start the board shows at once with the CEO's terminal open. A quiet link, Open the board without a CEO, shows the board while none runs, so goblins at work stay in view, and the CEO bar then offers Start the CEO in place of its terminal icon, which leads back to the first-run page.
Keys pass through raw, the terminal follows the console's size, taken back with the next key after another viewer resized it, and Ctrl-] leaves it running, whether the console sends that key as a byte or as a Windows key event.
A host refuses to start for a terminal that already runs, so a second start never takes over the first one's record.
`goblins peek` of a native terminal reads its screen from its console, exactly as the terminal's program would read it, rather than rendering the terminal's output: the rows written, without trailing blanks.
`goblins peek gb-<id>`, the form fleet-view suggests, reads a native task's terminal as `goblins peek <id>` does.
`goblins fleet-view` reads a native task's current state from its own terminal, as the monitor does, never from Herdr: a turn on its screen is working, and an idle terminal whose host recorded itself under the task's id is the task's own, so the row shows the task's latest report; with no running host the row is unknown.
A Herdr task's idle pane still shows unknown, since no Herdr answer proves the pane is the task's.
The terminal's input waker, attached to its console from start to end, reads the window when the host asks, so a read is a message to a process already running, never a process of its own, and the host never attaches to the console, so a Ctrl-C typed to the terminal, or its console closing, never reaches the host.
A host started by an older goblins still starts its own program for every read, and an install leaves such a host running and puts the new build at that program's path, so every build still answers that read.
A read that fails is an error naming the terminal, never an empty screen.
`goblins spawn`, and the board's Start with it, starts every goblin in a native terminal of its own, named by its task id, except a Claude Code goblin its home puts on stream-json pipes, as below. A pi goblin starts with `--approve` where its pi advertises it, so it never asks to trust the folder's project files and saves no trust.
The harness starts as its own program: claude.exe itself, and codex and pi through `cmd /c`, since their npm shims are scripts, and an argument cmd would read as more than text is refused.
Each is the program the spawning process's `PATH` finds, where the spawn validated it, started by its path, so a stub harness first on a rig's `PATH` is the one that starts, never the one the terminal's `PATH`, the user's, would find, and a harness the spawning process cannot find is refused.
A shim whose path has a space in it starts by its short name, since cmd drops the opening quote of a quoted path once another argument is quoted too and runs the path's first word, and one on a volume that keeps no short names, such as a Dev Drive, is refused.
The terminal's environment starts from the one Windows gives a new process of the user, built from the user's and the machine's configured variables, never from the spawning process's own, so nothing the spawning session set reaches the goblin.
It names the spawning home as `GOBLINS_HOME` and `GOBLINS_STATE_OVERRIDE` all the same, and the projects root the spawner names, where it names one, as `GOBLINS_PROJECTS_ROOT`, so every `goblins` command the goblin runs acts on the home that started it even where the user's environment names another, as it does for a scratch home's or a second install's goblins.
The harness billing keys and every known session marker, such as `CLAUDECODE`, `CLAUDE_CODE_CHILD_SESSION` and the Herdr pane's variables, are dropped from it all the same, then the credentials of the services the task carries, which are the ones its brief names and no other ([Credentials by need](contract/authentication.md#credentials-by-need)), and the launch's variables, `GOBLINS_ROLE=goblin` among them, are added and win: a native task has no credentials script.
A Claude Code setting such as `CLAUDE_CODE_GIT_BASH_PATH` therefore reaches a native goblin only when it is configured for the user or the machine, not when only the spawning session sets it.
The one thing of the spawning process's own environment a terminal starts with is its codex guard: a task's `PATH` begins with its own guard folder, `state\codex-guard\<id>`, then the guard folders the spawning process runs under, which `GOBLINS_CODEX_GUARD` names, so a scratch home's goblin a rig in a goblin's terminal spawns keeps that goblin's guard ([Pausing and automatic resume](contract/dispatch.md#pausing-and-automatic-resume)).
The spawn reads the terminal's screen throughout and types only where it recognizes what it reads.
A startup dialog it knows is answered only once it has shown for a moment, by moving the focus down and checking each move on the screen before confirming: Claude's trust dialog, which focuses "No, exit" first, Codex's update prompt (Skip), trust prompt (Yes, or Trust and continue in Codex 0.160's folder trust prompt) and hook review (Continue without trusting, never Trust all), and the project trust prompt of a pi without `--approve` (Trust (this session only), never a trust pi saves).
Trusting a hook is the Overlord's decision, so a Codex goblin runs without the hooks its review listed, and the spawn reports them to the CEO as `goblins notify` does, with a working status and a wake naming the command hooks of `hooks.json` in `CODEX_HOME` and in the project's `.codex`; a hooks file it cannot read is named in that report, never a reason to stop, and `config.toml` `[hooks]` tables are not named.
A prompt a spawn may not answer, or a screen it does not recognize within the startup budget, stops the spawn with the terminal named and its screen quoted.
Codex's optional Daybreak security offer is drawn inline above the composer rather than in its place: over a composer that holds no draft a live one is dismissed with Escape and never with a choice, while a composer that holds one takes every key.
So every delivery, a spawn's, a `goblins send`, a wake and a board answer to the CEO, dismisses a live offer with Escape, waits a moment and reads it again before the key, and never chooses its option.
The rows a dismissed offer leaves on screen are history, not a dialog, to everything that reads a native screen: a ready composer under them is not held back, a wake left untyped never names the offer as its reason, and a harness update finds its stopping point under them (#1556).
The instruction is typed into the composer once the composer has stayed ready for two seconds with no startup dialog drawn over it and no live inline offer, submitted once the composer shows it, and the spawn succeeds only once the harness shows it working. A composer that proves it holds unsent text, a draft somebody left there, stops the spawn before anything is typed.
A new goblin can already be in a turn before its composer ever reads ready, as one a `goblins send` reached before its instruction was: once its status row has shown it working for as long as a composer must read ready, it has started, and its instruction is typed into that turn, which holds it for its next tool call, and counts as delivered once queued there.
An idle Codex can hold typed text undrawn and take it in slowly, so while the text does not show the terminal is widened by one column and set back to make it redraw, and the wait allows time for each character and keeps extending while its screen keeps changing, up to ten minutes, since a resumed Codex has taken text in at about a character a second; an instruction over 400 characters is written whole to the task's `instruction.md` and a one-line pointer to it is typed instead, on spawn, switch and resume.
Every harness is typed that one-line pointer, Claude Code and pi as well as Codex, for an instruction over 400 characters or of more than one line: a harness takes a long burst of typing as a paste, and Claude Code 2.1.29x hands its model a paste as a block with nothing written beside it, which a model does not take as the operator's own words.
Codex can also take an Enter as part of a paste, so an instruction to Codex is submitted with End and then Enter: End ends the paste, so the Enter after it submits.
That key is pressed once, since a loaded Codex can take it seconds late with the text still showing, and an Enter pressed again would reach it after the instruction as an empty submission.
A harness that still holds the instruction and nothing else in its composer a whole 90-second window after its key, with no turn and no dialog or list of choices asking over it, lost that key instead, as a resumed Codex goblin did on 2026-10-31 until the CEO pressed Enter by hand (#1873).
A spawn, switch or resume then presses the same key once more and gives it another window, unless Claude Code's once-more Enter for a key taken as a new line was already pressed, so beside the Enters a pi that is still starting turns away no harness gets more than one after its first; a `goblins send` does not, since its sender is told and sees the screen.
Claude Code can take its Enter as a new line on a loaded machine, as it did on 2026-10-20 when a resumed goblin's instruction sat in its composer over an empty row until the resume's window ran out, so Enter is pressed once more, no sooner than a second after the first, while the text still shows there over an empty row the composer did not end with as the Enter was pressed, and no turn has started.
Text that shows over no such row is also what a Claude Code that has not taken its Enter yet shows, as a loaded machine can leave it, and an Enter pressed again then would reach it after the instruction, so it gets none (#1232).
pi 0.85 draws its editor and takes typed text before its startup completes, which on a machine without fd and rg includes downloading them, and until then it puts a submitted instruction back in its editor and says "Startup is still in progress"; while it says so and did not before the submit, Enter is pressed again every second, for up to the two-minute startup budget, and the time pi has to show itself working counts from the last press.
Codex's empty composer shows its placeholder rather than the context left.
On Codex 0.160 its footer can use a multi-word model display name, as in `GPT-6.1 Terra xhigh · <folder> · <title>`; that footer is excluded from the composer while real unfinished drafts are preserved (`TestCodexDisplayNameFooterDoesNotBecomeAnUnsentDraft` in `internal/harness/screens_test.go`).
A spawn that fails while its terminal still runs keeps that terminal, with its task record and worktree, delivered or not, so the session stays addressable with `goblins peek` and can be recovered rather than duplicated, and the spawn's error says so; one whose terminal has ended closes it, which ends the harness and everything it started, and retires the task. A terminal that already ran under the task's id refuses the spawn's host and is left running.
If the terminal's host still runs but does not answer a close, the spawn's error says so, and the worktree and task record stay, so the task can still be reached.
A home whose `config/fleet.json` sets `claude_goblins_on_stream`, which is off unless the home sets it and which no release turns on, starts each Claude Code goblin, on a spawn, a switch or a resume, as `claude -p` on stream-json pipes in a stream host named by its task id, with no terminal (#2374).
Every other goblin runs in its terminal, and so does the one start right after a stream session of the task that ended by itself; once that terminal's host ends, the next start goes on stream-json pipes again.
Before it is given anything, the session is asked Claude Code's `initialize` and `get_context_usage`, which start no model turn, and it is refused unless it is signed in to a Claude plan, ran a SessionStart hook, included a skill and loaded each CLAUDE.md that exists in its worktree and in the user's Claude Code folder (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, else `.claude` in the profile), all of which a `--bare` session skips (`claudestream.ProveStart`).
It is refused too when any SessionStart hook reports an outcome other than success, as a goblin hook whose program cannot start does, since the supervisor would never learn of that session while the user's own hooks ran beside it.
When the stream host cannot start, the session is refused, or Claude Code does not report taking the instruction, the session is closed and the same start runs in the terminal, and why goes to the task's status log as a `working:` line and wakes the CEO, as `goblins notify` does.
Its instruction, whole, and every delivery to it are written as one user line with an id of its own, taken once Claude Code's own `command_lifecycle` report of that id says it started; a line still queued in a running turn is held for its next tool call, as one typed into a turn is, a delivery that may not reach a turn writes nothing, and nothing is ever left typed on a prompt line.
`goblins send --key Escape` or `Ctrl-C` sends Claude Code's interrupt request, which ends its turn, and Enter and Ctrl-U are refused, since it has no composer.
Closing it, as a switch or a cleanup does, closes Claude Code's input, and its host ends whatever lingers.
What reads a terminal's screen reads the session's state from its host instead: the monitor's sample, with the session's rows for the screen, `goblins peek`, which shows those rows, and the idle checks of `goblins cleanup`, an engine change and a gate run's end, which find it idle only while Claude Code waits for a line.
`goblins attach` refuses it and names `goblins switch <id> --terminal`, which takes it over: the goblin starts again on its own conversation in a terminal, with no fallback reported, and `goblins switch <id> --restart` hands it back to stream-json pipes.
The board's Send now, Edit and Cancel on a message it holds refuse it and say why.
The board's view of it reads the session's state from its host, with no terminal scrape (`streamView`): it is told `{"type":"stream"}`, that no history replays and that typing is held, so the box sends what he writes as a board message, which is delivered as a line.
Soon after each line Claude Code writes, the state is read again and told as the view's screen: working while a turn runs, Thinking or Writing as the reply's newest block does, and asking while a tool call waits for an answer.
Once the view claims a size, it is drawn the session's rows, under a header naming the take over, with every control character Claude Code's words hold left out.
Escape or Ctrl-C typed in the view, Ctrl-C in the box with nothing selected in it, and the box's Stop, which a stream session's view shows during a turn though typing is held, send the interrupt request, even while a gate holds the task, since an interrupt writes nothing into the branch; other typing is dropped.
The view closes with Claude Code's exit code once it ends, and as a terminal's view does once the task it was opened for is gone.
A `-p` session shows no workspace trust dialog and runs the worktree's hooks and MCP servers, as a terminal start does once it answers Claude Code's trust dialog Yes, I trust this folder, which every start of a goblin's worktree does: a stream goblin trusts what its terminal would, the worktree the fleet made for its task, and its proof refuses a session whose SessionStart hook did not run.
`goblins send` reaches a task by its id or `gb-<id>` through its own native terminal, or its stream session as above, and nothing else: text is typed into the composer and submitted once the composer shows it, as the spawn delivers its instruction, dismissing a live inline offer first so a message that begins with a digit is never taken as the offer's choice, and `--key` writes the key straight to the terminal.
A composer that proves it holds words never sent takes nothing, from `goblins send` or any board delivery, since what was typed would join them, and the send is refused saying so.
A task an older build recorded in Herdr and a name that is no task are refused by name with nothing typed, and `goblins peek` of either fails the same way, since it reads native terminals only.
Once delivered, a send leaves the board's message receipt; a send queued in a goblin's turn leaves its receipt with the supervisor, which shows it only once the goblin's record of the conversation shows the text taken.
Text typed into an idle harness is delivered once its own native hooks report it took a prompt after the submit (Claude Code's and Codex's `UserPromptSubmit`, Pi's `agent_start`, marked `prompt` in their events), or, where no hook has reported, once its screen shows it working when it was not working before.
Text sent while the harness is already in a turn is held by the harness, which hands it to its model at the next tool call, once the call running then ends, or as the turn ends if it calls no tool first: Claude Code 2.1.292 and Codex 0.160 did so live on 2026-10-06, and pi 0.85 documents it.
An ordinary delivery does not interrupt the turn: the text is typed and submitted once, and no Escape is pressed; a message he sends now presses the harness's send-now key once, as above.
A prompt hook proves nothing then, since Claude Code runs `UserPromptSubmit` when it queues the text, before the goblin has it, so the send is delivered only once the harness's own record of the conversation shows the text handed to its model: a `queued_command` attachment or a prompt in Claude Code's transcript for the goblin's worktree, a user message in the Codex rollout that names the worktree, or a user message in pi's session for it.
The record is searched for the words a reader sees, never for the text character for character (`monitor.TextDigest`): each run of whitespace counts as one space, since a harness wraps and trims what it takes, and a character that draws nothing, such as a word joiner or a zero-width space, counts for nothing, since a harness may drop it.
Claude Code 2.1.322 made each tab of a paste four spaces and dropped a word joiner as it took two of the Overlord's messages into the CEO's turn on 2026-11-12, so its record never held them character for character: both read Queued, then Not confirmed, and his Send again typed both a second time (#1915).
The record is read back as far as the delivery's submit, however much its harness wrote since (`HostProgress.Took`): the first look reads it from its end 4 MiB at a time, until one read holds entries that carry a time and none of them at or after the submit, and each later look reads only the entries the record gained, so a delivery watched for a day costs no more than its record's growth.
The look read only the record's last 4 MiB before, and the CEO's record had grown 10 MiB since it took two of those messages and 40 MiB since it took them the first time, so no look could find any of the four (#1915).
A delivery stored by a build that named text character for character is searched for by the words stored with it as typed, named as this build names them, beside the name stored with it (`Awaiting.digests`): it reads Sent once this build runs, for as long as it is still watched, 24 hours from its submit.
Shown there within five seconds, `goblins send` prints `sent <id>`; otherwise it prints `queued for <id>`, saying the text lands at the goblin's next tool call, and exits 0, since the text was typed once and must not be sent again; a board answer in that case reads sent and is settled as an answer to a goblin in a turn.
A credential stored for the project after a goblin started reaches it while its terminal's host runs, when its task carries the service that declares the name: its task's credential script is rewritten and the goblin is told, through its terminal, to re-source it.
A service granted to a running task with `goblins auth grant` reaches it the same way.
An MCP server granted with `goblins auth grant <id> --mcp <server>` does not: a harness reads its MCP servers when it starts, so the goblin is given it at its next terminal.
A service or an MCP server taken back with `goblins auth revoke` stays with the terminal running then until it ends. The task's credential script is rewritten without the service and no notice is sent, since loading that script takes no service's variable away, and its next terminal starts without either.
