On the board a run item is a card in the Command Center stack, counted in the header badge while it is ready or running.
The card shows why the CEO needs it, the shell with its mark (the GNU Bash mark for Git Bash, a terminal glyph for either PowerShell, since Simple Icons carries no PowerShell mark), an Admin badge with a shield when it runs elevated, the exact command in a terminal block, as [The Messages view](#the-messages-view) describes one, its lines as written with Copy and Full screen, and the folder it runs in.
One button runs it, enabled only while the item is ready and the board is connected, and it says where the command runs: **Run in PowerShell**, **Run in PowerShell 7** or **Run in Git Bash**, or **Run as administrator** with a note that Windows will ask to confirm.
Run turns the card into the command's own terminal, in place of the notice it once showed, as the Overlord asked on 2026-10-08 ("why can we not render terminal right in command center in place of the notification").
It is a native terminal the supervisor hosts the way it hosts a goblin's, drawn on the card by the board's own terminal view with a cursor and the keyboard: he types into it, sees its output as it comes, pastes, presses Ctrl+C, and a browser sign-in it opens works as it does in a window.
No window and no Windows Terminal tab opens for any item, interactive or not, in the browser board or the desktop window.
The card keeps the command's title, text and folder above the terminal and **Stop** under it, which ends the command and everything it started and reads Stopped.
On a phone the terminal fits the card and takes the phone's keyboard when he taps it; a phone with no keyboard of its own can still read it.
When the command exits, the card completes by itself, with no key to press and no window left waiting: **Complete** when it ended without trouble, which its exit code says whatever its last line of output says, or **Failed:** and the last line it printed, or why it never finished, such as its terminal closing first.
The card never shows the exit code; the inbox's history keeps it beside how the item ended, such as `Complete · exit 0`, and lists an item that expired or was withdrawn with its reason.
A command that completes or is stopped, or that expires or is withdrawn while its card is on screen, finishes the card as an answered item's does: its check with Complete or Stopped, or Expired or Withdrawn, then the next open item or You're all done.
A command that failed keeps its card on screen with what its terminal showed, which is trouble he may need to read, until he moves on; an Update item keeps its own card.
What its terminal showed reads as terminal text under the command, in the same block (#2524).
The block ends in Failed once the command failed, never with its exit code, a command that never exited included, such as one whose terminal closed first or whose Windows prompt was declined.
A goblin's wait on the Overlord himself (`goblins notify --waiting-on overlord`, a review item whose id is `waiting-<task>-<n>`) shows as a status card: who waits and what for, in the goblin's own words, and no answer box; it stays until he answers or clears it, the goblin withdraws it, its task is gone or the CEO answers it, whatever the goblin reports meanwhile.
Its words show once: a wait with a page opens it from the card's one Open review button and shows no page preview, which would repeat them and open the page a second time.
The goblin's words keep their lines and read formatted, never as raw markup: the first line heads the card, `**bold**` is bold, `- ` lines are bullets, a value in backticks is code with a copy button beside it, drawn as a link when the value is an address, and an address in the words is a link with no copy button.
Values the Overlord must enter somewhere, such as DNS records, go in a Markdown pipe table on the lines after the lead (a header row, a separator row of dashes, one row each), which the card shows as a table, each backticked value with its own copy button; `goblins notify`'s help shows this one way to send a table.
The Command Center's list rows, the pinned CEO bar, alerts and the Answer button read a wait on one line, without its table.
The card opens what the wait points at, as its main button, with Skip beside it, and the pencil too unless it opens a page: the page the goblin named with `--lavish` (Open review), else a file it delivered with `goblins deliver` (Open the file), else the link it gave with `--link` (Open the link); a wait that names none of these offers Skip and the pencil alone. Its Skip tells the goblin to go on without it.
A wait that names none of them and that its goblin declared to follow a decision (`goblins notify --follows <question-id>`) shows no card while that decision is open and closes with its answer ([Deliberate CEO questions](#deliberate-ceo-questions) says when a wait must declare).
A web address the goblin only names in its words is never offered as the link, and `--link` must be https (or plain http on this machine or the tailnet) with no credentials, query or fragment, checked before anything is recorded.
The board never opens a path from a wait's text, only the link the goblin gave or an item it already holds.
Every item runs its script with nothing between it and its terminal, so a sign-in, `goblins attach` or anything else that needs a real terminal works on the card; `--interactive`, which once asked for that, is gone.
A goblin hands him a command with `goblins notify <task-id> --waiting-on overlord "<why>" --run <file>` (or on a `--blocked` question): a `.ps1` file runs in Windows PowerShell and a `.sh` file in Git Bash, in the goblin's worktree, never elevated, and the file is read before anything is recorded.
A wait that carries a command is that run card alone, named for the goblin (`<goblin> asks you to run this`), with the goblin's words as its title; a question keeps its own card beside it.
A command can carry a message he sends himself, from `goblins run-request --message-file <path>` or a goblin's `--message-file` beside `--run`, which the run item holds as `mail` and its card shows under the command as a [step card](#step-cards) does; on a `--blocked` question with choices the message goes on the question's card instead.
The goblin's notify leaves the command in `state/goblin-runs-inbox`, and the supervisor takes from there only what can be a live goblin's own item: it names a task whose running goblin has that identity, its ID is `run-<task>-<notify>`, and it never asks for administrator rights; its folder is set to the goblin's worktree, whatever the record says, and anything else is refused, said once among the board's issues, and removed.
The CEO's own run items still reach the board only over the supervisor's pipe, and the folder they once came through stays unread.
When the command ends the goblin, not the CEO, is told that he ran it and its exit code; a goblin's command nobody ran is withdrawn once its goblin reports, after it, done, failed or a newer wait on him, is replaced or is gone, as its wait would be, and reads Withdrawn.
Before a command is published, by `goblins run-request` or `goblins notify --run`, its own shell parses it without running any of it, and a command that does not parse is refused with the line the shell names, since it would run nothing at his click.
A PowerShell command whose param block makes a parameter mandatory is refused too, naming it: Run starts the command with no arguments, so it would stop at PowerShell's prompt for the value, as the install card for main-6b7e7bc9 did on 2026-10-18.
A failed item's card says in one line why it failed and that the CEO, or the goblin that asked, has its output and takes the next step.
An item runs once and expires 24 hours after it was created; running it again needs a new item.
The registered CEO withdraws an item nobody ran with `goblins run-request --withdraw <id> --reason "<why>"`, over the same pipe and proof: the item reads Withdrawn by the CEO with the reason, leaves the Command Center for the inbox's history, and `state/runs.audit` records the withdrawal on its own line (when, the item, its script digest, `withdrawn` and the reason); Run on it is refused with that reason from then on.
Replacing an item is withdrawing it and publishing the new command under a new ID, and an item that already ran or expired cannot be withdrawn.
A connection repair the Overlord asked for from the Connectors page is the board's own item, not the CEO's, and cannot be withdrawn.
Nor can the terminal the Overlord opened for a credential request, which is the board's own item too.
Run starts the command in a terminal of exactly the shell the item names: Windows PowerShell 5.1, PowerShell 7 (`pwsh` on `PATH`) or Git Bash (the `bash.exe` beside Git for Windows' `git.exe`, never the WSL `bash.exe`); a shell that is not installed fails the item with the reason.
The supervisor starts `goblins host` for it as it does for a goblin, recorded under the item's own directory, `state/runs/<digest>/hosts/run.json`, apart from every goblin's and the CEO's, so nothing that counts, resumes or reaps goblins' terminals sees it.
The terminal runs a runner that starts the item's script in its own shell, attached to the terminal, writes the script's exit code to `exit.txt` when it exits, and then waits at most a minute: the supervisor reads what the terminal shows, keeps it as the item's output and closes the terminal, so it ends by itself even when nobody is watching.
When `goblins resume` (including `--harness`) or `goblins resume` with no task runs in a command card (`GOBLINS_HOST_ID=run`), it finishes CEO startup and goblin recovery, then returns without the final chooser or attaching the CEO's terminal.
The runner can then record completion while the replacement CEO keeps running; a CEO startup failure still returns a nonzero exit code.
A CEO restart or board reload preserves the run's output and outcome without rerunning the command, and an item already stopped without an exit receipt stays stopped rather than being assumed successful.
The card's view is `/api/terminal/native?run=<id>`, which takes the board's token and exact Origin like every terminal view, opens only while the command runs and closes when it ends; a credential request's terminal opens only on the board's own page on this PC.
**Stop** (`run_stop` in the board's action checks, naming only the item and its identity) reads what the terminal shows, records the item stopped and closes the terminal and everything in it; a command that finished meanwhile keeps its exit code.
An admin item asks Windows to start that host elevated and out of sight, through a hidden Windows PowerShell helper's `Start-Process -Verb RunAs -WindowStyle Hidden` on `goblins.exe`, so the consent prompt is Windows' own and names `goblins.exe`; until he confirms it, the card says to confirm the Windows prompt, and once Windows starts the host its elevated terminal runs on the card like any other.
A declined prompt ends the item failed with that reason.
The host's pipe carries a medium integrity label, so the unelevated board reaches the elevated host; its terminal's input then comes from the board, as the input of every terminal the board draws does.
What Windows makes impossible: the consent prompt itself cannot be drawn on the card, since Windows shows it on its own secure desktop, and on a standard account, where the prompt asks for an administrator's password, the elevated host runs as that other account, which cannot open this user's board, so its card shows no terminal while its command still runs and its exit code still comes back.
A terminal that closes before the command finishes, as a restart of the machine closes it, ends the item failed.
When the command finishes, its exit code and the end of its output are on the item: what its terminal showed, or for the Update item, which runs out of sight with no terminal, the last 64 KiB it printed; the CEO receives the result as the Overlord's answer, with the end of the output.
Every run appends a line to `state/runs.audit`: the time, the item's ID, the SHA-256 of exactly the script file that ran, and its exit code, or none when it did not finish.
Finished, expired and withdrawn items are pruned a week after they end; a waiting or running item is never dropped, and a new request is refused while all 64 held items are one or the other.
Output is stored on the board, what the terminal shows when the command ends included, typing that the command shows as it is typed among it, so the CEO never puts a secret in a run command or requests one that prints a secret or asks for one where it is shown.
Items that speak for the CEO (its questions, its own items with their withdrawals, every clear, files from `goblins deliver` without `--task`, and `goblins answer`) reach the board only over the supervisor's pipe, which proves the sender descends from the registered CEO process, like run items; the question, review and answer inboxes refuse any file that claims to be the CEO's and name it on the board.
Known limit: every goblin runs as the same Windows user as the CEO, and a process of that user can still spoof another goblin's items, including withdrawing them, since a goblin's identity is a hash of its task record, which any of them can read.
It can also spoof the CEO's live presentation notices (`goblins present` without `--task`), text typed into a goblin's terminal, including a line that starts with `CEO:`, and the wake queue and status files the CEO reads.
It can rewrite the supervisor's own database file (`state/.supervisor.json`) while `goblins serve` is stopped, and anything it runs as a descendant of the CEO's harness process is the CEO by this proof.
It can also debug or inject into the CEO process itself: the pipe closes the file inbox path and the pipe squat, not the same-user boundary.

## Update Code Goblins

A newer release of Code Goblins reaches the Overlord as an item of its own in the Command Center, the Update item, and only he presses it.
The supervisor reads the latest release of `fpresta0607/code-goblins` from GitHub's API when it starts and every six hours after, with the ETag of the answer it keeps in `state\update\release.json`, so an unchanged release costs nothing against GitHub's limit; a look that fails keeps what the last one found and says nothing.
`"check_for_updates": false` in `config\fleet.json` turns the look off, and an item still waiting then leaves with that reason.
`CODE_GOBLINS_RELEASE_API` points the look at a stub release server, whose host the release's page and files must then be on; an example board (`serve --example`) looks only when it is set.
A release newer than the build the board runs, compared by its `vMAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` tag part by part, gets one item, a run item of its own kind, which the board makes itself and the CEO cannot withdraw.
Its card wears the electric ring instead of the glass border, with the release goblin in place of an asker: Code Goblins, when the release was published, a New version chip, its state, Update Code Goblins as its title, the version the board runs and the new one in the display face, up to three lines of what is new, read from the release notes' What's Changed list without who made each change, a What's new link to the notes, and a line on what the update checks: an unsigned release says so and that the update installs it only when each file matches the release's SHA-256, naming the one its notes list for `goblins.exe`, and a signed one names its publisher.
It shows no command, and its one button, **Update**, sends the item's ID and identity like Run, through the same action checks.
The supervisor then also proves the request comes from a board of the Overlord's own, by the proof of [AFK mode](#afk-mode)'s switch on the board, and refuses anything else, the CEO's or an agent's browser included.
A board it refuses shows one short sentence that says where to press Update, and the CEO is told what the supervisor found in a `review` wake keyed `update`, which opens with `Updating Code Goblins is the Supreme Overlord's alone`.
Nothing presses it for him, in AFK mode or out of it, and while AFK mode is on it is held for him and announced to no one.
Pressed, the supervisor writes a grant for exactly that item and release to `state\update\grant.json` and runs the item's command out of sight, `goblins update --to <tag> --run <item>` with the home named, which takes the grant once, within two minutes, in place of a terminal of his own.
The card follows it from what it prints, read each second from `GET /api/runs/<id>/output` while the page shows, never while it is hidden in the tray or a background tab, and at once when it shows again: Download, Check each file's SHA-256, Restart the board on the new version and Bring the CEO's contract and skills up to date, each done, under way or failed, with its output under Output.
The board is away for under a minute while the update restarts it, the card says so, and the supervisor that comes back finishes the item.
The update is bounded, so the card never shows it under way without end: past a minute with the board away it is stopped where it is and the previous build put back, and past a minute before it changed anything it is stopped with nothing changed.
It ends Updated, and the page reloads on the new board after a moment; Rolled back, when the new build was not installed and the previous one serves again, from its kept copy included; or Not updated, when nothing was changed, such as a download that failed its checksum, or the board did not come back.
Either way the card ends on the one sentence the update ended on, with nothing to paste and no command to run, and what the update printed stays under Output for whoever wants why and how long each step took.
An update that did not install has the board look again at once, and a new item for the same release follows, which the card's one **Try again** shows and starts in the one press.
That next update puts the previous build back first when the last one stopped part way, so no recovery is ever his to run.
Recovery by hand stays for when nothing else can: with `GOBLINS_HOME` set to the home and `GOBLINS_STATE_OVERRIDE` to its `state` folder, `state\update\candidate.exe update --recover` puts the previous build back from any folder, with both commands gone.
A newer release published meanwhile replaces a waiting item, which reads Replaced with why, as does one whose release the board now runs; an item waits for him until then and never expires after a day as a command does.
The Command Center lists it as Code Goblins with the release goblin, `Update to <new> from <old>`, a download icon and the same ring, the bar's alert announces it once as Code Goblins, and History keeps how it ended.
The item is the release's one signal on the board: no banner points to it, since everything that asks him something reaches him in the Command Center.
An update or an install that moved a home from a release before the rename to goblins' names is followed by one notice on the board with what changed, which terminals restart under the new names, each only once idle with nothing typed on its prompt line, and the way back, `goblins cutover --undo '<folder>'`, which Dismiss hides once nothing waits to restart ([Updating from a release before the rename](https://github.com/fpresta0607/code-goblins/blob/main/docs/install.md#updating-from-a-release-before-the-rename)).
A board built from a clone, whose version is not a release's, gets no item: its clone updates it, with `git pull` and `.\install.cmd -Dev`.
The item runs the home's own `goblins.exe` where the home keeps it, in `bin`, or at the root of a home a build before `bin` set up.
The desktop window follows the update beside `goblins.exe`, and one in the tray moves onto it by itself: nothing unsent, no answer in progress, and in the tray.
A window he has open stays put, however long it goes untouched, until he closes it to the tray: the move ends the window and opens another, and on 2026-10-09 his window was replaced that way ten minutes after an update, the time a shown window then had to go untouched before it was moved.
The page asks `POST /api/window/move` once for each connection, the moment the window is in the tray: as the page hears it is hidden, or as it connects while hidden, with no timer looking again (#1598); and the supervisor, once it has proven the page is shown by his own window as the AFK switch proves it, moves only a window that runs a renamed copy beside the home's `goblins-window.exe`: it ends that window and opens the home's program through the desktop shell, so the new window starts with the desktop's environment and none of the supervisor's.
The shell that opens it then exits, which leaves the new window with no parent at the desktop, and the board takes it as his by the program it runs.
A window moved from the tray starts in the tray, which `state/window-move.json` tells it.

## Credential requests

When the CEO or a goblin needs a secret the project's scope does not hold, such as `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`, it asks for it by name and the Overlord pastes the value on the board; nobody asks for a value in chat or puts one in a brief:

```powershell
goblins auth request --project acme-shop --why "Charge test cards in the checkout tests" --link https://dashboard.stripe.com/test/apikeys --step "Sign in to the shop's Stripe account" --step "Reveal the test secret key and copy it" STRIPE_TEST_SECRET_KEY
goblins auth request --project acme-shop --task add-billing --why "Check webhook signatures" STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET
goblins auth request --project acme-shop --task add-billing --grant stripe --why "Check webhook signatures" --wait
```

A request takes names only, at most ten, each an environment variable name, with one line saying what they are for and optionally the https page they come from, with no credentials, query or fragment in its link.
It may also give up to eight steps for getting the values on that page, each one line of at most 200 characters, one `--step` each, in order.
Anything shaped like a value is refused before anything is filed: a known key prefix (such as `sk_`, `rk_`, `ghp_`, `xox`, `AKIA` or `eyJ`), an equals sign, or long random text, in a name, the reason, a step or the link, and no refusal repeats what it refused.
The registered CEO files over the supervisor's pipe, proven the way run items prove it; a goblin files for its own task with `--task <its id>`, from its own terminal, proven the way its questions are, through `state/credential-requests-inbox`, and a request in that inbox that claims to be the CEO's is refused and named among the board's issues.
The board holds each request in `snapshot.credentials`: its ID, a generation minted when the board took it, who asked and for which task, the project, the names, the reason, link and steps, the names the scope already holds, each name's format hint, and its state, open, saved, done, dismissed, withdrawn or expired.
It also says where each value goes: the repository, which is the checkout the scope is named for when it is on this machine, the credential scope, and every service of the project's `auth.json` that reads the name, beside the project's goblins, whose `auth.ps1` carries the whole scope.
It never holds a value.
A project's `auth.json` may declare format hints, which the request carries for the card to warn with and never to refuse a value:

```json
"formats": [
  {"names": ["STRIPE_*"], "prefixes": ["sk_", "rk_"], "warn": [{"prefix": "sk_live_", "say": "A live secret key can do anything on the account; use a restricted rk_live_ key."}]}
]
```

A name in `names` is exact or ends in `*` to match every name starting with what comes before it, and the first matching format applies.

A request may also name a local env file with `--env-file <file>`, such as `.env.docker.local` for a project's docker compose setup: each saved value then also sets its `NAME=` line in that file, beside the credential scope.
The file must be an env file name (`.env`, `.env.<name>` or `<name>.env`) at the root of the project's main checkout, never a goblin's worktree; a plain file or one not made yet; ignored by git (`git check-ignore`) and not tracked (`git ls-files`), so a value written there can never be committed.
`goblins auth request` checks it before filing, the board checks it when it takes the request and again before each write, and a goblin's request may name an env file only in its own task's checkout.
The board rewrites the file in place and never replaces it, because a goblin worktree an older build made shares a project's env files as hardlinks to the same file, while one made since holds its own read-only copy, which keeps the value it was made with: every line that sets the name changes, keeping an `export` prefix, and nothing else does; it never writes through a link, and a file swapped for one between the check and the write is refused.
A value is written as it is when it is plain and in single quotes otherwise, which docker compose and the board's own env reader both read literally; a value with a single quote, a line break or another control character stays in the scope only, and the card and the CEO say why.
A value typed in the card's terminal reaches the file the same way, read back from the scope for each name the terminal provably stored.

The save is `POST /api/credentials/save` with the request's ID and generation, a value for each name it stores, which from the card is one row's name, and the names he confirmed replacing.
It is the one board endpoint that takes a secret value.
Every other endpoint that changes anything still refuses one, as the connection repairs always have: each reads a fixed request shape and refuses a body that carries a value, secret, password, token or credential field, as text, an object or a list, before it reads anything else, and `internal/supervisor/value_guard_test.go` reads the board's route table and fails the moment any other endpoint starts reading such a field.
The save is taken only from the board's own page on this machine: its Host must name 127.0.0.1, localhost or ::1 with the board's port, its peer must be this machine, and any forwarding or Tailscale identity header (`X-Forwarded-*`, `Forwarded`, `Via`, `X-Real-Ip` or any `Tailscale-*` header) refuses it, so a board shared through `tailscale serve`, which reaches the same port on this machine, never takes a value.
It also passes every check other actions pass: POST only, the exact Host and Origin, and the per-session `X-CEO-Token`.
It must name an open request and its current generation, and only names that request asks for and has not stored yet, each with a non-empty value of one line and at most 16 KiB.
Each value must also have the shape of the credential its name names, the check `goblins auth store` makes too (`auth.ValueProblem`): no control character, at least four characters, at least eight for a name ending in KEY, SECRET or TOKEN, and for a name that says its kind, the prefix that kind starts with and at least 20 characters.
The kinds a name says are Stripe secret and restricted keys (`sk_` or `rk_`, with `test_` or `live_` when the name says TEST or LIVE), Stripe publishable keys (`pk_`), Stripe webhook secrets (`whsec_`), GitHub tokens (`ghp_`, `github_pat_`, `gho_`, `ghu_` or `ghs_`), and OpenRouter (`sk-or-`), Anthropic (`sk-ant-`), OpenAI (`sk-`) and Resend (`re_`) keys.
A refusal names the name and why, never any of the value, and ends `Nothing was stored.`: the Overlord's `STRIPE_TEST_SECRET_KEY` once arrived as the single Ctrl+V character a paste with nothing to paste types into a terminal, and still showed stored.
Each name takes one save: a save of a name the request already stored is refused.
A request closes as saved once every name it asks for is in the scope, stored by its saves or held before, so it takes its names one row at a time, and a request nobody finishes expires after 24 hours, when the CEO is told the names still unsaved so it can ask again.
While the request's terminal is open, its save is refused and it does not expire; it expires on the first check after the terminal ends.
A name the project's scope already holds is replaced only when the save confirms it; otherwise the save is refused naming the names to confirm, and nothing is stored.
Every refusal stores nothing and leaves the request open.
The board holds at most 64 requests, never drops an open one to make room, and prunes a closed one a week after it closed.

A taken save goes straight into the project's scope of the credential store through the same store `goblins auth store` writes, Windows Credential Manager or the file store.
For a card naming a task, a secret saved on the board or typed in its terminal also grants the service that declares that name to the card's recorded task generation, through the same grant path as `goblins auth grant`.
Each saved name must identify exactly one service, unchanged since filing, and the task must still belong to the card's project and generation; otherwise the value stays in the vault and the card's reason and CEO notice say why no grant was made.
These task saves and terminal opens require the Overlord's own board, as connector grants do, and an automated key fetch grants nothing.
No other task gains a service.
The request closes before the board answers, and then the board runs `goblins auth store`'s own refresh: each live goblin of the project gets its `auth.ps1` regenerated from the store, for the services its task carries and no other, and the one-line re-source notice, and the CEO gets a `review` wake naming the names stored, the project and the goblins told.
A saved value reaches goblins only through their `auth.ps1` environment, never their context: that script is an owner-only file under `state/tasktmp/<task>/`, which `goblins cleanup` deletes before it archives the task (refusing to archive one it cannot delete), and the notice names the script, never the value.
A resume or a switch removes the script too: the goblin's new terminal starts with what its task carries in its environment, and a script written for the last one could hold more.
Beside that, and the env file a request names, the value is nowhere else: not in `state/` otherwise, the inboxes, the board's snapshot or event stream, its logs, telemetry, a wake, an error message or the save's answer, which names names and states only.
A save's body is never logged, and a panic while one is handled answers with a fixed message and logs nothing of it.

When a task needs a service already declared by its project, the goblin or registered CEO can file `goblins auth request --project <project> --task <task> --grant <service> --why "<reason>" --wait`.
This card carries the task, service and one-line reason, with Grant and Deny and no value inputs or browser steps.
Filing it grants nothing.
Grant posts to `/api/connections/grant`, naming the card's ID and generation as `request` and `request_generation` beside the task generation and service; the existing owner-board check applies on the PC.
His private board grants it too, as he decided on 2026-10-29 ("Allow it as is"), knowing the gate proves that board his only by Tailscale's headers, which a program on this PC can forge: a grant through the gate is recorded as from `his own private board (by Tailscale's headers, which a program on this PC can forge)`.
Only a service grant card's Grant moves to the private board: granting or revoking a connector from the Connectors page, typing values and opening a browser sign-in window stay on the PC.
The card closes as `granted` only after the grant lands, the task is told to re-source, and the CEO gets the existing grant wake.
Deny, withdrawal, expiry, a stale task or card generation, a different project or a missing service declaration grants nothing.

The terminal fallback is `goblins auth store --project <project> <NAME>` on this machine: with no value argument it reads the value from stdin, and a value typed at a console is read without being shown, and its stored line then shows no part of it, not even the redacted shape.
It refuses a value of the wrong shape with exit code 2, saying why and storing nothing, by the same check as the save.
`POST /api/credentials/terminal` with the request's ID and generation opens that fallback for the Overlord: a PowerShell terminal on the request's card, a run item the board makes itself, that runs `goblins auth store` for each name the request does not have yet that the scope does not hold, and for each name the scope holds that the request confirms replacing, so he types or pastes each value there.
A value `goblins auth store` refuses is asked for again, three times at most, and a third refusal ends the terminal with that name unstored.
His keys travel from the board's own page on this PC through the supervisor to that terminal, as they do into every terminal the board draws, and are stored nowhere: `goblins auth store` does not show them, so the screen the item keeps when it ends holds none of them.
It takes the save's checks (this machine only, the open request and its generation), its terminal's view opens only on the board's own page on this PC, and it opens one terminal at a time per request; with nothing left to type but names the scope holds, it is refused naming them to confirm.
When the terminal finishes, the board checks each row it provably stored, a name the scope did not hold before and holds now, or every name it ran for when it finished cleanly, and tells the CEO the names; `goblins auth store` refreshed the project's goblins itself.
A clean finish closes the request as saved, like a save; a terminal that failed or was stopped leaves it open with what it stored recorded, unless every name is stored.

On the board each open request is a card in the Command Center, counted under Open Command Center and eligible for a Windows notification only while the tab is hidden or the window is minimized.
The card says who asks, and its header says what the values are for and how to get them: the page to open, then each step, numbered in order, each starting with a capital.
Below it a table has two columns, Name and Value, and nothing else, so it fits the Overlord's window and a phone's width with no sideways scrolling: a long name wraps after an underscore and a field narrows.
Each row is a name and a hidden field to paste its value into, with a mark at the field's end: an empty ring until the value is stored, a lock that stores a typed value, then a check beside Stored.
A muted line above the table says where the values go, the project's vault for its goblins and the env file the request names, and a muted line under a name names each `auth.json` service that also reads it.
A stored row's check says Stored, or Replaced, with how in muted words beside it: pasted on the card, typed in the terminal or fetched from the page.
A field never holds its value: what is pasted or typed is taken from the edit before it reaches the field and kept by the card in the page's memory, and the field is given one dot for each character, so copying out of it copies dots.
Pasting those dots back into a field is refused: the field stays as it was and a note under it says to copy the value again from where it came from.
It is a plain text field with no name or id and with autocomplete and spell check off, not a password field, because a browser keeps, or offers to keep, what a field held.
Edge and Chrome offer to save what a password field held, whatever its autocomplete says: with one, Edge showed **Save your password?** after a save on the card and named the Microsoft account it would go to.
Edge also keeps what any other text field held, even one drawn as dots with autocomplete off, in its profile's `Web Data`.
With only dots in the field, neither browser offers anything or stores anything.
Nothing blocks pasting into a field, and its value is in neither the field nor the page's markup.
A paste stores its row at once, and a typed value stores when he presses Enter or the row's lock: the card sends it once from its memory, in a save of that one name.
A row's field empties once its save is answered, taken or refused, except while the card asks **Replace a stored credential?** about a name the scope holds: **Cancel** sends nothing and keeps the pasted value, and **Replace and save** sends the same value with that name confirmed.
A refused value's reason shows under its row, which stays open with no check.
A value that does not start the way its format hint expects, or starts the way a hint warns about, shows the hint's words under its field, muted, after a warning icon: a paste of it waits for his Enter or the lock instead of storing at once, and either still stores it.
Below the table the exact `goblins auth store` lines have **Copy** and **Run**: Run opens the terminal for the names the scope does not hold, and asks the same question before a terminal that would type a stored one; while that terminal is open it runs on the card, where he types each value, and Save and Run wait for it to end.
A board opened from anywhere but 127.0.0.1, localhost or ::1, such as through `tailscale serve`, shows the card read-only: no field, no Save and no Run, only the commands to copy, which a page without the clipboard selects for Ctrl+C.
A request that closes as saved while its card is open finishes as every other Command Center item does, whether its values were pasted on the card or typed in its terminal: the card gives way to **Stored** with the names saved, and the next item follows by itself, so no card is left whose only way on is Skip.
History lists it as saved or expired, or as a [step card](#step-cards) closed another way.

`tests/acceptance/credential_canary_windows.ps1 -Binary <goblins.exe>` proves the path end to end on a scratch home, with a random canary under a throwaway credential scope it deletes at its end.
A stand-in goblin files the request from its own terminal, a browser on a throwaway profile enters the canary on the real card, and the proof counts the files that hold it: none in the browser's profile, `state/`, `data/`, the logs, the snapshot, the event stream or the agent transcripts it is given, and one, the goblin's owner-only `auth.ps1`, which `goblins cleanup` then removes.
A fresh browser profile is not isolation: Edge signs a new profile in to the Windows account by itself and syncs it, which pulls that account's saved entries into the profile and sends up what is typed.
So the proof starts the browser with sync and automatic sign-in switched off on a profile that disallows sign-in, reads the browser's own sign-in state before it opens the board, and fails with nothing typed if an account shows or the state cannot be read; at the end it checks that the profile stayed signed out and holds no saved form entry it did not type.
It also fills a plain sign-in form with values it makes up, which a browser does keep and does offer to save, so a clean result cannot come from a search or a probe that sees nothing.
`-Visible` runs it in a window, where a browser shows its offer to save a password, and checks that none follows the card's save; `-Browser` names another browser, such as Chrome.

### Step cards

A credential request is also the one card for any step a goblin's browser or computer-use work reaches that only the Overlord can take, such as creating an account, a sign-in, a CAPTCHA, accepting terms or a token to copy:

```powershell
goblins auth request --project acme-shop --task shop-signup --why "Create the shop's test account" --link https://shop.example.com/signup --step "Prove you are a human" --step "Accept the terms" --wait
goblins auth request --project acme-shop --task shop-signup --why "Connect the tests to the new account" --link https://shop.example.com/settings/api --plain ACCOUNT_EMAIL --label ACCOUNT_EMAIL="The account's email" --label SHOP_API_TOKEN="The API token from Settings" SHOP_API_TOKEN --wait
goblins auth request --project acme-shop --task shop-signup --why "Sign in to the shop in my browser" --link https://shop.example.com/login --window --wait
```

- A secret NAME goes into the vault as above, and a `--plain` NAME is a value that goes back to the asker: the card gives it a plain field, refuses a key in it by the rules of [A browser step only he can take](contract/authentication.md#a-browser-step-only-he-can-take), sends his word that a value is no secret as `not_secret` in the save where a refusal offers it, keeps the value in the board's record for the asker's wait and never in the snapshot, which names it under `received` once it arrived.
- Each input may carry a label of a few words, at most 60 characters, which the card shows above its name and its heading uses.
- A card that asks for no input has a Done button, `POST /api/credentials/close` with `"action": "done"`, and its heading is what it is for.
- `--message-file <file>` puts a message he sends himself on the card, such as an email, read once when the card is filed: the file's optional `To:` line (an email address, or an https page with no credentials, query or fragment) and `Subject:` line, a blank line, then a body of at most 4000 characters.
  A word of the subject or body shaped like a key is refused without being repeated, as in a step, and an assignment is judged by its value.
  The record carries it as `mail`, with `to`, `subject` and `body`, and the card shows the address with its copy button or the page as a link, the subject, and the body in a box, with **Copy**, which copies the body exactly, and **Copy subject** when it has one.
  A card whose step is only sending that message needs no page, step or input.
- A goblin's step card is its wait on the Overlord: a `goblins notify --waiting-on overlord` without a page while a card the goblin filed itself is open opens no second item, and the notify names the card and tells the goblin to wait on it in the background with `goblins auth request --wait-on <card> --task <id>`, which ends when the card closes.
  The board reads the goblin as waiting until it reports again, as for a wait on a run card.
  Done on the card, his or the goblin's, its inputs arriving, or his answer from Something else?, also closes as answered each plain wait of that goblin opened while the card was open, so nothing asks him for the step again; a wait opened before the card, or on a page, stays its own item.
- `--window`, which needs `--wait` and the asker's own browser session on the board, gives the card **Take control**, `POST /api/credentials/window`, taken only on this PC.
  The supervisor opens the card's link in the goblin's own browser (see [A goblin's own browser](#a-goblins-own-browser)), on the page it had open or a new one, gives him control of it, and the card shows that browser's Screen view, so no window opens on the desktop.
  A browser that could not open offers the button again, saying why, as does a card whose browser closed before the card did, because Chrome exited, the browser went unused or the supervisor restarted.
  His **Hand back** closes the card as done with his words, which the goblin's wait prints after its Done line, and any other close of the card gives the browser back to the goblin too.
  Words that hold one shaped like a key are never passed on, as his answer on a card is not: the card then says only that the step is taken.
- Every open credential and step card has Skip and the pencil beside its decision buttons. Skip is `POST /api/credentials/close` with `"action": "dismiss"`, from any board, and the pencil's Send is the same with `"action": "answer"` and his `"text"`, which closes the card as answered, refuses words shaped like a key, tells the goblin his answer and ends its wait with exit 4, printing `he answered instead: <his words>`. The card closes as dismissed, the goblin that asked is told in its terminal to go on without it, and the CEO gets a `review` wake.
- The card's place, and its History row, then show one line: the `goblins auth store` command to give a secret it still asks for later, or, for a card with none, `goblins auth request --reopen <card>`. That command, run by the CEO or the asking goblin, brings the card back open under a new generation for the rest of its day, and the goblin is told to wait on it again.

`--wait` waits on the card by looking at the board's record five times a second, reading it whenever it changed, and ends the moment the card closes: exit 0 once every input arrived or the step is done, printing each plain value as `NAME=value` and each secret by its name alone, exit 3 when he closed it, and 1 when it was withdrawn or expired.
A card the board refused, neither on the board nor in its inbox, ends the wait at once.
The asker closes its own card with `--done <card>` once it sees the step done and takes it back with `--withdraw <card> --reason "<why>"`, both over the supervisor's pipe from its own terminal, and the CEO may withdraw any card.
A goblin's open card is withdrawn once its goblin is gone or was replaced, or reported done or failed after it asked.
A card closed as done, dismissed or withdrawn takes no value, and History lists it so.

### A goblin's own browser

Each goblin's browser session drives a Chrome the supervisor starts for it, headless, 1280 by 800 pixels, on a profile in the task's folder, so a sign-in made there stays with the task.
Spawn points the session at it: `CHROME_DEVTOOLS_AXI_BROWSER_URL` is `ws://<board>/api/browser/devtools?generation=<generation>&task=<id>`, which the supervisor answers only for the task's current session, only from a process of this PC's own Windows user, and never from a web page, whose WebSocket always names its origin.
The Chrome starts on the session's first command, closes two minutes after nothing uses it and once its goblin ends, starts again on the next use if it exited, and has no debugging port: its DevTools are on a private pipe, as a login's are.
While it runs, the snapshot's task carries `browser: true`, the goblin's card shows **Browser active** as a button that opens `/?screen=<task>`, its Screen view full size in a tab of its own, and its panel offers **Screen** beside Chat and Task.
A browser that closes, or a session with none, gives the panel's Screen view way to Chat, which stays when a browser starts again, so only his pick, from the selector or a chat row's Open screen, shows the Screen view (#2362).

The Screen view is `GET /api/screen?task=<id>&generation=<generation>&token=<instance>`, a WebSocket from the board's own origin.
It shows the page the goblin or a sign-in opened or sent somewhere last, a blank page only when every page is blank, by its title and host alone, never its address, which can carry a sign-in's code.
A title that reads as an address, with no space and a scheme's :// or a host followed by a path, query or fragment, as Chrome gives a page with no title of its own or one whose title has not drawn yet, shows as untitled, by its host alone.
It sends the page's screencast as JPEG frames, each only once the view drew the one before, so a slow board is sent no more than it draws.
A view streams only while it is on screen in a visible tab: one switched away from, scrolled out of sight, in a closed Command Center or in a hidden tab closes its socket, and the supervisor stops the page's screencast as it closes, so no frame is made or sent for nobody.
At most 16 views are open at once; another is closed with "Too many screens are open."
On a small screen (40rem or less) the screen's box takes the page's own shape at its full width, so the page never sits small between two bands, and a step card's Screen view draws Take control and Hand back as the Screen tab does (CEO decision 1019951).

**Take control**, from the board on this PC only, makes him the browser's driver: each click, wheel turn, key and paste on the view's screen goes to the page through DevTools' Input domain, and while he drives the goblin's own commands are refused with "A person is driving this browser from the Code Goblins board. Wait until they hand it back, then try again.", except those that only set up a page the session attaches to.
Nothing typed on the screen is logged, kept or shown by the board.
While the screen has the keyboard, F6 gives it back to the board.
**Hand back**, from any view, gives the browser back to the goblin with a few words of what he did, at most 300 characters, as a paste is at most 4000, each counted in characters whatever bytes they take.
Control that no view shows for 30 minutes goes back to the goblin by itself.

## Browser key fetching

A key a reviewed key page shows can be fetched by the fleet instead of pasted: Go code in the supervisor reads it from the page and stores it straight into the project's vault, and no agent ever sees it.
When the page asks for a sign-in, the Overlord signs in himself, in a window of its own that opens only on his click.

### Logins

A login is one provider account's kept browser session: its own Chrome profile under `state\browser-logins\<id>`, readable by his Windows user alone and never copied into a worktree.
What is kept is the session, never a password, a code or a magic link.
Chrome itself encrypts the profile's cookies with a key Windows keeps for his user.
The registry, `state\browser-logins.json`, records each login's provider, the account's name, the account ID his last sign-in showed, the projects that use it, when he last signed in, when a browser the broker lent or a fetch started on it last closed, and the Chrome the broker runs on it now.

A project names the logins its goblins use in its `auth.json`, at most one for each provider:

```json
"logins": [
  {"provider": "stripe", "account": "PrecisionDocs", "account_id": "acct_1ABC", "signs_out_after_fetch": false}
]
```

`provider` is the ID of the recipe the login fetches with, `account` is the name he reads (ASCII letters, digits, spaces, dots, dashes and underscores, starting with a letter or digit, at most 64), and `account_id`, when set, is the account a fetch reads keys on, whatever account his sign-in landed on.
Projects that name the same provider and account share one login, so he signs in once for them all.
A project that names no login for a provider gets one of its own, named for the project, which no other project reaches: a borrow, a fetch or a sign-in card for a project that names its account is refused until the project that owns it names it too.
`signs_out_after_fetch` deletes the login's profile and records it signed out once the browser each fetch read in has closed, a goblin's lent browser included, so the next fetch brings his sign-in card.

### Recipes

A recipe in `config/key-pages.json` says, for one provider, the hosts a browser on its login may reach, the key page, the address pattern that means signed in, the address pattern whose one group is the account ID, and each key it reads by the accessible names a person sees.
Stripe's is the first:

```json
{"id": "stripe", "name": "Stripe", "key_page": "https://dashboard.stripe.com/test/apikeys",
 "signed_in_path": "^/(acct_[a-zA-Z0-9]+/)?test/apikeys/?$", "account_path": "^/(acct_[a-zA-Z0-9]+)/test/apikeys/?$",
 "hosts": ["stripe.com", "*.stripe.com", "stripe.network", "*.stripe.network", "stripecdn.com", "*.stripecdn.com"],
 "keys": [{"name": "STRIPE_TEST_PUBLISHABLE_KEY", "row": "Publishable key", "prefix": "pk_test_"},
          {"name": "STRIPE_TEST_SECRET_KEY", "row": "Secret key", "reveal": "Reveal test key", "prefix": "sk_test_"}]}
```

Recipes live in the repository, so each one is reviewed before it runs, and a fetch runs only from a recipe the binary carries.
A recipe is refused when its key page is not plain https on one of its hosts, when a pattern is not anchored, when a control is named Roll, Delete, Revoke or Create, when a key is live (a LIVE name or a live prefix), or when its keys have no account path.
A browser is lent and a sign-in card filed only for a provider a recipe names, so a new provider's recipe starts with its hosts, key page and signed-in pattern, by pull request.
With that merged, a Claude Code goblin can borrow the signed-in browser to explore the key page while the keys are masked, and add the keys it reads by another pull request.

### The broker

The supervisor's browser broker starts each login's Chrome, one at a time per login, since Chrome opens a profile once, and only when free memory is above the fleet's floor.
A goblin's Chrome is headless and reaches only its recipe's hosts: every other name resolves to nothing.
Chrome is never given a debugging port: it inherits two private pipes, and the broker serves DevTools on loopback only to a connection it proves comes from the same Windows user, with Host and Origin checked.
A task borrows a login's browser with `goblins auth browser <provider> --task <id>` from its own terminal, which points the task's chrome-devtools-axi session at it, and gives it back with `--release`.
The broker closes it 30 minutes after it was last lent, since asking again renews it, and once the task ends or loses the provider's service.
Only a Claude Code task borrows one, since only its hook can seal browser reads, and the task must carry the provider's declared service.
The handoff refuses unless `chrome-devtools-axi security --json` reports the `windows-user-v1` bridge transport, so no login's browser reaches a bridge another Windows user could drive.

### The sign-in card

A sign-in card asks him to sign in to one login.
A goblin files one with `goblins auth browser <provider> --task <id> --sign-in`, and a fetch files one when the page asks for a sign-in.
The card names who needs it, the provider and the account, and numbered steps, and has one button, **Open the sign-in window**.
Only his click on the board on this PC opens anything: a normal Chrome window on the login's profile, at the provider's key page, with no host rules, so he signs in as he would anywhere, with a password, a passkey or a code from his phone.
Nobody else types anything in it, and the fleet never sees what he does.
The broker reads only whether the page's address has reached the recipe's signed-in pattern, and which account it names, never the page.
Once it has, the window closes, the login records the account and the time, and the card says **Signed in** and moves on like every other command.
If he closes the window first, the card keeps its button.
A card nobody has clicked expires after a day, and his first click shortens that to 30 minutes from that click.
A card that expires, or whose task left, was replaced or lost its grant, closes its window.

### Fetching

`goblins auth fetch <request> --task <id>` has the supervisor fetch the keys an open credential request still needs from the request's link, when a recipe reads that page.
The request must be the task's own, or of no task in the task's project, and the task must carry the provider's declared service.
A Codex or pi goblin can fetch too, since the read happens inside the supervisor.
The fetch reads on the account the project names, or else on the one his last sign-in showed, and refuses when there is neither.
It uses the browser the task holds, sealing it first, or starts a headless Chrome of its own and closes it after.
A browser another task holds is refused.
In a tab of its own it loads the account's key page and waits for the signed-in address on that account: a page that settles elsewhere asks for a sign-in, and one on another account is refused before anything is read.
For each key it waits for the key's row, clicks the recipe's Reveal control with a real mouse click, and reads the key from beside the row's name.
A key that stays masked, two different keys beside one row, two Reveal controls or a missing row stop the fetch, and so does a value that fails the key's shape check, holding dots, an ellipsis, bullets or whitespace.
It then loads a blank page and closes its tab, so no key is left on screen.
The keys are stored through the request card's own save, which refreshes the project's goblins and wakes the CEO with the names, and each stored row says **fetched from the page** beside its Stored.
The command, and so the goblin, prints the names stored and nothing of the page.
When the fetch stops short, the command prints why.
When the page asks for a sign-in, the fetch files his sign-in card and the login reads signed out, and once he has signed in the fetch carries on by itself.
A fetch that carries on and stops wakes the CEO with why.

### Fetch it for me

A request of a goblin whose link a recipe reads offers **Fetch it for me** above its paste fields, saying what it reads, from which page, with which login.
His click on the board on this PC starts the same fetch for the request's goblin and shows it running.
Keys stored close the card as a save does, and a sign-in needed brings his sign-in card, after which the card shows the fetch carrying on.
A fetch that stopped, from his click or after his sign-in, wakes the CEO with why and shows **Fetch stopped** with the reason in muted words beside it.
The paste fields and the terminal stay.

### The seal and the key alarm

Every Claude Code goblin starts with two hooks of its own, handed to it with `--settings`, beside the CEO's: `state\goblin-claude-settings.json`, written anew at each spawn and switch.
The two that run at every tool call post to the board, which does their work in its own process, so no tool call starts a process for them (#1610).
They post to the board the home's board record names when the goblin starts, or else to the address the home's board listens at.
Each of a goblin's other hooks runs the home's `goblins-hook.exe`, the same `goblins` built as a program with no console, where it is the same build as the home's `goblins.exe`, and `goblins.exe` otherwise: Claude Code starts every hook hidden, which gives a console program a console host of its own, a second process for each hook, while Windows starts a windowed one alone and it reads the hook's payload and answers through the same pipes.
The seal runs before each Bash, PowerShell or Monitor call and each file tool's call, as a post to the board's `/api/goblin-hook/seal`, and holds every call to the goblin's path boundary and a sandbox task's commands to its host rule ([Credentials by need](contract/authentication.md#credentials-by-need)).
It refuses a shell command that runs a `find`, or a `grep`, `egrep` or `fgrep` made recursive (`-r`, `-R`, `--recursive`, `--dereference-recursive` or `-d recurse`), whose search root is a drive root, such as `/`, `/c/` or `C:\`, or a bare `\` in PowerShell, with `[drive-root-search]` and the folder to name instead, since such a search walks the whole drive for hours and goes on after its tool call's shell ends; a `find` given `-maxdepth` and a search of any folder run.
It refuses a chrome-devtools-axi command while a fetch reads in the browser the goblin's task holds.
It also refuses every command while a Python environment of the goblin's worktree, the folder its Claude Code started in, which its task record names, holds code that runs at every Python start that no known package ships and the CEO did not accept, and wakes the CEO with the file and its package ([Code that runs at every Python start](contract/worktrees.md#code-that-runs-at-every-python-start)).
The seal is a file under `state\browser-seals`, written before the key page loads and removed once the keys are hidden.
A seal left behind lifts by itself three minutes after it was written, a minute past a fetch's own bound, a starting supervisor clears any left, and one that cannot be read refuses.
A seal the board does not answer refuses too: Claude Code blocks the call when the post fails (`onFailure: block`).
The key alarm runs after every tool call, beside the goblin's native hook, as a post to the board's `/api/goblin-hook/tool`, and wakes the CEO once per call whose output holds a secret key, naming the goblin, the tool and the kind of key, never the key, so the key can be rolled.
It matches each kind of secret key by its prefix and its length, and most kinds by their mix of letters and digits too, so a commit hash, a UUID, a publishable key or a masked key never wakes it, though a long name that starts like a key can.
The native hook is still held to its own limit and the alarm to Claude Code's default of ten minutes, a part past its limit left unfinished as Claude Code would have ended it.
Each post carries the goblin's variables in headers Claude Code fills from its terminal's environment, such as `X-CEO-TASK-ID`.
The board does a post's work only when the terminal's id and proof it carries (`GOBLINS_HOST_ID`, `GOBLINS_HOST_PROOF`) prove it came from the goblin's own terminal, and only for a post from this PC, so no web page, other goblin or other machine can speak for a goblin.
So each post reads the terminal's proofs, and each seal the goblin's task record too, files the board reads in its own process.

A Codex or pi goblin runs the same seal, and no key alarm yet (#1366).
A Codex goblin's launch carries it as a `hooks.PreToolUse` override matching Bash, the name Codex gives a shell command and an `exec_command` call alike, which runs `goblins hook goblin-seal-codex` with the home's `goblins.exe` under Codex's own limit for a hook, as Claude Code's is for its seal.
Codex runs it only once the Overlord has trusted it in a Codex goblin's `/hooks`, as it runs the goblin's other hooks, and a home whose paths would need quoting starts its Codex goblins without it, which the start's output says.
A pi goblin starts with `--extension state\goblin-pi-extension.ts`, written anew at each spawn and switch, beside the native extension every pi session loads: its `tool_call` handler runs `goblins hook goblin-seal-pi` with the home's hook program before each tool call, and blocks the call with the seal's reason.
Both seals look in the folder their session started in, which their payload names, and answer with exit 0 and the refusal as JSON on stdout: Codex runs a hook in the session's Windows PowerShell, which ends with 1 for every exit code but 0, so the exit 2 Claude Code reads never reaches it.
The pi extension blocks the call too when the seal cannot start or answer within the native hooks' 30 seconds, as pi blocks a call whose handler throws.

### The turn-end nudge

A goblin whose turn ends on a promise of an action it never took, such as "I will now run the tests" with no tool call after it, is told once to do it, rather than sitting idle until the supervisor's idle wake reaches the CEO (#2364).
Its turn goes on with one message: if it meant to act, it acts now, and if the turn is meant to end there, as on a question to the CEO, a done report or a wait its brief asked for, it ends the turn again in one line.
A nudge that should not have come costs that one short turn and nothing else: it changes no record and wakes no one, and the turn it continues is never nudged again.
`internal/turnnudge` reads the last paragraph of the turn's last reply and nudges when a clause opens with "I will", "I'll", "I'm going to", "I am about to", "Let me", "Let's" or "Time to" and a verb of doing, or with a verb in -ing and "now", as in "Running the gate now".
It does not nudge a last paragraph that asks anything, a wait such as "I'll wait for CI", "Standing by" or "I'll report back", or an action held until something else happens, such as "I'll merge once CI is green", and what follows a wait or a held action in the paragraph is held by it.
Each harness's goblin gets it from the hook that already runs at its turn's end, so it needs no hook of its own and no new trust in a Codex goblin's `/hooks`:

- A Claude Code goblin's and a Codex goblin's Stop run `goblins native-hook`, which answers a goblin's Stop with `{"decision":"block","reason":...}` on stdout with exit 0, the reply both read as "go on with this prompt", and with `{}` otherwise.
  It reads the reply and `stop_hook_active` from the Stop's payload, so a turn that a Stop hook already continued is never nudged, and it lets a Claude Code turn end while background work or a session timer the payload names will wake the session.
- A pi goblin's extension asks `goblins hook goblin-turn-end` before a run its own prompt started settles (`agent_before_settle`), and when that answers with the nudge, the run goes on once with the nudge as its message to the model.
  It does not ask for a run that already went on once, that was aborted or failed, or that has work queued behind it, and a hook that cannot answer lets the run end.

A gate's agents and the CEO are never nudged.
The payloads it reads are those of Claude Code 2.1.322, Codex 0.160.0 and pi 1.4.0; a harness that does not report the turn's last reply, or a pi with no `agent_before_settle`, nudges nothing and ends its turns as before.

### Logins on the Connectors page

The **Connectors** page lists **Browser logins** as rows like its connectors': each login a running goblin's project uses, signed in or out, the projects it serves, when he signed in and when it was last used.
Its **Sign out** button deletes the login's profile and keeps the login, so the next fetch brings his sign-in card.
**Forget** asks first, then deletes the profile and the login.
His account and its keys at the provider are not touched.
Both act only from the board on this PC, only on a login of a running goblin's project, and refuse while a browser of the login runs, closes or signs in, saying to try again once it closes.

### Proofs

The proofs run against stand-in sign-in and key pages on this machine, never a real account.
`TestBrowserSignInWindowOnAHiddenDesktop` and `TestAKeyFetchAfterHisSignInReachesOnlyTheVault` run on a desktop of their own that no one sees: his click opens the window there, a stand-in sign-in completes the card, and the fetch carries on headless, reveals the secret key with a real click and stores both keys.
Each key is then in the vault and in no file of the home, the login's Chrome profile included, no answer to the goblin and no notice to the CEO.
`TestReadKeysRevealsAndReadsOnlyTheRecipesKeysInATabItCloses` and `TestReadKeysRefusesPagesItMustNotReadWithoutRepeatingThem` run the reader against a headless Chrome, the second for every page it must refuse.

## AFK mode

AFK mode is the Overlord's switch for running the fleet while he is away: the CEO decides everything his authority covers and logs each decision, nothing is asked of him or held for him, what only he can do gets a backlog row and is listed in his report, and nothing on the board prompts him.
The [AFK contract](contract/afk.md#afk-mode) holds its terms; this is how the supervisor keeps them.

The supervisor is the only writer of AFK mode's three files in `state/`:

| File | What it holds |
| --- | --- |
| `afk.json` | The switch: whether it is on, the stretch it names (`session`), since when and from where, the allowance and the free disk read then, and after it turned off, when and from where. A switch the CEO made at his ask names the CEO there and keeps his words (`asked`, `ended_asked`). |
| `afk.audit` | One JSON line for each switch, each decision the CEO logged with its evidence, the decisions it answered in his place among them, each with its `diagnosis`, what was `tried`, its `options` and its `answer`, and each step only he can take (`his`), each item that waited on him in the Command Center, each `strike` of a line the CEO logged by mistake, which names that line's `at` as its `item` and carries the reason as its evidence, and each `settle` of a `his` line the CEO saw to later, which names the line the same way and carries what became of it as its evidence. Every line carries its stretch, a settle the stretch on when it was made, a strike the stretch on when it was made or else the last that ended, and a switch the CEO made at his ask carries his words as its evidence. No line is ever removed. |
| `afk-report.json` | The report of the last stretch that ended. |

`goblins afk on` and `goblins afk off` send `afk-on` or `afk-off` over the supervisor's pipe, the one a run item travels over, and need `goblins serve` running.
The supervisor makes the switch only for a process it proves runs in a terminal of the Overlord's own, read from the process at the other end of the pipe: its ancestry, each parent created no later than its child, and its environment.
It refuses, in this order, a process whose environment carries `GOBLINS_ROLE=goblin` or `NO_MISTAKES_GATE`, one whose ancestry reaches the registered CEO, one whose environment names a native terminal (`GOBLINS_HOST_ID`) or a Herdr pane (`HERDR_PANE_ID`), where the fleet runs an agent, and one under an agent harness: one among its ancestors (`claude`, `codex`, `pi`, `kimi` or `node`, by executable name), or a harness's own variable in its environment (`CLAUDECODE`, `AI_AGENT`), which still marks a command whose parents were cut off, as Git Bash's `timeout` leaves one.
A process it cannot read, or one that started after the request, is refused too, never taken for his; a terminal run as administrator is one it cannot read, and the refusal says so.
So is a process whose ancestry reaches neither the Windows desktop (`explorer`) nor Windows Terminal (`WindowsTerminal`): its parents were cut off, as Git Bash's `env` leaves a command with those variables removed too, and as Git Bash leaves the Overlord's own command, so the refusal tells him to run it in PowerShell or cmd.
Those two refusals are the ones he meets himself in a terminal of his own, so each is one short sentence that says what to do, PowerShell or cmd or the switch in the Code Goblins window, with nothing of how a terminal is proven.
What it accepts is recorded with the shell the command was typed in, such as `his own terminal (powershell.exe pid 4242)`.

A request that carries `asked`, the words `goblins afk on --asked` or `goblins afk off --asked` passes, is the registered CEO making the switch at the Overlord's ask, and takes another proof.
The supervisor refuses a process whose environment carries `GOBLINS_ROLE=goblin` or `NO_MISTAKES_GATE`, then proves the process runs under the registered primary CEO, the proof a question takes, and refuses one it cannot prove.
The words are kept on one line, and words that are blank or longer than 500 characters are refused.
What it accepts is recorded as `the CEO at his ask (claude pid 4242)`, with his words as `asked` in `afk.json`, as the evidence of the switch's line in `afk.audit`, in the notice in the CEO's wake queue and in the report.
It does not reset a switch that cannot be read, which stays the Overlord's.
This proof says who asks and nothing of whose words those are: the CEO quotes them, and the supervisor cannot tell an ask of his from one the CEO misread or was handed by a goblin, a page or a tool's output.
The [AFK contract](contract/afk.md#afk-mode) holds that part, and the record shows him what it was switched for.
His own switch, from the board or a terminal of his own, turns it either way whoever made the last one.

Turning it on keeps the allowance and the free disk every start reads, the less free of the home's drive and the Dev Drive, writes the log line before the switch, and puts a `review` notice keyed `afk` in the CEO's wake queue; turning it off keeps the report first, so a stretch never ends without one, and the notice tells the CEO to write it into its terminal.
A switch waits on no program: the allowance it keeps is the supervisor's last reading of each provider from the `quota-axi` read it already makes once a minute, and a provider's reading older than an hour is none.
Free disk is one system call, read as every start reads it at each switch, and a disk that cannot be read stops no switch.
On 2026-10-08 the switch's own read held each turn of AFK mode for up to 20 seconds, and the report opened every status log the home and its archive held, which held turning it off for up to a minute on this machine.
The report now opens only the status logs written since the stretch began, by the time the folder listing gives and the time an archived log's name ends with.
Turning it on removes the report of the stretch before, from `state/afk-report.json` and from what the board's view kept of that stretch's log, so reports never pile up.
The adversary this proof names is an agent that follows its contract and tries the command, the pipe or a wrapper around either.
A process of the same Windows user that writes `state/afk.json` itself is the boundary the board's other items already have.

The board's own switch, `POST /api/afk` with `{"on": true}` or `{"on": false}`, is proven of the program that shows the board.
The endpoint first refuses a body it cannot read, then a request that is not the board's own page on this PC: one whose peer is not this machine, or one a proxy handled, as a board shared through Tailscale serve is, except an off from his private board (below).
It then asks Windows which process owns the other end of the connection (`GetExtendedTcpTable`), which for a browser is the browser's own network process, and reads that process as it reads a pipe's caller: its environment and its ancestry, with the same refusals of an agent's in the same order.
The program that is his is the one just under the desktop, and where the parents stop short of the desktop the last one still running, since a program outlives what opened it.
From that program down to the process that holds the connection the supervisor reads what each one is, and refuses a browser another program can drive: one started with `--remote-debugging-port`, `--remote-debugging-pipe`, `--enable-automation` or `--headless`, or with Firefox's `-marionette`, as a test's browser and a headless one are.
It reads that program's own environment for the same marks of an agent.
It refuses a program that runs as another Windows user, or in another Windows session, than the supervisor, since the board answers every session of the PC at one address.

One of three things then proves the board his:

- Its parents reach the desktop, as those of a browser he starts from the desktop or from a terminal of his own do.
- It is this home's own desktop window: a process Windows has named `goblins-window.exe` since its start, running a program in the home's `bin`, where an update leaves an open window on a renamed copy.
- Its browser holds a grant this home gave that browser program.

The window's proof needs no parent, because the window rarely keeps one at the desktop: the supervisor's own move onto an update opens it through a shell that exits, and the installer, `goblins --window` and a click on a notification leave it the same way.
Until 2026-10-09 the first proof stood alone, and the Overlord's own window was refused after it had moved itself onto v0.5.8.
Whoever can put a program in the home's `bin` can replace the supervisor's own, so that folder is what the proof stands on.

A grant keeps a browser he started his after its opener is gone.
When a browser loads the board while its parents reach the desktop, the supervisor gives it a random secret in a cookie no page can read (`ceo-board`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Strict`) and records the secret's SHA-256 with the browser's program in `state/board-grants.json`.
A later request from that browser is his by the grant after its opener has exited, and after the browser has restarted itself as one does after its own update, for 30 days from when it was given.
Only a load whose parents reach the desktop gives a grant, and a browser whose grant is younger than a day keeps it, so a grant never renews itself.
His desktop window needs none.
The secret alone proves nothing: a cookie for `127.0.0.1` goes to every port of it, so any local server he opens in that browser is handed it.
It counts only from the browser program it was given to, under every check above, so a copy in an agent's browser, in a script or in another browser is refused.
A record of grants that cannot be read grants nothing, and the next grant given replaces it.

A browser that was never proven and holds no grant is refused, and so is anything an agent started: one a test runner started under `node`, one opened from a goblin's or the CEO's terminal, or one whose harness left its variable in its environment.
A board that is refused shows one short sentence that says what to do, with nothing of how a board is proven.
A browser is sent to the Code Goblins window or to `goblins afk on` in PowerShell, and his window, which cannot be sent to itself, is told to quit from its tray icon and open again from the Start menu.
What the supervisor found goes to the CEO as a `review` wake keyed `afk`, with the sentence the board showed.
What it accepts is recorded with the program that is his, such as `his own board (goblins-window.exe pid 4242)`, and the switch is then made as the command's is.
Off while it is already off answers as off, and a switch that cannot be read answers on with 409 and off by resetting it.
A switch that fails after the board was proven his answers 409 with one sentence, and the CEO is told why in the same wake.
The adversary is the same one, at the board: an agent that follows its contract and tries the switch from a browser it started, through a proxy or from another machine.
An agent that drives the Overlord's own running browser or window through his desktop is his to the supervisor, as a process that writes `state/afk.json` is his user; the [AFK contract](contract/afk.md#afk-mode) forbids both, and nothing here stops either.

His [private board](remote-access.md), the board remote access shares over his own tailnet, turns the switch on and off, as he decided on 2026-10-29 ("Allow it now as it is"), over proving his phone by its own tailnet address and over keeping on to the PC.
Remote access's gate marks a request it admitted, with his login for the PC's private name from the private origin, and a switch it admitted is recorded for the CEO as from `his own private board (by Tailscale's headers, which a program on this PC can forge)`, which his pages show as your private board.
Those are Tailscale's headers, and an agent on this PC can forge them, sending them itself or through a Serve route of its own it adds for a moment, so nothing proves the private board his.
A forged off ends his stretch early, with its report and his cards, and tells the CEO he is back; a forged on hands the CEO his authority. He chose it knowing both.
On asks first on the private board as it does on the PC.
Another user of his tailnet, a request without Tailscale's identity and another proxy never pass the gate.

Every snapshot carries `afk`, which is what the board shows of the switch.
Its `state` is `off`, `on`, or `unreadable` for a switch that cannot be read, which is never taken for on.
While it is on it carries `since` and `from`, `asked` (his words, for a switch the CEO made at his ask), `decided` (how many decisions the log holds for the stretch) and `held`: each item the log holds for him in the stretch, with whether it still waits on him, what became of it, its goblin's latest report since and `recommendation`, the choice a held question's asker recommended, empty for none.
While it is off it carries `page`, the board-local URL of the last report, `/api/afk/report/<session>/index.html`.
The log's file and each held goblin's status log are read for a snapshot only when they have changed since the last one, and a log that cannot be read is said as the snapshot's error.
